Real-variable theory of function spaces with operator-valued A p A_{p} weights in Banach spaces Thanks: This project is partially supported by the National Natural Science Foundation of China (Grant Nos. 12431006, 12371093, and 124B2004), the Beijing Natural Science Foundation (Grant No. 1262011), the Fundamental Research Funds for the Central Universities (Grant No. 2253200028), and the Research Council of Finland (Grant Nos. 346314, 364208, and 371637).
Tuomas P. Hytönen, Yinqin Li, Dachun Yang, Wen Yuan

Abstract
. While the theory of matrix-weighted function spaces is well established, the majority of previous results in the infinite-dimensional operator-valued setting deal with “no go” theorems, showing the impossibility of some prospective generalizations. However, we show that a complete real-variable theory of Besov and Triebel–Lizorkin spaces with operator-valued Muckenhoupt A p A_{p} weights can still be developed, once correctly formulated. This covers operator-weighted extensions of results like the φ \varphi -transform characterization in terms of discrete sequence spaces, the boundedness of almost diagonal operators, and applications to the T ⁡ ( 1 ) T(1) theorem and trace/extension theorems. A key tool is a version of the reverse Hölder inequality, which is weak enough to follow from the operator-valued A p A_{p} condition (unlike a variant that had to be imposed as an additional assumption in some previous works), yet strong enough to be used much like its classical counterpart. In contrast to the established scalar and matrix-weighted theories, our approach cannot build on operator-weighted L p L^{p} results, as these fail in a very definite way. We also strengthen the existing “no go” statements in Hilbert spaces, showing (among other counterexamples) that every infinite-dimensional Banach space has an operator-valued A p A_{p} weight V V for which the Hilbert transform is unbounded on L p ​ ( V ) L^{p}(V) . This is nontrivial, since the lack of Hilbert space structure also complicates the construction of A p A_{p} weights. Building on results from Banach space theory, we achieve this unboundedness by combining two distinct methods in two different classes of spaces (so-called K K -convex ones and those that are not UMD), whose union covers all Banach spaces.



2010 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 42B25, 46E40;
Secondary 42B35, 46E35, 42B20, 42C40

1.
Introduction

Since its discovery in the 1970’s, Muckenhoupt’s theory of
A_{p}
weights has become one of the central topics in harmonic analysis with diverse applications. Originally dealing with scalar-valued weight functions, the extension of this theory to matrix
A_{p}
weights is also well developed since the 1990’s.

The theme of this paper is operator-valued
A_{p}
weights, a further extension of the matrix
A_{p}
theory to possibly infinite-dimensional target spaces. Much less is known about this area, and much of what is known concerns “no go” theorems pointing to the failure of natural extensions of familiar results from finite dimensions, even in the supposedly simplest case of
p=2
and a Hilbert space target, not to mention other values of
p
and more general Banach spaces, where relatively little has been said so far.

Nevertheless, at least a definition of
A_{p}
weights is readily available in great generality. While much of the recent literature on matrix
A_{p}
weights has been based on an equivalent (in finite dimensions) condition given by Roudenko
[
59
, Lemma 1.3]
, the original definition by Nazarov–Treil
[
55
, p. 21]
and Volberg
[
66
, p. 450]
, stated in terms of dual norms, makes perfect sense for an arbitrary Banach space
\mathfrak{X}
. In a way, this generality even clarifies the picture, since one can no longer identify
\mathfrak{X}
with its dual
\mathfrak{X}^{*}
, making it necessary to distinguish between norms on the original space and on the dual space. Some aspects of this general framework were already developed by Lauzon
[
46
]
, but many of his results are only proved under additional postulates concerning versions of the reverse Hölder inequality that are not implied by the
A_{p}
condition in general; for
p=2
and Hilbert targets, such theory under reverse Hölder postulates was first developed by Pott
[
58
]
.

The aim of this article is to show that the natural
A_{p}
condition for operator-valued weights, acting on a general Banach space,

(i)

retains several key features of the classical
A_{p}
condition;

(ii)

is necessary but
insufficient
for a weighted theory in
L^{p}
;

(iii)

is nevertheless sufficient for developing substantial parts of the theory of function spaces of Besov and Triebel–Lizorkin type.

We will elaborate on each of these points shortly, but let us first provide some more context.

In both the scalar-weighted and the matrix-weighted cases, the resolution of the basic problems in
L^{p}
(like the boundedness of the maximal operator and the Hilbert transform) came first, and served as stepping stones for the weighted theory of other function spaces. For scalar weights, the said
L^{p}
results were obtained in
[
52
]
and
[
38
]
, respectively, and for matrix weights, in
[
55
,
65
,
66
]
and
[
17
,
35
]
, in the reverse order. The scalar-weighted theory of Besov and Triebel–Lizorkin spaces seems to have started with
[
15
]
, the matrix-weighted theory of Besov spaces with
[
59
]
, and a systematic theory of matrix-weighted Triebel–Lizorkin spaces with
[
28
]
.

Against this background, the fact that we obtain a reasonable theory of Besov and Triebel–Lizorkin spaces with operator weights, even in the lack of a corresponding
L^{p}
theory, might seem surprising. However, a natural point of analogy is found in the (unweighted) theory of singular integrals of Banach space valued functions. The problem of the
L^{p}
boundedness of basic singular operators is understood in this situation, and known to be characterised by the UMD property of the target space
\mathfrak{X}
; this is a celebrated result of Burkholder
[
16
]
and Bourgain
[
8
]
; see also
[
39
, Theorem 5.1.1]
for a detailed treatment. However, the boundedness on (unweighted) Besov and Triebel–Lizorkin spaces is available much more generally, without any restrictions on
\mathfrak{X}
(see
[
41
, Chapter 14]
). What we obtain for operator-weighted spaces is essentially the same, with the only difference that we do not have the characterisation of the weights for the
L^{p}
boundedness. But even in the unweighted theory, the known characterisation of spaces by the UMD property does not play any role in the development of the theory of Besov and Triebel–Lizorkin spaces with values in arbitrary spaces.

The rest of this Introduction is divided into three sections, where we elaborate on each of the points (
i
), (
ii
), and (
iii
) above, indicating our main results and providing some further background. The rest of the paper is then divided into four parts, where we develop the theory on each of these points in full detail. The content and the order of both Sections
1.1
,
1.2
, and
1.3
of this Introduction, and Parts
I
,
II
, and
III
of the rest of the paper, is the same as that of points (
i
), (
ii
), and (
iii
) above. The final Part
IV
continues further with point (
iii
) within a new axiomatic framework.

1.1.
Key properties of
A_{p}
weights

Concerning point (
i
) above, some key lemmas for scalar and
matrix weights that we are able to extend to our general setting are

(a)

reverse Hölder inequalities (Proposition
5.1
);

(b)

quantitative doubling properties (Lemma
6.7
).

The role of reverse Hölder inequalities for both scalar and matrix weights is well established since
[
18
]
and
[
17
,
35
]
, respectively.
For operator-valued weights, as already mentioned, Pott
[
58
]
(for
p=2
and Hilbert targets) and Lauzon
[
46
]
(more generally) showed that many results can still be extended,
provided that versions of reverse Hölder inequalities are assumed
, but these are genuine additional assumptions on the weights, no longer consequences of the
A_{p}
condition as they were in finite dimensions.

We wish to make the claim that, in the operator-weighted setting, the notion of a reverse Hölder inequality should be (only slightly) revised from the matrix-valued version of
[
17
,
35
]
. This revised formulation has the benefit of being implied by the operator-valued
A_{p}
condition (as we show in Proposition
5.1
) while still strong enough to serve the role of the classical reverse Hölder inequality in several applications, notably in the development of the theory of Besov and Triebel–Lizorkin spaces with operator weights further below.

The role of various doubling properties is also well established in analysis. In point (
b
) above, we have especially in mind types of estimates that were introduced into the analysis of matrix-weighted Besov spaces by Roudenko
[
60
, Definition 1.3]
, and that have played a significant role in related developments ever since; cf.
[
28
, Definition 2.1]
and
[
10
, Section 2.1]
. In the matrix-weighted theory, these estimates take the form

(1.1)

\|A_{Q}A_{R}^{-1}\|\leq c(Q,R),

where
A_{Q}
and
A_{R}
are
reducing operators
of the matrix weight, and
c(Q,R)
has moderate growth in terms of the size ratio and the relative distance of the cubes
Q
and
R
. A reducing operator is a matrix inducing an equivalent Euclidean norm for a given norm on
\mathbb{R}^{m}
or
\mathbb{C}^{m}
; it is produced by the John ellipsoid theorem
[
43
]
, a strictly finite-dimensional tool.

While we have no analogue for reducing operators in infinite-dimensional study, we can still find useful substitutes for bounds of the form (
1.1
) with the left-hand side replaced by the ratio of the operator-weight-induced norms over the cubes
Q
and
R
. Indeed, such a result is proved in Lemma
6.7
, and then successfully applied in the theory of operator-weighted Besov and Triebel–Lizorkin spaces in Part
III
of the paper.

1.2.
L^{p}
results

Concerning operator-weighted
L^{p}
spaces, we are going to show that the operator-valued
A_{p}
condition remains

(a)

necessary and sufficient for the weighted
L^{p}
boundedness of basic averaging operators (Corollary
8.3
; previously proved for
p=2
and Hilbert targets by Aleman–Constantin
[
1
, Lemma 2.2]
);

(b)

sufficient for the weighted
L^{p}
boundedness of certain sparse operators (Corollary
8.4
; previously proved for
p=2
and Hilbert targets by Limani–Pott
[
49
, Theorem 2.1]
); but

(c)

insufficient
for the weighted
L^{p}
boundedness of the Hilbert transform
\mathcal{H}
(Theorem
10.1
; previously proved for
p=2
and Hilbert targets by Gillespie–Pott–Treil–Volberg
[
32
, Theorem 1.3]
, with a sharper version in
[
34
, Theorem 1.1]
);

(d)

as a tool for (
c
) but with independent interest, we show that certain operator-valued bounded mean oscillation (BMO) conditions are similarly insufficient for the boundedness of the commutator
[B,\mathcal{H}]
on the
unweighted

L^{p}(\mathbb{R};\mathfrak{X})
(Theorem
9.1
; previously proved for
p=2
and Hilbert targets by Nazarov–Pisier–Treil–Volberg
[
54
, Theorem 1.2]
and Gillespie–Pott–Treil–Volberg
[
32
, Theorem 1.2]
,
[
34
, Theorem 1.3]
; see also
[
33
]
for related results on martingale transforms.).

Related to point (
d
), we should also mention the classical works of Sarason
[
62
]
and Page
[
56
]
on the factorisation of Hardy spaces of operator-valued functions and the boundedness of Hankel operators with operator-valued symbols, both of which are known to be essentially equivalent to the boundedness of the commutators
[B,\mathcal{H}]
with operator-valued
B
. In contrast to the negative results of point (
d
),
[
62
]
and
[
56
]
achieve characterisations in infinite-dimensional Hilbert spaces, but these are expressed in terms of quotient norms whose relation to the real-variable theory that we develop remains unclear.

The
sparse operators
appearing in (
b
) have become instrumental in the scalar-weighted theory since the work of Lerner
[
47
]
, and also in the matrix-weighted theory since the work of Nazarov–Petermichl–Treil–Volberg
[
53
]
, thanks to the fact they dominate classical operators in a way that is compatible with both scalar and matrix weighted estimates. In contrast to this, the positive result about the operator-weighted boundedness of sparse operators in (
b
), together with the negative result for the Hilbert transform in (
c
), shows that Calderón-Zygmund operators are no longer dominated by sparse operators in the infinite-dimensional setting, making sparse operators less relevant objects in this theory than in finite dimensions. For
p=2
and Hilbert targets, this was already pointed out by Limani–Pott
[
49
]
.

In some sense, points (
c
) and (
d
) are perhaps expected; after all, if things go wrong even in a Hilbert space, as already shown in
[
32
,
34
,
54
]
, how could they possibly behave better in more complicated situations. However, the “good” properties of a Hilbert space also ensure the existence of a plenty of operators (such as bounded projections onto any closed subspace, certainly not available in a general Banach space), therefore also a plenty of operator-valued
A_{p}
weights (resp. BMO functions), making a condition over all
A_{p}
weights (resp. BMO functions) more difficult to be satisfied than in a setting with few
A_{p}
weights (resp. BMO functions) only. In another extreme, there are Banach spaces with “very few operators”, in the sense that every bounded linear operator has the form
\lambda\cdot I+K
of a scalar-multiple of the identity plus a compact operator
K
; the first such example is a celebrated construction of Argyros and Haydon
[
3
]
. In a space with this scalar-plus-compact property, one might imagine that operator
A_{p}
weights (resp. BMO functions) are “not far” from matrix
A_{p}
weights (resp. BMO functions), making it plausible that the positive results of the finite-dimensional theory might still be pushed to hold. This suggests that achieving the negative results in (
c
) and (
d
) for any given Banach space
\mathfrak{X}
requires some effort.

Our constructions of the counterexamples in both (
c
) and (
d
) split into two cases using different properties of the Banach space under consideration. The first case is when
\mathfrak{X}
is
not
a UMD space, in which case the Hilbert transform is unbounded on the unweighted
L^{p}(\mathbb{R};\mathfrak{X})
. The other case is when
\mathfrak{X}
is
K
-convex, in which case the counterexamples are obtained by carefully embedding a sequence of the finite dimensional Hilbertian counterexamples of
[
54
]
into
\mathfrak{X}
in a quantitatively controlled manner, using an elaboration of Dvoretzky’s theorem in
K
-convex spaces due to Figiel and Tomczak-Jaegermann
[
23
]
as a key tool. Since every UMD space is
K
-convex, but not conversely, these two cases cover all Banach spaces, even with some overlap.

1.3.
Besov and Triebel–Lizorkin spaces

In contrast to the predominantly “negative” results about the spaces
L^{p}(V)
, we will show that Besov and Triebel–Lizorkin spaces
\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}

with operator
A_{p}
weights can be meaningfully defined so that they enjoy various good properties.
To be precise, we introduce pointwise operator-weighted space
\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(V)

and average operator weighted space
\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho)

(see Definition
12.3
) and obtain the following:

(a)

the equivalence
between
\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(V)
and
\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho)

for Besov spaces in the full parameter range
(Theorem
14.3
; extending some results in
[
59
]
and
[
26
]
)
and for Triebel–Lizorkin spaces under some upper bound on
q

(Theorem
14.3
and Lemma
14.5
,
extending
[
28
, Theorem 3.5]
and Proposition
14.10
, partially extending
[
28
, Corollary 3.9]
);
surprisingly, we find that in infinite-dimensional case,
the aforementioned equivalence for Triebel–Lizorkin spaces
does not hold when
q=\infty
(see Proposition
14.13
).
Although the optimal range ensuring this equivalence is still unclear,
we achieve a satisfactory theory of both the
average-weighted space
\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho)
in the full parameter range
and the pointwise-weighted space
\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(V)
under the about upper bound on
q
, covering:

(b)

the equivalence of the operator-weighted space with the
corresponding discrete sequence space
\dot{a}^{s}_{p,q}
via the
\varphi
transform
(Theorems
15.3
and
15.4
; extending matrix-weighted analogues
[
59
, Theorem 1.4]
and
[
26
, Corollary 3.2 and Theorem 3.4]
in the Besov case with
p\in(1,\infty)
resp.
p\in(0,1]
and
[
28
, Theorem 2.3]
in the Triebel–Lizorkin case);

(c)

the boundedness of various operators, including the Hilbert transform (Corollary
15.15
) and almost diagonal operators (Theorems

17.5
and
17.12
;
extending a matrix-weighted version in
[
28
, Theorem 2.6]
); in particular,
for the pointwise operator-weighted spaces, we show that our
assumptions on almost diagonal operators are optimal in some sense
(see Remark
17.13
);

(d)

a “sandwich” embedding

\dot{F}^{0}_{p,1}(\rho)\subset L^{p}(V)\subset F^{0}_{p,\infty}(\rho)

of
L^{p}(V)
between two Triebel–Lizorkin spaces with the same smoothness and integrability (Propositions
16.1
and
16.2
; this is a substitute of the equality
\dot{F}^{0}_{p,2}(V)=L^{p}(V)
for matrix
A_{p}
weights
[
28
, Section 4]
, and an extension of a similar sandwich relation in the unweighted but Banach space valued case
[
41
, Proposition 14.6.13]
).

Once we have laid down these foundations of the theory of operator-weighted Besov and Triebel–Lizorkin spaces in Part
III
, it turns out that extensions of many more results from the matrix-weighted versions of these spaces can be developed in an axiomatic framework, taking only a few of the key properties established in Part
III
as postulates of an abstract class of
discretizable spaces
that we study in Part
IV
. The results obtained in this abstract setting include molecular and wavelet characterizations of these spaces and their applications to the
T(1)
theorem for Calderón–Zygmund operators.
We also apply these results to the boundedness of trace and extension operators in operator-weighted Besov and Triebel–Lizorkin spaces. The results of Part
IV
extend several earlier results in the matrix-weighted setting from
[
26
,
27
,
28
,
59
]
.

In the classical theory, Triebel–Lizorkin spaces arise as an
extension
of the scale of
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})=\dot{F}^{0}_{p,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
for
p\in(1,\infty)
, and this relation persists even for matrix-
A_{p}
-weighted versions, as shown in
[
28
, Section 4]
. In view of (
c
) and (
d
) above, while recalling (
c
) of Section
1.2
, the operator-weighted Triebel–Lizorkin spaces should rather be seen as a
substitute
for
L^{p}(V)
, being relatively close to
L^{p}(V)
according to (
d
), yet sufficiently different so that good properties that fail in
L^{p}(V)
remain true in
\dot{F}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho)
. This phenomenon is already present in the unweighted but Banach space -valued versions of these spaces (cf.
[
41
, p. 294]
); and we extend it to the general operator
A_{p}
weighted setting.

Matrix-weighted Besov spaces were extensively studied in
[
26
,
27
,
59
,
60
]
and a systematic study of matrix-weighted Triebel–Lizorkin spaces was started in
[
28
]
. This has been followed by numerous extensions by many authors
[
9
,
10
,
11
,
12
,
13
,
14
,
4
,
5
,
48
,
67
]
.
However, all these works deal with finite-dimensional spaces, where operators are just finite matrices.

Despite the various “no go” theorems around the Hilbert transform in operator-weighted
L^{p}
spaces mentioned above, an important indication for us, that positive results could still be achieved in other spaces, was a work of Aleman and Constantin
[
1
]
, where they characterised the boundedness of the Bergman projection on operator-weighted
L^{2}
spaces in terms of an operator-valued Békollé–Bonami
B_{2}
condition—a well-known relative of the Muckenhoupt
A_{2}
condition in the Bergman context. While their precise setting is different, it also has important similarities with the theory of Besov and Triebel–Lizorkin spaces. Notably, the arguments of
[
1
]
make critical use of the mean value property of analytic functions, which bears some similarity with versions of the Calderón reproducing formula commonly applied in the context of Besov and Triebel–Lizorkin spaces; indeed, the components of the Littlewood–Paley decomposition are analytic functions! Building on this analogy, we are able to extend a lot of the recent matrix-weighted theory of Besov and Triebel–Lizorkin spaces to the operator-weighted context, achieving the types of results listed above.

While the settings are not directly comparable, our results go arguably beyond mere analogues of those of
[
1
]
in the following sense: In
[
1
]
, only
L^{2}
spaces of Hilbert space-valued functions and the corresponding
B_{2}
weights are considered, while we treat the full scale of Besov and Triebel–Lizorkin spaces
\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}
of Banach space-valued distributions and the corresponding Muckenhoupt classes
A_{p}
for all
p\in(0,\infty)
. (Recall that, already in the matrix-weighted setting, the single scalar-valued
A_{1}
condition splits into a scale of weight classes
A_{p}
with
p\in(0,1]
, first introduced by Frazier and Roudenko
[
26
]
.) This, in turn, leads to the following qualitative difference: In the Hilbertian
L^{2}
case, the reducing operator can be computed explicitly by

(1.2)

\begin{split}\fint_{Q}\|W^{\frac{1}{2}}(x)\mathfrak{e}\|^{2}\,dx&=\fint_{Q}(W^{\frac{1}{2}}(x)\mathfrak{e},W^{\frac{1}{2}}(x)\mathfrak{e})\,dx\\
&=\fint_{Q}(W(x)\mathfrak{e},\mathfrak{e})\,dx=(\langle W\rangle_{Q}\mathfrak{e},\mathfrak{e})=\|\langle W\rangle_{Q}^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathfrak{e}\|^{2},\end{split}

and these identities remain perfectly valid in infinite-dimensional Hilbert spaces. Thus, even in the lack of John’s ellipsoid theorem, the methods around reducing operators, familiar from the matrix-weighted theory, are still available for operator weights in
L^{2}
with Hilbert targets, but not if
either

L^{2}
is replaced by
L^{p}
or the Hilbert target by a Banach target. In our investigation, we make
both
these replacements, so that (
1.2
) completely fails, and only the expression on the left, or its
L^{p}
analogue, remains for us to work with. Thus, while
[
1
]
made the important opening move in positive results for the boundedness of singular operators on operator-weighted spaces under Muckenhoupt-style assumptions without side conditions, our investigation takes it a step further still from the established framework of the matrix-weighted theory.

Part I
Basic properties of operator weights

In this part, we develop the relevant background for operator-valued weights to be used in the study of operator-weighted
L^{p}
spaces in Part
II
and operator-weighted Besov and Triebel–Lizorkin spaces in Part
III
.

2.
L^{p}
spaces of operator-valued functions

Let
\Omega

be a measure space and
\mathfrak{X}
a Banach space.
A function
f:\Omega\to\mathfrak{X}
is said to be
simple

if
f=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathfrak{e}_{i}\mathbf{1}_{A_{i}}
with
\mathfrak{e}_{i}\in\mathfrak{X}
and each

A_{i}\subseteq\Omega
is measurable, and
strongly measurable
if it is the pointwise limit of simple functions. Then, in particular,
\|f(\cdot)\|_{\mathfrak{X}}
is a pointwise limit of nonnegative simple functions, and hence measurable as a scalar-valued function.

For all
p\in[0,\infty]
, we define

L^{p}(\mathfrak{X}):=L^{p}(\Omega;\mathfrak{X}):=\begin{cases}\{f:\Omega\to\mathfrak{X}\text{ strongly measurable}\},&p=0,\\
\{f\in L^{0}(\Omega;\mathfrak{X});\ \ \|f\|_{L^{p}(\Omega;\mathfrak{X})}<\infty\},&p\in(0,\infty],\end{cases}

where we may use the shorter notation
L^{p}(\mathfrak{X})
if
\Omega
is understood from the context and where

\|f\|_{L^{p}(\Omega;\mathfrak{X})}:=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\Big(\int_{\Omega}\|f(\omega)\|_{\mathfrak{X}}^{p}d\omega\Big)^{\frac{1}{p}},&p\in(0,\infty),\\
\operatornamewithlimits{ess\,sup}_{\omega\in\Omega}\|f(\omega)\|_{\mathfrak{X}},&p=\infty\end{cases}

is a norm for
p\in[1,\infty]
, a
p
-norm (
\|\mathfrak{u}+\mathfrak{v}\|^{p}\leq\|\mathfrak{u}\|^{p}+\|\mathfrak{v}\|^{p}
) for
p\in(0,1]
, and hence a quasi-norm (
\|\mathfrak{u}+\mathfrak{v}\|\leq c(\|\mathfrak{u}\|+\|\mathfrak{v}\|)
) for all
p\in(0,\infty]
. The space
L^{p}(\Omega;\mathfrak{X})
are complete for all
p\in(0,\infty]
.
For
p\in[1,\infty]
, a detailed treatment of these spaces can be found in
[
39
, Chapter 1]
.

Let
\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X})
be the space of bounded linear operators on
\mathfrak{X}
. Since
\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X})
is another Banach space, the previous notion could be applied to
F:\Omega\to\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X})
, but this would often result in an unnecessarily stringent requirement. The following notion is more flexible: A function
F:\Omega\to\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X})
is said to be
strongly measurable in the strong-operator sense
if
F(\cdot)\mathfrak{e}:\Omega\to\mathfrak{X}
is strongly measurable for each
\mathfrak{e}\in\mathfrak{X}
. Then

L^{p}_{\operatorname{so}}(\Omega;\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X})):=\Big\{F:\Omega\to\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X});F(\cdot)\mathfrak{e}\in L^{p}(\Omega;\mathfrak{X})\ \forall\ \mathfrak{e}\in\mathfrak{X}\Big\}.

If
f:\Omega\to\mathfrak{X}
and
F:\Omega\to\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X})
, one can define the pointwise product
Ff:\Omega\to\mathfrak{X}
in the obvious way by
(Ff)(\omega):=F(\omega)f(\omega)
. The above notions of measurability for
\mathfrak{X}
-valued and
\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X})
-valued functions are compatible in the following sense:

Lemma 2.1
.

If
f\in L^{0}(\Omega;\mathfrak{X})
and
F\in L^{0}_{\operatorname{so}}(\Omega;\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X}))
, then
Ff\in L^{0}(\Omega;\mathfrak{X})
.

Proof.

If
f=\mathbf{1}_{E}\mathfrak{e}
for some measurable set
E
and vector
\mathfrak{e}\in\mathfrak{X}
, then
Ff=\mathbf{1}_{E}F\mathfrak{e}\in L^{0}(\Omega;\mathfrak{X})
by the defining condition of
V\in L^{0}_{\operatorname{so}}(\Omega;\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X}))
. The case of simple
f
follows by linearity. For general
f\in L^{0}(\Omega;\mathfrak{X})
, the defining condition of strong measurability implies that
f
is the pointwise limit
f=\lim_{k\to\infty}f_{k}
of some simple
f_{k}
. Then
Ff_{k}\in L^{0}(\Omega;\mathfrak{X})
for each
k
, and
Ff=\lim_{k\to\infty}Ff_{k}\in L^{0}(\Omega;\mathfrak{X})
as the pointwise limit of functions in this space.
∎

The following lemma shows that
L^{p}_{\operatorname{so}}(\Omega;\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X}))
also comes with a natural quasi-norm; however, it is not complete in general (see
[
39
, bottom of p. 64]
).

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
(\mathfrak{X},\|\ \|)
be a Banach space,
\Omega
be a measure space,
p\in(0,\infty]
, and
F\in L^{p}_{\operatorname{so}}(\Omega;\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X}))
.
Then
\mathfrak{e}\mapsto F(\cdot)\mathfrak{e}
is continuous from
\mathfrak{X}
to
L^{p}(\Omega;\mathfrak{X})
, and hence

\|F\|_{L^{p}_{\operatorname{so}}(\Omega;\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X}))}:=\sup_{\|\mathfrak{e}\|_{\mathfrak{X}}\leq 1}\|F(\cdot)\mathfrak{e}\|_{L^{p}(\Omega;\mathfrak{X})}

is finite.

Proof.

We note that
L^{p}(\Omega;\mathfrak{X})
is a complete quasi-normed space; in particular, it is an F-space in the sense of
[
61
, 1.8(e)]
, i.e., a topological vector space whose topology is induced by a complete metric that is invariant under vector addition:
d(\mathfrak{e},\mathfrak{u})=d(\mathfrak{e}+\mathfrak{v},\mathfrak{u}+\mathfrak{v})
for all
\mathfrak{e},\mathfrak{u},\mathfrak{v}\in\mathfrak{X}
.

The map
\mathfrak{e}\mapsto F(\cdot)\mathfrak{e}
from
\mathfrak{X}
to
L^{p}(\Omega;\mathfrak{X})
is obviously well defined and linear. We check that it is closed. For this, suppose that
\mathfrak{e}_{n}\to\mathfrak{e}
in
\mathfrak{X}
and
F(\cdot)\mathfrak{e}_{n}\to f
in
L^{p}(\Omega;\mathfrak{X})
. Passing to a subsequence, it follows that
F(\cdot)\mathfrak{e}_{n}\to f
almost everywhere, but also
F(\cdot)\mathfrak{e}_{n}\to F(\cdot)\mathfrak{e}
, since each
F(\omega)
is continuous on
\mathfrak{X}
. By the uniqueness of the limit in
\mathfrak{X}
, it follows that
f=F(\cdot)\mathfrak{e}
almost everywhere, and hence
\mathfrak{e}\mapsto F(\cdot)\mathfrak{e}
is closed, as claimed. Now the Closed Graph Theorem for F-spaces,
[
61
, 2.15]
, implies that the said map is continuous.

Suppose for contradiction that
\|F\|_{L^{p}_{\operatorname{so}}(\Omega;\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X}))}=\infty
. Then we can find some
\mathfrak{e}_{n}\in\mathfrak{X}
of norm one such that
\|F(\cdot)\mathfrak{e}_{n}\|_{L^{p}(\Omega;\mathfrak{X})}>n
. But then
\frac{1}{n}\mathfrak{e}_{n}\to 0
in
\mathfrak{X}
, while
\|F(\cdot)\frac{1}{n}\mathfrak{e}_{n}\|_{L^{p}(\Omega;\mathfrak{X})}>1
, which contradicts the fact that these norms should converge to
0
by the proven continuity.
∎

Lemma 2.3
.

Under the assumptions of Lemma
2.2
, suppose in addition that
F(\omega)
is invertible at almost every
\omega\in\Omega
. Then the quasi-norm

\rho_{L^{p}(\Omega,F)}(\mathfrak{e}):=\|F(\cdot)\mathfrak{e}\|_{L^{p}(\Omega;\mathfrak{X})}

is qualitatively equivalent to
\|\mathfrak{e}\|
, i.e., the positive equivalence constants are allowed to depend on
p,\Omega,F
in an unspecified way.

Proof.

We already know from Lemma
2.2
that
\rho_{L^{p}(\Omega,F)}(\mathfrak{e})\lesssim_{p,\Omega,F}\|\mathfrak{e}\|
, so it remains to prove the converse.

We claim that the image
F\mathfrak{X}:=\{F(\cdot)\mathfrak{e}\in L^{p}(\Omega;\mathfrak{X}):\mathfrak{e}\in\mathfrak{X}\}
is a closed subspace of
L^{p}(\Omega;\mathfrak{X})
. Indeed, suppose that
F(\cdot)\mathfrak{e}_{n}\to f
in
L^{p}(\Omega;\mathfrak{X})
. Then a subsequence converges in
\mathfrak{X}
at almost every
\omega\in\Omega
. Removing another null set and applying
F(\omega)^{-1}
on both sides, we conclude that
\mathfrak{e}_{n}\to F(\omega)^{-1}f(\omega)
at almost every
\omega\in\Omega
. Since
\mathfrak{e}_{n}
is independent of
\omega
, this shows that
F(\omega)^{-1}f(\omega)\equiv\mathfrak{e}
at almost every
\omega\in\Omega
. Hence
f=F(\cdot)\mathfrak{e}\in F\mathfrak{X}
, which proves the closedness of
F\mathfrak{X}
.

As a closed subspace of the complete space
L^{p}(\Omega;\mathfrak{X})
, the space
F\mathfrak{X}
itself is complete. The map
\mathfrak{e}\mapsto F(\cdot)\mathfrak{e}
is obviously surjective from
\mathfrak{X}
to
F\mathfrak{X}
. By the pointwise invertibility of
F
, it is also injective. Now the Open Mapping Theorem for F-spaces,
[
61
, 2.12]
, shows that
F^{-1}:F\mathfrak{X}\to\mathfrak{X}:F(\cdot)\mathfrak{e}\mapsto\mathfrak{e}
is also continuous, which means that

\|\mathfrak{e}\|\lesssim_{p,\Omega,F}\rho_{L^{p}(\Omega,F)}(\mathfrak{e}).\qed

For the sake of easy reference, we record the following well-known fact about the inverse and the adjoint:

Remark 2.4
.

Let
\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{Y}
be Banach spaces and
A\in\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{Y})
have inverse
A^{-1}\in\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{Y},\mathfrak{X})
. Then one easily checks that the adjoint of the inverse,
(A^{-1})^{*}\in\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X}^{*},\mathfrak{Y}^{*})
, is the inverse of the adjoint
A^{*}\in\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{Y}^{*},\mathfrak{X}^{*})
. Thus, without ambiguity, we can simplify notation to write simply

A^{-*}:=(A^{-1})^{*}=(A^{*})^{-1}\in\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X}^{*},\mathfrak{Y}^{*}).

3.
Operator-valued weights

Let us start with a brief background for the setting that we are going to adopt.

A scalar-valued weight
0<w\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
may be equivalently seen as a modifier of the underlying measure, or of the function in the weighted
L^{p}
space, via the trivial correspondence

(3.1)

\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|f(x)|^{p}w(x)\,dx\Big)^{\frac{1}{p}}=\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|v(x)f(x)|^{p}\,dx\Big)^{\frac{1}{p}},

where

v:=w^{\frac{1}{p}}\in L^{p}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n}).

If
f:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{C}^{m}
and we would like to weigh its value by a matrix-valued weight, only the right-hand side of (
3.1
) gives a meaningful definition. Due to the tradition of viewing
w
in (
3.1
) as “the weight”, much of the matrix-weighted theory has been developed under the assumption that
W\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathscr{L}(\mathbb{C}^{m}))
is an almost-everywhere positive-definite-valued function, and by writing formulas in terms of the pointwise
p
-th root
V(x):=W(x)^{\frac{1}{p}}
, which is defined via the functional calculus of self-adjoint operators. This still makes perfect sense for operator weights on an infinite-dimensional Hilbert space as in
[
1
,
58
]
. However, already in the matrix-weighted theory, one can see that it is
de facto

V
, and not
W
, that is the primary object of study, and the repeated appearance of
p
-th roots in the formulas is mostly a notational nuisance only caused by the insistence of writing expression in terms of
W
instead of the more natural
V
.
This point becomes even more prominent when we pass to a general Banach space
\mathfrak{X}
.

Let us then motivate a notion of
\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X})
-valued weights
V
and weighted spaces
L^{p}(V)
of
\mathfrak{X}
-valued functions, modelled after the right-hand side of (
3.1
). It seems reasonable to require that

(1)

L^{p}(V)\subseteq L^{0}(\mathfrak{X})
;

(2)

L^{p}(V)\supseteq\{f\in L^{0}(\mathfrak{X})\text{ simple and boundedly supported}\}
;

(3)

given
f\in L^{0}(\mathfrak{X})
, we have
f\in L^{p}(V)
if and only if
Vf\in L^{p}(\mathfrak{X})
;

(4)

given
g\in L^{p}(\mathfrak{X})
, we have
V^{-1}g\in L^{p}(V)
.

Let
Q
be a cube and
\mathfrak{e}\in\mathfrak{X}
. Since
\mathbf{1}_{Q}\mathfrak{e}
is simple and boundedly supported, it follows that

\mathbf{1}_{Q}\mathfrak{e}\in L^{p}(V)
by (
2
), hence
\mathbf{1}_{Q}V\mathfrak{e}\subseteq L^{p}(\mathfrak{X})
by (
3
).
Being true for all cubes
Q
and
\mathfrak{e}\in\mathfrak{X}
, this shows that
V\in L^{p}_{\operatorname{so},\mathrm{loc}}(\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X}))
.

On the other hand, we also have
\mathbf{1}_{Q}\mathfrak{e}\in L^{p}(\mathfrak{X})
.
Hence
\mathbf{1}_{Q}V^{-1}\mathfrak{e}\in L^{p}(V)
by (
4
), thus
\mathbf{1}_{Q}V^{-1}\mathfrak{e}\in L^{0}(\mathfrak{X})
by (
1
).
Being true for all cubes
Q
and
\mathfrak{e}\in\mathfrak{X}
, this shows that
V^{-1}\in L^{0}_{\operatorname{so}}(\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X}))
.

Based on this, we give:

Definition 3.1
.

Let
\mathfrak{X}
be a Banach space and
V:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X})
.
Then
V
is called a
weight
if

(i)

V\in L^{0}_{\operatorname{so}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X}))
,

(ii)

V(x)
is invertible at a.e.
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and

(iii)

V^{-1}\in L^{0}_{\operatorname{so}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X}))
.

The following result clarifies the role of condition (
iii
) in Definition
3.1
:

Proposition 3.2
.

Let
\mathfrak{X}
be a Banach space and
V:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X})
be a function that satisfies (
i
) and (
ii
) of Definition
3.1
.

(i)

If
\mathfrak{X}
is separable, then
V
also satisfies (
iii
) of Definition
3.1
.

(ii)

Without separability, the previous conclusion fails, in general.

Proof.

(
i
): For a separable Banach space
\mathfrak{X}
, we recall (see
[
39
, Corollary 1.1.10]
) that a function
f:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathfrak{X}
is
strongly measurable
(a pointwise limit of simple functions) if and only if it is
measurable
(i.e., the inverse image
f^{-1}(B)
is a measurable subset of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
for every
B
in
\operatorname{Bor}(\mathfrak{X})
, the
Borel
\sigma
-algebra
generated by the open subsets of
\mathfrak{X}
). The argument that follows is more conveniently expressed via the latter notion of inverse images.

Let
\mathfrak{X}
and
V
be as in the assumptions. We consider the map

\Phi:\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathfrak{X}\to\mathfrak{X},(x,\mathfrak{e})\mapsto V(x)\mathfrak{e}.

This is a so-called
Carathéodory function

[
2
, Definition 14.74]
:

(i)

\Phi(\cdot,\mathfrak{e})=V(\cdot)\mathfrak{e}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathfrak{X}
is measurable for every
\mathfrak{e}\in\mathfrak{X}
. Indeed, this is the assumption that
V\in L^{0}_{\operatorname{so}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})
, combined with the above remark about measurability and strong measurability.

(ii)

\Phi(x,\cdot)=V(x):\mathfrak{X}\to\mathfrak{X}
is continuous. This is clear from
V(x)\in\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X})
.

By
[
2
, Lemma 14.75]
, Carathéodory functions are
jointly measurable
, i.e., for every closed
F\subseteq\mathfrak{X}
, the pre-image
\Phi^{-1}(F)
satisfies

\Phi^{-1}(F):=\{(x,\mathfrak{e})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathfrak{X}:\Phi(x,\mathfrak{e})\in F\}\in\operatorname{Leb}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\times\operatorname{Bor}(\mathfrak{X}),

where the right-hand side is the product
\sigma
-algebra. In particular, we can take
F=\{\mathfrak{u}\}
with
\mathfrak{u}\in\mathfrak{X}
. Then also

(3.2)

S:=\{(x,\mathfrak{e})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times B:\Phi(x,\mathfrak{e})\in\{\mathfrak{u}\}\}=\Phi^{-1}(\{\mathfrak{u}\})\cap(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times B)

belongs to
\operatorname{Leb}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\times\operatorname{Bor}(\mathfrak{X})
for every
\mathfrak{u}\in\mathfrak{X}
and
B\in\operatorname{Bor}(\mathfrak{X})
.

Since
\mathfrak{X}
is (in particular) a complete separable metric space,
[
45
, Theorem 12.3.4]
guarantees that, for every
S\in\operatorname{Leb}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\times\operatorname{Bor}(\mathfrak{X})
, its
\mathbb{R}^{n}
-projection

\pi(S):=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:\exists\mathfrak{e}\in\mathfrak{X},(x,\mathfrak{e})\in S\}

belongs to the completion (called the
Lebesgue extension
in
[
45
]
) of the
\sigma
-algebra
\operatorname{Leb}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Since
\operatorname{Leb}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is already complete, it follows that

\pi(S)\in\operatorname{Leb}(\mathbb{R}^{n}).

For the concrete
S
in (
3.2
), we observe that

\Phi(x,\mathfrak{e})\in\{\mathfrak{u}\}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad V(x)\mathfrak{e}=\mathfrak{u}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\mathfrak{e}=V(x)^{-1}\mathfrak{u},

hence

\exists\mathfrak{e}\in B:\Phi(x,\mathfrak{e})\in\{\mathfrak{u}\}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\exists\mathfrak{e}\in B:V(x)^{-1}\mathfrak{u}=\mathfrak{e}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad V(x)^{-1}\mathfrak{u}\in B,

and thus

\begin{split}\pi(S)&=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:\exists\mathfrak{e}\in B,\Phi(x,\mathfrak{e})\in\{\mathfrak{u}\}\}\\
&=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:V(x)^{-1}\mathfrak{u}\in B\}=(V(\cdot)^{-1}\mathfrak{u})^{-1}(B)\end{split}

is the inverse image of
B\in\operatorname{Bor}(\mathfrak{X})
under
V(\cdot)^{-1}\mathfrak{u}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathfrak{X}
.
Since these sets are measurable for all
B\in\operatorname{Bor}(\mathfrak{X})
, it means that the function
V(\cdot)^{-1}\mathfrak{u}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathfrak{X}
is measurable and hence strongly measurable. Valid for every
\mathfrak{u}\in\mathfrak{X}
, it means that
V(\cdot)^{-1}\in L^{0}_{\operatorname{so}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})
, which proves assertion (
i
).

(
ii
):
Consider the non-separable Hilbert space
\mathfrak{X}=\ell^{2}([0,\infty))
with the uncountable complete orthonormal system
(\mathfrak{u}_{t})_{t\in[0,\infty)}
. For each
t\in(0,1)
, we define
V(t)\in\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X})
via its action on this orthonormal system as follows:

V(t):\begin{cases}\mathfrak{u}_{t+n+1}\mapsto\mathfrak{u}_{t+n},&n\in\mathbb{N}:=\{0,1,2,\ldots\},\\
\mathfrak{u}_{t}\mapsto\mathfrak{u}_{0},\\
\mathfrak{u}_{n}\mapsto\mathfrak{u}_{n+1},&n\in\mathbb{N}\end{cases}

and
V(t):\mathfrak{u}_{s}\mapsto\mathfrak{u}_{s}
for all other
s\in[0,\infty)
. Since
V(t)
is a permutation of an orthonormal system, it is clearly invertible. For a function on
\mathbb{R}
, we can simply take
V(t)=I
for
t\in\mathbb{R}\setminus(0,1)
.

To check that
V\in L^{0}_{\operatorname{so}}(\mathbb{R};\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X}))
, let
\mathfrak{e}=\sum_{s\in[0,\infty)}x_{s}\mathfrak{u}_{s}\in\mathfrak{X}
. Note that at most countably many
x_{s}
are non-zero. Then

\begin{split}V(t)\mathfrak{e}&=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}x_{t+n+1}\mathfrak{u}_{t+n}+x_{t}\mathfrak{u}_{0}+\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}x_{n}\mathfrak{u}_{n+1}+\sum_{s\in[0,\infty)\setminus(\mathbb{N}\cup(t+\mathbb{N}))}x_{s}\mathfrak{u}_{s}\\
&=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(x_{t+n+1}-x_{t+n})\mathfrak{u}_{t+n}+x_{t}\mathfrak{u}_{0}+\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}x_{n}(\mathfrak{u}_{n+1}-\mathfrak{u}_{n})+\mathfrak{e}.\end{split}

The key observation is that, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we have
x_{t+n}=0
for a.e.
t\in(0,1)
, by the fact that at most countably many
x_{s}
are non-zero. Hence, indeed, several terms in the expansion of
V(t)\mathfrak{e}
vanish almost everywhere, and we arrive at the simpler formula

V(t)\mathfrak{e}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}x_{n}(\mathfrak{u}_{n+1}-\mathfrak{u}_{n})+\mathfrak{e}\quad\text{for a.e.}\quad t\in(0,1).

Thus, being a constant almost everywhere,
V(t)\mathfrak{e}
is clearly measurable.

On the other hand, the inverse
V(t)^{-1}
satisfies
V(t)^{-1}\mathfrak{u}_{0}=\mathfrak{u}_{t}
. Thus the range of
V(\cdot)^{-1}\mathfrak{u}_{0}
contains the uncountable orthonormal system
(\mathfrak{u}_{t})_{t\in(0,1)}
, which is clearly non-separable. Every strongly measurable function has a separable range, so
V(\cdot)^{-1}\mathfrak{u}_{0}\notin L^{0}(\mathfrak{X})
, and hence
V(\cdot)^{-1}\notin L^{0}_{\operatorname{so}}(\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X}))
.
∎

Definition 3.3
.

A weight is called a
p
-weight
, where
p\in(0,\infty]
, if

V\in L^{p}_{\mathrm{loc},\operatorname{so}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X})):=\left.\begin{cases}F\in L^{0}_{\operatorname{so}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X}));&\mathbf{1}_{Q}F\in L^{p}_{\operatorname{so}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X}))\\
&\text{ for all cubes }Q\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}\end{cases}\right\}

Definition 3.4
.

Let
\mathfrak{X}
be a Banach space,
p\in(0,\infty]
, and
V:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X})
be a
p
-weight. Then

(3.3)

L^{p}(V):=\{f\in L^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X}):Vf\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})\}

with norm

\|f\|_{L^{p}(V)}:=\|f\|_{L^{p}(V;\mathfrak{X})}:=\|Vf\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})}.

Remark 3.5
.

Lemma
2.1
provides the following elaboration of the meaning of the defining condition (
3.3
): Given
f\in L^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})
, we automatically know that
Vf\in L^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})
. Hence, the second condition that
Vf\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})
does not impose any additional measurability restrictions on
f
; it only amounts to checking a size condition on the measurable function
Vf
.

Remark 3.6
.

Specialising back to a Hilbert space
\mathfrak{X}=H
, we note that

\|A\mathfrak{e}\|_{H}^{2}=(A\mathfrak{e},A\mathfrak{e})=(A^{*}A\mathfrak{e},\mathfrak{e})=((A^{*}A)^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathfrak{e},(A^{*}A)^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathfrak{e})=\|(A^{*}A)^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathfrak{e}\|_{H}^{2},

where
(A^{*}A)^{\frac{1}{2}}
is a non-negative operator. Hence the general weighted norm

\|f\|_{L^{p}(V;H)}=\|f\|_{L^{p}((V^{*}V)^{\frac{1}{2}};H)}

always reduces to a norm with respect to a non-negative weight; moreover, the condition that
V
is invertible almost everywhere implies that
(V^{*}V)^{\frac{1}{2}}
is (strictly) positive-definite almost everywhere (as typically assumed in the Hilbert space theory).

The following result, collecting some basic facts about the
L^{p}(V)
spaces just introduced, is relatively routine, but illustrates the role of the different conditions that we imposed.

Proposition 3.7
.

Let
\mathfrak{X}
be a Banach space,
p\in(0,\infty]
, and
V:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X})
be a
p
-weight. Then

(i)

L^{p}(V)
is isometrically isomorphic to
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})
, where the isomorphisms are given by pointwise multiplication by
V:L^{p}(V)\to L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})
and its pointwise inverse
V^{-1}:L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})\to L^{p}(V)
.

(ii)

L^{p}(V)
is a Banach space if
p\geq 1
, or a quasi-Banach space if
p<1
.

(iii)

\{f\in L^{0}(\mathfrak{X})\text{ simple, boundedly supported}\}\subseteq L^{p}(V)
; it is dense if
p<\infty
.

Proof.

Let
f\in L^{p}(V)
. It follows directly from Definition
3.4
of
L^{p}(V)
that
Vf\in L^{p}(\mathfrak{X})
and
\|Vf\|_{L^{p}(\mathfrak{X})}=\|f\|_{L^{p}(V)}
. Hence
V:L^{p}(V)\to L^{p}(\mathfrak{X})
is an isometry.

Conversely, let
g\in L^{p}(\mathfrak{X})\subseteq L^{0}(\mathfrak{X})
. Since
V^{-1}\in L^{0}_{\operatorname{so}}(\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X}))
by Definition
3.1
(
iii
) of a weight, it follows from Lemma
2.1
that
f:=V^{-1}g\in L^{0}(\mathfrak{X})
. This function satisfies
Vf=VV^{-1}g=g\in L^{p}(\mathfrak{X})
by our assumption on
g
, and hence
f\in L^{p}(V)
by Definition
3.4
of
L^{p}(V)
. Moreover,
\|f\|_{L^{p}(V)}:=\|Vf\|_{L^{p}(\mathfrak{X})}=\|g\|_{L^{p}(\mathfrak{X})}
, which shows that
V^{-1}:L^{p}(\mathfrak{X})\to L^{p}(V)
is also an isometry.

It is evident that both
VV^{-1}
and
V^{-1}V
are identities, showing that the said isometries are isomorphisms between the spaces in question. This completes the proof of (
i
). Then (
ii
) follows from the corresponding well-known properties of the unweighted
L^{p}(\mathfrak{X})
spaces via the established isomorphism.

(
iii
): Let
f=\mathbf{1}_{E}\mathfrak{e}
, where
E
is measurable and boundedly supported and
\mathfrak{e}\in\mathfrak{X}
. Then
Vf=\mathbf{1}_{E}V\mathfrak{e}\in L^{p}(\mathfrak{X})
by the assumption that
V
is a
p
-weight. The claimed containment “
\subseteq
” then follows by linearity.

For density, consider some
f\in L^{p}(V)\subseteq L^{0}(\mathfrak{X})
. As a strongly measurable function, it has separable range, so indeed
f\in L^{0}(\mathfrak{Y})
for some separable subspace
\mathfrak{Y}\subseteq\mathfrak{X}
. Let
(\mathfrak{u}_{k})_{k=1}^{\infty}
be a dense sequence in
\mathfrak{Y}
. Then each
V\mathfrak{u}_{k}\in L^{0}(\mathfrak{X})
also has a separable range, and hence all of these countably many functions take values in some common separable subspace
\mathfrak{Z}\subseteq\mathfrak{X}
. By choosing a larger
\mathfrak{Z}
if necessary, we may assume without loss of generality that
\mathfrak{Y}\subseteq\mathfrak{Z}\subseteq\mathfrak{X}
. For all
\mathfrak{u}\in\mathfrak{Y}
, the function
V\mathfrak{u}
is the pointwise limit of some
V\mathfrak{u}_{k}
, so they also take values in
\mathfrak{Z}
. By linearity, the same is true for
Vh
for all simple
h\in L^{0}(\mathfrak{Y})
and, by another limit,

(3.4)

Vh\in L^{0}(\mathfrak{Z})\qquad\forall\ h\in L^{0}(\mathfrak{Y}).

Since
\mathfrak{Z}
is separable, and we can choose a countable dense sequence
(\mathfrak{v}_{k})_{k=1}^{\infty}
in the unit sphere of
\mathfrak{Z}
. For all
A\in\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X})
, it then follows that

\|A|_{\mathfrak{Z}}\|:=\sup\{\|A\mathfrak{v}\|_{\mathfrak{Z}}:\mathfrak{v}\in\mathfrak{Z},\|\mathfrak{v}\|_{\mathfrak{X}}\leq 1\}=\sup_{k\geq 1}\|A\mathfrak{v}_{k}\|_{\mathfrak{Z}}.

Thus, both

\|V(\cdot)|_{\mathfrak{Z}}\|=\sup_{k\geq 1}\|V(\cdot)\mathfrak{v}_{k}\|_{\mathfrak{X}},\qquad\|V(\cdot)^{-1}|_{\mathfrak{Z}}\|=\sup_{k\geq 1}\|V(\cdot)^{-1}\mathfrak{v}_{k}\|_{\mathfrak{X}}

are measurable real-valued functions. In particular, they are finite almost everywhere.
Hence the sets

E_{\lambda}:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:|x|\leq\lambda,\|V(x)|_{\mathfrak{Z}}\|\leq\lambda,\|V(x)^{-1}|_{\mathfrak{Z}}\|\leq\lambda\}

are measurable, and
\mathbf{1}_{E_{\lambda}}(x)\uparrow 1
almost everywhere as
\lambda\to\infty
.

From dominated convergence, it follows that
\mathbf{1}_{E_{\lambda}}f\to f
in
L^{p}(V)
; indeed, this is equivalent to
\mathbf{1}_{E_{\lambda}}Vf\to Vf
in
L^{p}(\mathfrak{X})
, which follows from the usual dominated convergence in
L^{p}(\mathfrak{X})
. Given
\varepsilon>0
, we choose
\lambda
large enough so that
g:=\mathbf{1}_{E_{\lambda}}f
satisfies

(3.5)

\|f-g\|_{L^{p}(V)}<\varepsilon.

For all functions of the form
h=\mathbf{1}_{E_{\lambda}}h\in L^{0}(\mathfrak{Y})
, we observe that

\|h\|_{L^{p}(V)}=\|Vh\|_{L^{p}(\mathfrak{X})}\leq\lambda\|h\|_{L^{p}(\mathfrak{X})},

since
h(x)\in\mathfrak{Y}\subseteq\mathfrak{Z}
and
\|V(x)|_{\mathfrak{Z}}\|\leq\lambda
for
x\in E_{\lambda}
, and that

\|h\|_{L^{p}(\mathfrak{X})}=\|V^{-1}Vh\|_{L^{p}(\mathfrak{X})}\leq\lambda\|Vh\|_{L^{p}(\mathfrak{X})}=\lambda\|h\|_{L^{p}(V)},

since
(Vh)(x)\in\mathfrak{Z}
for
h\in L^{0}(\mathfrak{Y})
by (
3.4
) and since
\|V(x)^{-1}|_{\mathfrak{Z}}\|\leq\lambda
for
x\in E_{\lambda}
.

Summarising the last two estimates, we have

(3.6)

\lambda^{-1}\|h\|_{L^{p}(V)}\leq\|h\|_{L^{p}(\mathfrak{X})}\leq\lambda\|h\|_{L^{p}(V)}\qquad\forall\ h=\mathbf{1}_{E_{\lambda}}h\in L^{0}(\mathfrak{Y}).

Applying this estimate to
g:=\mathbf{1}_{E_{\lambda}}f\in L^{p}(V)\cap L^{0}(\mathfrak{Y})
in place of
h
, we see that
g\in L^{p}(\mathfrak{Y})
. By the density of simple functions in this unweighted space we can find a simple
s\in L^{p}(\mathfrak{Y})
with
\|g-s\|_{L^{p}(\mathfrak{Y})}<\varepsilon/\lambda
. Replacing
s
by
\mathbf{1}_{E_{\lambda}}s
is necessary, we can assume that
s=\mathbf{1}_{E_{\lambda}}s
.

Then we apply (
3.6
) to
h=g-s
to see that

(3.7)

\|g-s\|_{L^{p}(V)}\leq\lambda\|g-s\|_{L^{p}}<\lambda\frac{\varepsilon}{\lambda}=\varepsilon.

Combining (
3.5
) and (
3.7
), for any given
f\in L^{p}(V)
and
\varepsilon>0
, we have found a simple, boundedly supported
s\in L^{0}(\mathfrak{X})
such that
\|f-s\|_{L^{p}(V)}\lesssim\varepsilon
, which proves the claimed density.
∎

4.
Operator-valued
A_{p}
weights

Having discussed operator-valued weights at large, we next investigate more specific assumptions to be imposed on these weights in analogy with the Muckenhoupt
A_{p}
conditions that are well studied for scalar and matrix-valued weights. Our setting is modelled after that of
[
55
,
66
]
, which is phrased in terms of certain integral (quasi-)norms induced by the weights. We start with some generalities concerning these objects:

Definition 4.1
.

Let
\Omega
be a measure space,
\mathfrak{X}
be a Banach space,
u\in(0,\infty]
, and
V\in L_{\operatorname{so}}^{u}(\Omega;\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X}))
. Then, for
\mathfrak{e}\in\mathfrak{X}
, we define the
quasi-norm

\rho_{L^{u}(\Omega,V)}(\mathfrak{e}):=\|V(\cdot)\mathfrak{e}\|_{L^{u}(\Omega;\mathfrak{X})}.

If, in addition,
V
is invertible almost everywhere and

V^{-*}\in L_{\operatorname{so}}^{v}(\Omega;\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X}^{*})),

we can apply
the same definition to
\mathfrak{e}^{*}\in\mathfrak{X}^{*}
to obtain a
quasi-norm

\rho_{L^{v}(\Omega,V^{-*})}(\mathfrak{e}^{*}):=\|V(\cdot)^{-*}\mathfrak{e}^{*}\|_{L^{v}(\Omega;\mathfrak{X}^{*})}.

Lemma 4.2
.

If
\Omega
is a probability space, then for all
u,v\in(0,\infty]
, we have

(4.1)

|\langle\mathfrak{e}^{*},\mathfrak{e}\rangle|\leq\rho_{L^{u}(\Omega,V)}(\mathfrak{e})\rho_{L^{v}(\Omega,V^{-*})}(\mathfrak{e}^{*}).

Proof.

Since
\rho_{L^{u}(\Omega,V)}(\mathfrak{e})\leq\rho_{L^{\infty}(\Omega,V)}(\mathfrak{e})
by Hölder’s inequality and similarly for the second term on the right, it suffices to consider
u,v\in(0,\infty)
.
At all points of invertibility of
V
, we have

|\langle\mathfrak{e}^{*},\mathfrak{e}\rangle|=|\langle V(\omega)^{-*}\mathfrak{e}^{*},V(\omega)\mathfrak{e}\rangle|.

Raising this to power
s\in(0,\infty)
, integrating over
\omega\in\Omega
(minus a possible null set, where
V
fails to be invertible, but this has no effect on the result), and applying Hölder’s inequality with exponent
q\in(1,\infty)
, it follows that

(4.2)

|\langle\mathfrak{e}^{*},\mathfrak{e}\rangle|\leq\rho_{L^{sq}(\Omega,V)}(\mathfrak{e})\rho_{L^{sq^{\prime}}(\Omega,V^{-*})}(\mathfrak{e}^{*}).

Given
u,v\in(0,\infty)
, take
s:=\frac{uv}{u+v}\in(0,\infty)
and
q:=\frac{u+v}{v}\in(1,\infty)
so that
q^{\prime}=\frac{u+v}{u}
. Then
sq=u
and
sq^{\prime}=v
, so that (
4.2
) reduces to the claimed (
4.1
).
∎

For any quasi-norm
\rho
on
\mathfrak{X}
, we define the
dual norm
on
\mathfrak{X}^{*}
by

(4.3)

\rho^{*}(\mathfrak{e}^{*}):=\sup_{\mathfrak{e}\in\mathfrak{X}\setminus\{0\}}\frac{|\langle\mathfrak{e}^{*},\mathfrak{e}\rangle|}{\rho(\mathfrak{e})}.

Remark 4.3
.

The “dual norm”
\rho^{*}
is indeed a norm, even if the original
\rho
is just a quasi-norm. This is immediate from the defining formula.

Likewise, for a quasi-norm on
\mathfrak{X}^{*}
, its dual norm is defined on
\mathfrak{X}^{**}
, but can in particular be restricted to
\mathfrak{X}\subseteq\mathfrak{X}^{**}
via the canonical embedding.

Directly from (
4.1
) and (
4.3
), it follows:

Corollary 4.4
.

For all
u,v\in(0,\infty]
,

\begin{split}\rho_{L^{u}(\Omega,V)}^{*}(\mathfrak{e}^{*})&\leq\rho_{L^{v}(\Omega,V^{-*})}(\mathfrak{e}^{*}),\qquad\mathfrak{e}^{*}\in\mathfrak{X}^{*},\\
\rho_{L^{v}(\Omega,V^{-*})}^{*}(\mathfrak{e})&\leq\rho_{L^{u}(\Omega,V)}(\mathfrak{e}),\qquad\mathfrak{e}\in\mathfrak{X}.\end{split}

When
\Omega=Q\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a cube equipped with the normalised measure
dx/|Q|
, we denote
L^{p}(\Omega,V)=\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,V)
. In this situation, it is of interest to be able to reverse the inequalities of Corollary
4.4
, which leads to:

Definition 4.5
.

Let
p\in(0,\infty)
and
\mathfrak{X}
be a Banach space. Let
V:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X})
.
We say that

V\in\mathscr{A}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X})),

or that
V
satisfies the
\mathscr{A}_{p}
condition if
V
is a
p
-weight and the following condition holds with some finite constant
C
and for all cubes
Q\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
:

(i)

if
p\in(1,\infty)
, then also
V^{-*}
is a
p^{\prime}
-weight and

\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(Q,V^{-*})}(\mathfrak{e}^{*})\leq C\cdot\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,V)}^{*}(\mathfrak{e}^{*})\qquad\text{for all }\mathfrak{e}^{*}\in\mathfrak{X}^{*},

(ii)

if
p\in(0,1]
, then

\operatornamewithlimits{ess\,sup}_{y\in Q}\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,V)}(V(y)^{-1}\mathfrak{e})\leq C\|\mathfrak{e}\|\quad\text{for all }\mathfrak{e}\in\mathfrak{X}.

In either case, we denote the smallest such
C
by
[V]_{\mathscr{A}_{p}}
.

Remark 4.6
.

For
p\in(1,\infty)
, Definition
4.5
(
i
) agrees with the definition of the matrix
A_{p}
condition in both
[
55
, Eq.
(\mathcal{A}_{p,q})
on p. 21]
and
[
66
, Definition on p. 450]
. For
p\in(0,1]
, Definition
4.5
(
ii
) is a natural extension of
[
26
, Eq. (1.2)]
. Literally, their defining condition reads as

(4.4)

\operatornamewithlimits{ess\,sup}_{y\in Q}\Big(\fint_{Q}\|V(x)V(y)^{-1}\|_{\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X})}^{p}\,dx\Big)^{\frac{1}{p}}\lesssim 1,

where
\mathfrak{X}=\mathbb{C}^{m}
. However, in this finite-dimensional case, we have the norm equivalence

\|A\|_{\mathscr{L}(\mathbb{C}^{m})}\approx\sum_{i=1}^{m}\|A\mathfrak{u}_{k}\|_{\mathbb{C}^{m}},

where
(\mathfrak{u}_{k})_{k=1}^{m}
is a fixed orthonormal basis, from which it easily follows that (
4.4
) is equivalent to

(4.5)

\operatornamewithlimits{ess\,sup}_{y\in Q}\Big(\fint_{Q}\|V(x)V(y)^{-1}\mathfrak{e}\|_{\mathfrak{X}}^{p}\,dx\Big)^{\frac{1}{p}}\lesssim\|\mathfrak{e}\|_{\mathfrak{X}}

for all
\mathfrak{e}\in\mathfrak{X}=\mathbb{C}^{m}
. And this is precisely Definition
4.5
(
ii
). Thus, our definition is obtained by writing a condition equivalent to
[
26
, Eq. (1.2)]
in
\mathbb{C}^{m}
, and then extending this equivalent definition to general
\mathfrak{X}
. Note that the difference of (
4.4
) and (
4.5
) is similar to various other situations encountered in harmonic analysis of operator-valued functions, where useful conditions often involve integral norms of the operator-valued function evaluated at a fixed vector
\mathfrak{e}
as in (
4.5
), instead of operator norms as in (
4.4
); cf.
[
41
, Definition 11.2.1 and the Notes on page 71]
.

We next present some equivalent forms and consequences of the
\mathscr{A}_{p}
conditions. For the case
p\leq 1
, the following lemma will be useful:

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
\rho
be a
p
-norm on
\mathfrak{X}
with
p\in(0,\infty)
and let
G,H\in L^{0}_{\operatorname{so}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X}))
. Let
Q\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be measurable.
If for every
\mathfrak{e}\in\mathfrak{X}
, we have

(4.6)

\rho(G(y)\mathfrak{e})\leq C\|H(y)\mathfrak{e}\|\quad\text{for a.e. }y\in Q,

then for every
f\in L^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})
, we have

(4.7)

\rho(G(y)f(y))\leq C\|H(y)f(y)\|\quad\text{for a.e. }y\in Q.

Proof.

A strongly measurable function has a separable range by
[
39
, Theorem 1.1.6]
. Hence, we can pick a sequence
(\mathfrak{e}_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}
that is dense in
\{f(y):y\in Q\}
. By the assumed condition (
4.6
), we have

\rho(G(y)\mathfrak{e}_{n})\leq C\|H(y)\mathfrak{e}_{n}\|\quad\text{for all $n$ and all }y\in Q\setminus N_{n},

where
N_{n}
has measure zero. In particular, we have

\rho(G(y)\mathfrak{e}_{n})\leq C\|H(y)\mathfrak{e}_{n}\|\quad\text{for all $n$ and all }y\in Q\setminus N,

where
N:=\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}N_{n}
also has measure zero. For each
y\in Q\setminus N
, we can choose a subsequence of
\mathfrak{e}_{n}
such that
\mathfrak{e}_{n}\to f(y)
. Since both
\mathfrak{e}\mapsto\rho(G(y)\mathfrak{e})
and
\mathfrak{e}\mapsto\|H(y)\mathfrak{e}\|
are continuous, it follows that

\rho(G(y)f(y))\leq C\|H(y)f(y)\|\quad\text{for all }y\in Q\setminus N.

This is exactly the claim (
4.7
)
∎

Proposition 4.8
.

Let
p\in(0,1]
and
V:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X})
be a
p
-weight. Then the following conditions, each to hold for some finite constant
C
and all cubes
Q\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
, are equivalent:

(i)

V\in\mathscr{A}_{p}
in the sense of Definition
4.5
(
ii
), i.e., for all
\mathfrak{e}\in\mathfrak{X}
, we have

\operatornamewithlimits{ess\,sup}_{y\in Q}\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,V)}(V(y)^{-1}\mathfrak{e})\leq C\|\mathfrak{e}\|_{\mathfrak{X}}.

(ii)

For all
\mathfrak{e}\in\mathfrak{X}
, we have

\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,V)}(\mathfrak{e})\leq C\operatornamewithlimits{ess\,inf}_{y\in Q}\|V(y)\mathfrak{e}\|_{\mathfrak{X}}.

(iii)

For all
f\in L^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})
, we have

\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,V)}(f(y))\leq C\|V(y)f(y)\|_{\mathfrak{X}}\quad\text{for a.e. }y\in Q.

The minimal
C
in all these conditions are equal to each other and to
[V]_{\mathscr{A}_{p}}
.

Proof.

We apply Lemma
4.7
with the
p
-norm
\rho=\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,V)}
and different choices of
G
and
H
:

(
ii
)
\Rightarrow
(
iii
): The condition in (
ii
) is (
4.6
) with
G=I
and
H=V
. The condition in (
iii
) is (
4.7
) with these same
G
and
H
. Hence the implication follows from Lemma
4.7
with these choices.

(
iii
)
\Rightarrow
(
ii
): This is immediate by considering all constant functions
f\equiv\mathfrak{e}
.

(
i
)
\Rightarrow
(
iii
): The condition in (
i
) is (
4.6
) with
G=V^{-1}
(which belongs to
L^{0}_{\operatorname{so}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X}))
, since
V
is assumed to be a weight) and
H=I
, the identity. On the other hand, the condition in (
iii
) is (
4.7
) with these same
G
and
H
, and
Vf\in L^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})
(by Lemma
2.1
) in place of
f
. Hence the implication follows from Lemma
4.7
with these choices.

(
iii
)
\Rightarrow
(
i
): This follows by considering all functions of the form
f=V(\cdot)^{-1}\mathfrak{e}
(which belong to
L^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})
, since
V
is assumed to be a weight).
∎

As explained in Remark
4.6
, motivated by the earlier definitions for matrix weights, we have stated Definition
4.5
in terms of norms of
\mathfrak{e}^{*}\in\mathfrak{X}^{*}
for
p\in(1,\infty)
, and (quasi)norms of
\mathfrak{e}\in\mathfrak{X}
for
p\in(0,1]
. However, we next show that the case
p\in(1,\infty)
can also be equivalently expressed in terms of norms of
\mathfrak{e}\in\mathfrak{X}
. The following result is an extension of
[
66
, Proposition 1.2(2)]
, where the case
\mathfrak{X}=\mathbb{C}^{m}\simeq\mathfrak{X}^{*}
is treated. The extension is not entirely straightforward, owing some subtleties of the duality of
L^{p}
spaces of Banach space-valued functions.

Proposition 4.9
.

Let
p\in(1,\infty)
, and let
V:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X})
be a
p
-weight such that
V^{-*}
is a
p^{\prime}
-weight. Then the following conditions, each to hold with some finite constant
C
and all cubes
Q\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
, are equivalent:

(i)

V\in\mathscr{A}_{p}
in the sense of Definition
4.5
(
i
), i.e., for all
\mathfrak{e}^{*}\in\mathfrak{X}^{*}
,

\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(Q,V^{-*})}(\mathfrak{e}^{*})\leq C\cdot\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,V)}^{*}(\mathfrak{e}^{*}).

(ii)

For all
\mathfrak{e}\in\mathfrak{X}
,

\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,V)}(\mathfrak{e})\leq C\cdot\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(Q,V^{-*})}^{*}(\mathfrak{e}).

The best constants in both conditions coincide with
[V]_{\mathscr{A}_{p}}
.

Proof.

(
i
)
\Rightarrow
(
ii
):
By the elementary duality of vector-valued
L^{p}
spaces,

\begin{split}\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,V)}(\mathfrak{e})&=\|V(\cdot)\mathfrak{e}\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q;\mathfrak{X})}\\
&=\sup\Big\{\Big|\fint_{Q}\langle V(\cdot)\mathfrak{e},g\rangle\Big|;\|g\|_{\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(Q;\mathfrak{X}^{*})}\leq 1\Big\}.\end{split}

For any fixed
g
as above, the mapping
\mathfrak{e}\mapsto\fint_{Q}\langle V(\cdot)\mathfrak{e},g\rangle

defines an element of
\mathfrak{X}^{*}
, which we denote by
\mathfrak{e}^{*}
.
Thus

\begin{split}\Big|\fint_{Q}\langle V(\cdot)\mathfrak{e},g\rangle\Big|=|\langle\mathfrak{e},\mathfrak{e}^{*}\rangle|&\leq\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(Q,V^{-*})}^{*}(\mathfrak{e})\cdot\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(Q,V^{-*})}(\mathfrak{e}^{*})\\
&\leq\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(Q,V^{-*})}^{*}(\mathfrak{e})\cdot[V]_{\mathscr{A}_{p}}\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,V)}^{*}(\mathfrak{e}^{*})\end{split}

by the assumed
\mathscr{A}_{p}
property.
Applying the definition of dual norms and the specific choice of
\mathfrak{e}^{*}
,
we obtain, for any
\mathfrak{u}\in\mathfrak{X}
,

\displaystyle|\langle\mathfrak{u},\mathfrak{e}^{*}\rangle|=\Big|\fint_{Q}\langle V(\cdot)\mathfrak{u},g\rangle\Big|\leq\|V(\cdot)\mathfrak{u}\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q;\mathfrak{X})}\|g\|_{\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(Q;\mathfrak{X}^{*})}\leq\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,V)}(\mathfrak{u}),

and hence
\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,V)}^{*}(\mathfrak{e}^{*})\leq 1
,
which concludes the proof of (
ii
).

(
ii
)
\Rightarrow
(
i
):
This argument follows the same broad outline, but some details are slightly trickier.
By duality, again,

(4.8)

\begin{split}\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(Q,V^{-*})}(\mathfrak{e}^{*})&=\|V(\cdot)^{-*}\mathfrak{e}^{*}\|_{\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(Q;\mathfrak{X}^{*})}\\
&=\sup\Big\{\Big|\fint_{Q}\langle V(\cdot)^{-*}\mathfrak{e}^{*},f\rangle\Big|;\|f\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q;\mathfrak{X})}\leq 1\Big\},\end{split}

but we wish to show that the supremum can be restricted to a somewhat smaller set.

Recall that
V^{-1}\in L^{0}_{\operatorname{so}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X}))
(as part of the definition that
V
is a weight). Hence, for
f\in L^{p}(Q;\mathfrak{X})\subseteq L^{0}(Q;\mathfrak{X})
, we have
V^{-1}f\in L^{0}(Q;\mathfrak{X})
by Lemma
2.1
. In particular, the sets
E_{k}:=\{x\in Q:\|V(x)f(x)\|_{\mathfrak{X}}\leq k\}
are measurable and exhaust
Q
as
k\to\infty
. Since
\langle V(\cdot)^{-*}\mathfrak{e}^{*},f\rangle\in L^{1}(Q)
(as the pointwise pairing of
V(\cdot)^{-*}\mathfrak{e}^{*}\in L^{p^{\prime}}(Q;\mathfrak{X}^{*})
and
f\in L^{p}(Q;\mathfrak{X})
), dominated convergence guarantees that

(4.9)

\fint_{Q}\langle V(\cdot)^{-*}\mathfrak{e}^{*},f\rangle=\lim_{k\to\infty}\fint_{Q}\langle V(\cdot)^{-*}\mathfrak{e}^{*},\mathbf{1}_{E_{k}}f\rangle.

Like the original
f
, the truncated functions
f_{k}:=\mathbf{1}_{E_{k}}f
satisfy
\|f_{k}\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q;\mathfrak{X})}\leq 1
, but also, by construction, the pointwise bound
\|V^{-1}f_{k}\|_{\mathfrak{X}}\leq k
. We already observed that measurability
V^{-1}f\in L^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})
, and hence
V^{-1}f_{k}\in L^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})
. Combined with the pointwise bound, it follows that
V^{-1}f_{k}\in L^{\infty}(Q;\mathfrak{X})\subseteq L^{1}(Q;\mathfrak{X})
.

Thanks to the limit (
4.9
), the supremum in (
4.8
) is still reached if we only consider the truncated
f_{k}
in place of
f
. Denoting these simply by
f
again, we see that

(4.10)

\begin{split}&\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(Q,V^{-*})}(\mathfrak{e}^{*})\\
&\quad=\sup\Big\{\Big|\fint_{Q}\langle V(\cdot)^{-*}\mathfrak{e}^{*},f\rangle\Big|;\|f\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q;\mathfrak{X})}\leq 1,V^{-1}f\in L^{1}(Q;\mathfrak{X})\Big\}.\end{split}

For
f
as in (
4.10
), we can write

(4.11)

\fint_{Q}\langle V(\cdot)^{-*}\mathfrak{e}^{*},f\rangle=\fint_{Q}\langle\mathfrak{e}^{*},V^{-1}f\rangle=\Big\langle\mathfrak{e}^{*},\fint_{Q}V^{-1}f\Big\rangle=:\langle\mathfrak{e}^{*},\mathfrak{e}\rangle,

where the reduction from (
4.8
) to (
4.10
) was needed to guarantee the existence of the integral
\mathfrak{e}:=\fint_{Q}V^{-1}f\in\mathfrak{X}
. (Arguing in a simpler way as in the first part of the proof, we would have only obtained an element
\mathfrak{e}^{**}
of the second dual
\mathfrak{X}^{**}
, which would not be sufficient for what follows, since our assumption (
ii
) only involved vectors
\mathfrak{e}\in\mathfrak{X}
.)

Continuing from (
4.11
) and using assumption (
ii
), we obtain

\begin{split}\Big|\fint_{Q}\langle V(\cdot)^{-*}\mathfrak{e}^{*},f\rangle\Big|=|\langle\mathfrak{e}^{*},\mathfrak{e}\rangle|&\leq\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,V)}^{*}(\mathfrak{e}^{*})\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,V)}(\mathfrak{e})\\
&\leq\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,V)}^{*}(\mathfrak{e}^{*})\cdot C\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(Q,V^{-*})}^{*}(\mathfrak{e}).\end{split}

Applying the definition of the dual norm to estimate the last factor, for
\mathfrak{u}^{*}\in\mathfrak{X}^{*}
, we have

\begin{split}|\langle\mathfrak{u}^{*},\mathfrak{e}\rangle|&=\Big|\fint_{Q}\langle\mathfrak{u}^{*},V^{-1}f\rangle\Big|=\Big|\fint_{Q}\langle V(\cdot)^{-*}\mathfrak{u}^{*},f\rangle\Big|\\
&\leq\|V(\cdot)^{-*}\mathfrak{u}^{*}\|_{\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(Q;\mathfrak{X}^{*})}\|f\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q;\mathfrak{X})}\leq\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(Q,V^{-*})}(\mathfrak{u}^{*})\cdot 1.\end{split}

Hence

\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(Q,V^{-*})}^{*}(\mathfrak{e})=\sup_{\mathfrak{u}^{*}\in\mathfrak{X}^{*}\setminus\{0\}}\frac{|\langle\mathfrak{u}^{*},\mathfrak{e}\rangle|}{\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(Q,V^{-*})}(\mathfrak{u}^{*})}\leq 1,

thus

\Big|\fint_{Q}\langle V(\cdot)^{-*}\mathfrak{e}^{*},f\rangle\Big|\leq C\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,V)}^{*}(\mathfrak{e}^{*}),

and substitution into (
4.10
) shows that

\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(Q,V^{-*})}(\mathfrak{e}^{*})\leq C\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,V)}^{*}(\mathfrak{e}^{*}).

This is condition (
i
) that we wanted to prove.
∎

Corollary 4.10
.

Let
p\in(0,\infty)
and
V
be a
p
-weights. If
p\in(1,\infty)
, we also assume that
V^{-*}
is a
p^{\prime}
-weight. Then
V\in\mathscr{A}_{p}
if and only if there is a finite positive constant
C
such that, for all cubes
Q\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\mathfrak{e}\in\mathfrak{X}
,

\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,V)}(\mathfrak{e})\leq C\begin{cases}\rho^{*}_{\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(Q,V^{-*})}(\mathfrak{e}),&p\in(1,\infty),\\
\displaystyle\operatornamewithlimits{ess\,inf}_{y\in Q}\|V(y)\mathfrak{e}\|_{\mathfrak{X}},&p\in(0,1].\end{cases}

The best constant
C
is equal to
[V]_{\mathscr{A}_{p}}
.

Proof.

For
p\in(1,\infty)
, this is Proposition
4.9
. For
p\in(0,1]
, this is contained in Proposition
4.8
.
∎

Corollary 4.11
.

If
p,r\in(0,\infty)
and
V\in\mathscr{A}_{p}
, then,
for any
Q\in\mathscr{D}
and
\mathfrak{e}\in\mathfrak{X}
,

\displaystyle\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,V)}(\mathfrak{e})\lesssim\rho_{\textit{\L}^{r}(Q,V)}(\mathfrak{e}).

Proof.

If
p\in(0,1]
, then, by Corollary
4.10
, we immediately
find

\displaystyle\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,V)}(\mathfrak{e})\lesssim\operatornamewithlimits{ess\,inf}_{y\in Q}\|V(y)\mathfrak{e}\|_{\mathfrak{X}}\leq\rho_{\textit{\L}^{r}(Q,V)}(\mathfrak{e}).

On the other hand, if
p\in(1,\infty)
, then, combining
Corollaries
4.10
and
4.4
, we obtain

\displaystyle\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,V)}(\mathfrak{e})\lesssim\rho^{*}_{\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(Q,V^{-*})}(\mathfrak{e})\lesssim\rho_{\textit{\L}^{r}(Q,V)}(\mathfrak{e}).

Hence, we finish the proof of Corollary
4.11
.
∎

5.
Reverse Hölder properties

In this section, we explore versions of reverse Hölder inequalities satisfied by operator
\mathscr{A}_{p}
weights. This may at first seem to contradict
[
46
,
58
]
, where counterexamples are given to show that the
\mathscr{A}_{2}
condition does
not
imply a reverse Hölder inequality for operator-valued weights in infinite dimensions. The example sketched in
[
46
, paragraph after Corollary 4.7]
is very simple;
[
58
, Theorem 5.1]
provides a more elaborate example to show that even the weighted
L^{2}
boundedness of the Hilbert transform, which is known to be strictly stronger than the
\mathscr{A}_{2}
condition in infinite dimensions, still does not imply a reverse Hölder inequality. However, both
[
46
,
58
]
interpret the notion of a reverse Hölder inequality in the sense introduced in
[
17
, Eq. (2.1)]
for matrix weights; notwithstanding its established merits in the finite-dimensional theory, we wish to argue that this is no longer the “right” definition in infinite dimensions. The support for this argument comes, on the one hand, from the counterexamples of
[
46
,
58
]
just mentioned and, on the other hand, from our proposal of a substitute notion and the results that we are able to obtain by this alternate version.

Proposition 5.1
.

If
V\in\mathscr{A}_{p}
, then there are
\varepsilon,\eta>0
such that

\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p+\varepsilon}(Q,V)}(\mathfrak{e})\lesssim\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,V)}(\mathfrak{e})

and

\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}+\eta}(Q,V^{-*})}(\mathfrak{e}^{*})\lesssim\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(Q,V^{-*})}(\mathfrak{e}^{*})

for all cubes
Q\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
and all
\mathfrak{e}\in\mathfrak{X}
and
\mathfrak{e}^{*}\in\mathfrak{X}^{*}
.

Proof.

We note that the second inequality is trivial for
p^{\prime}=\infty=p^{\prime}+\eta
, so it is enough to consider
p\in(1,\infty)
in the part of the proof involving norms on
\mathfrak{X}^{*}
.

From Lemma
4.2
and the definition of the dual norm, it follows that

(5.1)

\begin{split}\rho_{\textit{\L}^{v}(Q,V^{-*})}^{*}(\mathfrak{e})&\leq\rho_{\textit{\L}^{u}(Q,V)}(\mathfrak{e}),\\
\rho_{\textit{\L}^{u}(Q,V)}^{*}(\mathfrak{e}^{*})&\leq\rho_{\textit{\L}^{v}(Q,V^{-*})}(\mathfrak{e}^{*})\end{split}

for all
u,v\in(0,\infty]
and all cubes
Q\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
Then, from
V\in\mathscr{A}_{p}
we deduce the following:

(5.2)

\begin{split}\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,V)}(\mathfrak{e})&\leq[V]_{\mathscr{A}_{p}}\left.\begin{cases}\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(Q,V^{-*})}^{*}(\mathfrak{e}),&\text{if $p>1$}\\
\displaystyle\operatornamewithlimits{ess\,inf}_{y\in Q}\|V(y)\mathfrak{e}\|_{\mathfrak{X}},&\text{if $p\leq 1$}\end{cases}\right\}\quad\text{by Corollary \ref {cor:Ap}}\\
&\leq[V]_{\mathscr{A}_{p}}\cdot\rho_{\textit{\L}^{u}(Q,V)}(\mathfrak{e})\\
\end{split}

by (
5.1
) in the first case and a trivial estimate in the second one.
Similarly, for
\mathfrak{e}^{*}\in\mathfrak{X}^{*}
, where we only consider
p\in(1,\infty)
, it follows that

\begin{split}\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(Q,V^{-*})}(\mathfrak{e}^{*})&\leq[V]_{\mathscr{A}_{p}}\cdot\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,V)}^{*}(\mathfrak{e}^{*})\quad\text{by Definition \ref {def:Ap}}\\
&\leq[V]_{\mathscr{A}_{p}}\cdot\rho_{\textit{\L}^{v}(Q,V^{-*})}(\mathfrak{e}^{*})\quad\text{by \eqref{eq:uvHolderDual}}.\\
\end{split}

Let us now fix some
u\in(0,p)
and
v\in(0,p^{\prime})
. Spelling out the previous inequalities, they say that, for all cubes
Q\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

\begin{split}\Big(\fint_{Q}\|V(y)\mathfrak{e}\|^{p}dy\Big)^{\frac{1}{p}}&\leq[V]_{\mathscr{A}_{p}}\Big(\fint_{Q}\|V(y)\mathfrak{e}\|^{u}dy\Big)^{\frac{1}{u}},\\
\Big(\fint_{Q}\|V^{-*}(y)\mathfrak{e}^{*}\|^{p^{\prime}}dy\Big)^{\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}}&\leq[V]_{\mathscr{A}_{p}}\Big(\fint_{Q}\|V^{-*}(y)\mathfrak{e}^{*}\|^{v}dy\Big)^{\frac{1}{v}}.\end{split}

These are classical reverse Hölder inequalities for the functions
\|V(\cdot)\mathfrak{e}\|^{u}
and
\|V^{-*}(\cdot)\mathfrak{e}^{*}\|^{v}
with exponents
p/u>1
and
p^{\prime}/v
, respectively. By the classical Gehring lemma
[
30
, Lemma 3]
, the same functions satisfy the reverse Hölder inequality with slightly larger exponents, which we may write in the form
(p+\varepsilon)/u
and
(p^{\prime}+\eta)/v
with
\varepsilon,\eta>0
. This means that

\begin{split}\Big(\fint_{Q}\|V(y)\mathfrak{e}\|^{p+\varepsilon}dy\Big)^{\frac{1}{p+\varepsilon}}&\lesssim\Big(\fint_{Q}\|V(y)\mathfrak{e}\|^{p}dy\Big)^{\frac{1}{p}},\\
\Big(\fint_{Q}\|V^{-*}(y)\mathfrak{e}^{*}\|^{p^{\prime}+\eta}dy\Big)^{\frac{1}{p^{\prime}+\eta}}&\lesssim\Big(\fint_{Q}\|V^{-*}(y)\mathfrak{e}\|^{p^{\prime}}dy\Big)^{\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}},\end{split}

which are precisely the claimed inequalities.
∎

6.
Comparing quasi-norms at different cubes

Starting with
[
60
]
, a significant role in the analysis of matrix-weighted Besov and Triebel–Lizorkin spaces has been played by estimates for expressions of the type

\|A_{Q}A_{R}^{-1}\|,

where
A_{Q},A_{R}
are the reducing operators related to the John ellipsoids of the (quasi)norms
\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,V)}
and
\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(R,V)}
. In our framework, where the reducing operators are not available, these will be replaced by analogous control of the ratios

\frac{\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,V)}(\mathfrak{e})}{\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(R,V)}(\mathfrak{e})},

uniformly over
\mathfrak{e}\in\mathfrak{X}\setminus\{0\}
. This has some general similarity with
[
46
, e.g. Eq. (4.1)]
, but our considerations will almost immediately take a different directions from those of
[
46
]
.

Lemma 6.1
.

For cubes
Q\subseteq S
, we have

(6.1)

\Big(\frac{|Q|}{|S|}\Big)^{\frac{1}{p}}\leq\frac{\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(S,V)}(\mathfrak{e})}{\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,V)}(\mathfrak{e})}\leq[V]_{\mathscr{A}_{p}}\Big(\frac{|S|}{|Q|}\Big)^{\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}},

where the first estimate does not require that
V\in\mathscr{A}_{p}
. If
V\in\mathscr{A}_{p}
and
\varepsilon,\eta>0
are as in Proposition
5.1
, we also have

(6.2)

\Big(\frac{|Q|}{|S|}\Big)^{\frac{1}{p+\varepsilon}}\lesssim\frac{\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(S,V)}(\mathfrak{e})}{\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,V)}(\mathfrak{e})}\lesssim\Big(\frac{|S|}{|Q|}\Big)^{\frac{1}{p^{\prime}+\eta}},

where the implied positive constants may depend on
[V]_{\mathscr{A}_{p}}
.

Proof.

We prove both (
6.1
) and (
6.2
) at once, writing out the details for (
6.2
). For (
6.1
), it suffices to take
\varepsilon=\eta=0
and observe that some steps are then avoided.

The first inequality in (
6.2
) follows from the elementary calculation

(6.3)

\begin{split}\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,V)}(\mathfrak{e})&=\Big(\fint_{Q}\|V(\cdot)\mathfrak{e}\|^{p}\Big)^{\frac{1}{p}}\leq\Big(\fint_{Q}\|V(\cdot)\mathfrak{e}\|^{p+\varepsilon}\Big)^{\frac{1}{p+\varepsilon}}\\
&\leq\Big(\frac{|S|}{|Q|}\fint_{S}\|V(\cdot)\mathfrak{e}\|^{p+\varepsilon}\Big)^{\frac{1}{p+\varepsilon}}=\Big(\frac{|S|}{|Q|}\Big)^{\frac{1}{p+\varepsilon}}\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p+\varepsilon}(S,V)}(\mathfrak{e})\\
&\lesssim\Big(\frac{|S|}{|Q|}\Big)^{\frac{1}{p+\varepsilon}}\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(S,V)}(\mathfrak{e})\end{split}

by Proposition
5.1
in the last step; this step is redundant if
\varepsilon=0
.

Turning to the second inequality in (
6.2
), let first
p\in(0,1]
. Then Corollary
4.10
followed by trivial estimates shows that

\begin{split}\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(S,V)}(\mathfrak{e})&\leq[V]_{\mathscr{A}_{p}}\operatornamewithlimits{ess\,inf}_{y\in S}\|V(y)\mathfrak{e}\|\\
&\leq[V]_{\mathscr{A}_{p}}\operatornamewithlimits{ess\,inf}_{y\in Q}\|V(y)\mathfrak{e}\|\leq[V]_{\mathscr{A}_{p}}\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,V)}(\mathfrak{e}),\end{split}

which proves the second inequality in (
6.2
) with
p^{\prime}=p^{\prime}+\eta=\infty
.

Let then
p\in(1,\infty)
. Similarly to (
6.3
), but with
V^{-*}
and
p^{\prime}
in place of
V
and
p
, we obtain

\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(Q,V^{-*})}(\mathfrak{e}^{*})\lesssim\Big(\frac{|S|}{|Q|}\Big)^{\frac{1}{p^{\prime}+\eta}}\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(S,V^{-*})}(\mathfrak{e}^{*}),

where the implied positive constant is
1
if
\eta=0
.

Dualising, it follows that

(6.4)

\begin{split}\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(S,V^{-*})}^{*}(\mathfrak{e})&=\sup_{\mathfrak{e}^{*}\in\mathfrak{X}^{*}\setminus\{0\}}\frac{|\langle\mathfrak{e}^{*},\mathfrak{e}\rangle|}{\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(S,V^{-*})}(\mathfrak{e}^{*})}\\
&\lesssim\Big(\frac{|S|}{|Q|}\Big)^{\frac{1}{p^{\prime}+\eta}}\sup_{\mathfrak{e}^{*}\in\mathfrak{X}^{*}\setminus\{0\}}\frac{|\langle\mathfrak{e}^{*},\mathfrak{e}\rangle|}{\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(Q,V^{-*})}(\mathfrak{e}^{*})}\\
&=\Big(\frac{|S|}{|Q|}\Big)^{\frac{1}{p^{\prime}+\eta}}\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(Q,V^{-*})}^{*}(\mathfrak{e}).\end{split}

Hence, if
V\in\mathscr{A}_{p}
and
p\in(1,\infty)
, then

\begin{split}\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(S,V)}(\mathfrak{e})&\leq[V]_{A_{p}}\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(S,V^{-*})}^{*}(\mathfrak{e})\qquad\text{by Corollary \ref {cor:Ap}}\\
&\lesssim[V]_{A_{p}}\Big(\frac{|S|}{|Q|}\Big)^{\frac{1}{p^{\prime}+\eta}}\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(Q,V^{-*})}^{*}(\mathfrak{e})\qquad\text{by \eqref{eq:SvsRdual}}\\
&\leq[V]_{A_{p}}\Big(\frac{|S|}{|Q|}\Big)^{\frac{1}{p^{\prime}+\eta}}\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,V)}(\mathfrak{e})\qquad\text{by \eqref{eq:uvHolderDual}}.\end{split}

Recalling that we already handled the case
p\in(0,1]
, we have completed the proof of Lemma
6.1
.
∎

The upper bounds in Lemma
6.1
motivate:

Definition 6.2
.

For
p\in(0,\infty)
, we say that
V
is a
(p,\beta)
-doubling weight

if
V
is a
p
-weight and for all cubes
Q\subset S
there holds

\frac{\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(S,V)}(\mathfrak{e})}{\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,V)}(\mathfrak{e})}\lesssim\Big(\frac{\ell(S)}{\ell(Q)}\Big)^{\frac{\beta-n}{p}}.

Here
\beta
is called the
doubling dimension
of
V
.

In the language of Definition
6.2
, part of Lemma
6.1
may be stated as:

Corollary 6.3
.

Every
V\in\mathscr{A}_{p}
is
(p,\beta)
-doubling with
\beta=n\max(1,p)
. If
p>1
, then it is also
(p,\beta)
-doubling for some
\beta\in[n,np)
.

Remark 6.4
.

We have chosen the potentially strange-looking normalisation of the exponent for consistency with the notion of the doubling exponent introduced in
[
59
, Definition 1.5]
and used in several subsequent papers. Note that an equivalent condition is

(6.5)

\int_{S}\|V(y)\mathfrak{e}\|_{\mathfrak{X}}^{p}dy\lesssim\Big(\frac{\ell(S)}{\ell(Q)}\Big)^{\beta}\int_{Q}\|V(y)\mathfrak{e}\|_{\mathfrak{X}}^{p}dy,

showing the connection to the doubling property of the measures

\|V(y)\mathfrak{e}\|_{\mathfrak{X}}^{p}dy,

uniformly over
\mathfrak{e}\in\mathfrak{X}
.

Definition
6.2
still differs slightly from
[
59
, Definition 1.5]
as follows: Defining (as usual) the doubling constant
c
as the best constant in the inequality

\int_{2B}\|V(y)\mathfrak{e}\|_{\mathfrak{X}}^{p}dy\leq c\int_{B}\|V(y)\mathfrak{e}\|_{\mathfrak{X}}^{p}dy,

over all balls
B
and
\mathfrak{e}\in\mathfrak{X}
,
[
59
, Definition 1.5]

defines
the doubling exponent as
\hat{\beta}:=\log_{2}c
. It is elementary to see that this specific value gives an
upper bound
for our
\beta
, i.e.,
\beta\leq\hat{\beta}
. However, in general the inequality can be strict.

To see this, it suffices to take a scalar weight
V=v
such that, for every
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
, we have
v|_{[0,1)^{n}+k}\equiv 1
or
N
if
\sum_{i=1}^{n}k_{i}
is even or odd, respectively. Then it is easy to see that
\int_{S}v^{p}\lesssim N^{p}\cdot\frac{|S|}{|Q|}\int_{Q}v^{p}
, so that only the implied positive constant, but not the exponent in (
6.5
) is affected by
N
. On the other hand, the doubling constant satisfies
c\approx N^{p}
. Hence
\beta=n
while
\hat{\beta}\approx p\log N
can be arbitrarily large.

On the other hand, it is clear that it is the rate of growth described by the exponent
\beta
in (
6.5
) that matters, not the multiplicative constant in this inequality.

Remark 6.5
.

The constant
\beta
in Definition
6.2
satisfies
\beta\in[n,\infty)
;
see, for instance,
[
10
, Remark 2.20]
.

Lemma 6.6
.

Let
V
be a
(p,\beta)
-doubling weight.
Then for all
\mathfrak{e}\in\mathfrak{X}
and all cubes
Q,R\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}

we have

\frac{\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,V)}(\mathfrak{e})}{\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(R,V)}(\mathfrak{e})}\lesssim B_{\frac{n}{p},\frac{\beta-n}{p},\frac{\beta}{p}}(Q,R),

where

(6.6)

B_{a,b,c}(Q,R):=\Big(1+\frac{\ell(R)}{\ell(Q)}\Big)^{a}\Big(1+\frac{\ell(Q)}{\ell(R)}\Big)^{b}\Big(1+\frac{|x_{Q}-x_{R}|}{\max\{\ell(Q),\ell(R)\}}\Big)^{c}.
