In particular, for all cubes
Q,R
of equal size,

\frac{\rho_{L^{p}(Q,V)}(\mathfrak{e})}{\rho_{L^{p}(R,V)}(\mathfrak{e})}\lesssim\Big(1+\frac{|x_{Q}-x_{R}|}{\ell(Q)}\Big)^{\frac{\beta}{p}}.

Proof.

Let
S
be the minimal cube that contains both
Q
and
R
. Then

(6.7)

\begin{split}\frac{\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,V)}(\mathfrak{e})}{\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(R,V)}(\mathfrak{e})}=\frac{\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,V)}(\mathfrak{e})}{\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(S,V)}(\mathfrak{e})}\frac{\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(S,V)}(\mathfrak{e})}{\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(R,V)}(\mathfrak{e})}\lesssim\Big(\frac{\ell(S)}{\ell(Q)}\Big)^{\frac{n}{p}}\Big(\frac{\ell(S)}{\ell(R)}\Big)^{\frac{\beta-n}{p}}\end{split}

by the first estimate of Lemma
6.1
(which does not need
\mathscr{A}_{p}
) for the first factor and Definition
6.2
for the second.

Then we observe that

\Big(\frac{\ell(S)}{\ell(Q)}\Big)^{\frac{n}{p}}\Big(\frac{\ell(S)}{\ell(R)}\Big)^{\frac{\beta-n}{p}}\approx\Big(1+\frac{\ell(R)}{\ell(Q)}\Big)^{\frac{n}{p}}\Big(1+\frac{\ell(Q)}{\ell(R)}\Big)^{\frac{\beta-n}{p}}\Big(\frac{\ell(S)}{\ell(Q)\vee\ell(R)}\Big)^{\frac{\beta}{p}},

and

\frac{\ell(S)}{\ell(Q)\vee\ell(R)}\approx 1+\frac{|x_{Q}-x_{R}|}{\ell(Q)\vee\ell(R)}.

This finishes the proof of Lemma
6.6
.
∎

Lemma 6.7
.

Let
V\in\mathscr{A}_{p}
, and let
\varepsilon,\eta\geq 0
be numbers for which
V
satisfies the conclusions of Proposition
5.1
.
Then for all
\mathfrak{e}\in\mathfrak{X}
and all cubes
Q,R\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
we have

(6.8)

\frac{\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,V)}(\mathfrak{e})}{\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(R,V)}(\mathfrak{e})}\lesssim B_{a,b,c}(Q,R),

where
B_{a,b,c}
is defined in (
6.6
) and

(6.9)

(a,b,c)=(\frac{n}{p+\varepsilon},\frac{n}{p^{\prime}+\eta},\frac{n}{p+\varepsilon}+\frac{n}{p^{\prime}+\eta}).

Proof.

The proof is like that of Lemma
6.6
, but replacing the initial estimate (
6.7
) by

\frac{\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,V)}(\mathfrak{e})}{\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(S,V)}(\mathfrak{e})}\frac{\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(S,V)}(\mathfrak{e})}{\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(R,V)}(\mathfrak{e})}\lesssim\Big(\frac{\ell(S)}{\ell(Q)}\Big)^{\frac{n}{p+\varepsilon}}\Big(\frac{\ell(S)}{\ell(R)}\Big)^{\frac{n}{p^{\prime}+\eta}},

where the estimates of both factors are those in Lemma
6.1
under the assumption that
V\in\mathscr{A}_{p}
.
∎

Remark 6.8
.

For many purposes, any growth of the type (
6.8
) is enough.
Lemma
6.7
shows that, for
V\in\mathscr{A}_{p}
, we can always take some
a<\frac{n}{p}
and
c<\frac{n}{\min(1,p)}
. Moreover,
b<\frac{n}{p^{\prime}}
, unless
p^{\prime}=\infty
, in which case we have the equality
b=\frac{n}{p^{\prime}}=0
. We refer the reader to
[
10
]
for several examples of weights, showing that, except for the restrictions just indicated, all possible growth profiles can appear already for scalar and matrix valued weights.

Corollary 6.9
.

Let
V\in\mathscr{A}_{p}
, and let
\varepsilon,\eta\geq 0
be numbers for which
V
satisfies the conclusions of Proposition
5.1
.
Then for all
\mathfrak{e}^{*}\in\mathfrak{X}^{*}
and all cubes
Q,R\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
with minimal containing cube
S
, we have

\frac{\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(Q,V^{-*})}(\mathfrak{e}^{*})}{\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(R,V^{-*})}(\mathfrak{e}^{*})}\lesssim\Big(\frac{|S|}{|Q|}\Big)^{\frac{1}{p^{\prime}+\eta}}\Big(\frac{|S|}{|R|}\Big)^{\frac{1}{p+\varepsilon}}\approx B_{b,a,c}(Q,R),

where the last expression is defined as in (
6.6
), with
a
,
b
, and
c
as in (
6.9
) (but note the swap of two parameters in the bound above compared to Lemma
6.7
). In particular, for all cubes
Q,R
of equal size,

\frac{\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(Q,V^{-*})}(\mathfrak{e}^{*})}{\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(R,V^{-*})}(\mathfrak{e}^{*})}\lesssim\Big(1+\frac{|x_{Q}-x_{R}|}{\ell(Q)}\Big)^{c}\leq\Big(1+\frac{|x_{Q}-x_{R}|}{\ell(Q)}\Big)^{n}.

Here all the implicit positive constants are independent of
Q
,
R
, and
\mathfrak{e}^{*}
.

Proof.

The proof is entirely analogous to that of Lemma
6.7
, replacing each step by its analogue on the dual side.
∎

Let us also record the simple:

Lemma 6.10
.

Let
\mathfrak{X}
be a Banach space, and let
\rho_{Q}
be quasi-norms indexed by cubes
Q\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
that satisfy

\frac{\rho_{Q}(\mathfrak{e})}{\rho_{R}(\mathfrak{e})}\lesssim B_{a,b,c}(Q,R)\qquad\forall\ \mathfrak{e}\in\mathfrak{X}.

Then the dual norms
\rho_{Q}^{*}
on
\mathfrak{X}^{*}
satisfy

\frac{\rho_{Q}^{*}(\mathfrak{e}^{*})}{\rho_{R}^{*}(\mathfrak{e}^{*})}\lesssim B_{b,a,c}(Q,R)\qquad\forall\ \mathfrak{e}^{*}\in\mathfrak{X}^{*}.

Proof.

By assumption, we have

\frac{1}{\rho_{R}(\mathfrak{e})}\lesssim\frac{1}{\rho_{Q}(\mathfrak{e})}B_{a,b,c}(Q,R).

By the definition of the dual norm, it follows that

\begin{split}\rho_{R}^{*}(\mathfrak{e}^{*})&=\sup_{\mathfrak{e}\in\mathfrak{X}\setminus\{0\}}\frac{|\langle\mathfrak{e}^{*},\mathfrak{e}\rangle|}{\rho_{R}(\mathfrak{e})}\\
&\lesssim\sup_{\mathfrak{e}\in\mathfrak{X}\setminus\{0\}}\frac{|\langle\mathfrak{e}^{*},\mathfrak{e}\rangle|}{\rho_{Q}(\mathfrak{e})}B_{a,b,c}(Q,R)=\rho_{Q}^{*}(\mathfrak{e}^{*})B_{a,b,c}(Q,R).\end{split}

The claim follows by swapping the names of
Q
and
R
and noting that
B_{a,b,c}(R,Q)=B_{b,a,c}(Q,R)
by definition (
6.6
).
∎

7.
Stability of weights under some operations

In this section, we record two lemmas of relatively elementary nature that will be helpful in streamlining some considerations further below. Since these results have a somewhat technical nature and we only need them for
p\in(1,\infty)
, we restrict our considerations to this range for brevity, leaving the extension to
p\in(0,1]
for the interested reader.

Lemma 7.1
.

Let
p\in(1,\infty)
, let
V\in\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{Y})
, and let
\iota:\mathfrak{X}\to\mathfrak{Y}
be an isomorphism. Then
\iota^{-1}V\iota\in\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})
and

[\iota^{-1}V\iota]_{\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})}\leq\|\iota^{-1}\|\|\iota\|[V]_{\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{Y})}.

Proof.

Noting that
(\iota^{-1})^{*}=(\iota^{*})^{-1}=:\iota^{-*}
, we see that
\iota^{*}:\mathfrak{Y}^{*}\to\mathfrak{X}^{*}
is also an isomorphism. For
\mathfrak{e}^{*}\in\mathfrak{X}^{*}
, we have

\begin{split}\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(Q,(\iota^{-1}V\iota)^{-*})}(\mathfrak{e}^{*})&=\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(Q,\iota^{*}V^{-*}\iota^{-*})}(\mathfrak{e}^{*})\leq\|\iota^{*}\|\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(Q,V^{-*})}(\iota^{-*}\mathfrak{e}^{*})\leq\|\iota\|[V]_{\mathscr{A}_{p}}\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,V)}^{*}(\iota^{-*}\mathfrak{e}^{*}).\end{split}

For
\mathfrak{u}\in\mathfrak{Y}
,

\begin{split}|\langle\iota^{-*}\mathfrak{e}^{*},\mathfrak{u}\rangle|&=|\langle\mathfrak{e}^{*},\iota^{-1}\mathfrak{u}\rangle|\\
&\leq\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,\iota^{-1}V\iota)}^{*}(\mathfrak{e}^{*})\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,\iota^{-1}V\iota)}(\iota^{-1}\mathfrak{u})\\
&\leq\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,\iota^{-1}V\iota)}^{*}(\mathfrak{e}^{*})\|\iota^{-1}\|\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,V)}(\mathfrak{u}).\end{split}

Hence

\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,V)}^{*}(\iota^{-*}\mathfrak{e}^{*})\leq\|\iota^{-1}\|\rho^{*}_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,\iota^{-1}V\iota)}(\mathfrak{e}^{*}),

and thus

\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(Q,(\iota^{-1}V\iota)^{-*})}(\mathfrak{e}^{*})\leq\|\iota\|[V]_{\mathscr{A}_{p}}\|\iota^{-1}\|\rho^{*}_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,\iota^{-1}V\iota)}(\mathfrak{e}^{*}),

which is equivalent to the claim of the lemma.
∎

Lemma 7.2
.

Let
p\in(1,\infty)
, let
\mathfrak{X}=\mathfrak{X}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{X}_{2}
and
V\in L^{p}_{\mathrm{loc},\operatorname{so}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})
with

V^{-*}\in L^{p^{\prime}}_{\mathrm{loc},\operatorname{so}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X}^{*})

be a weight of the form
V=V_{1}\oplus V_{2}
with respect to the above splitting of
\mathfrak{X}
. Then

[V]_{\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})}\approx\sum_{i=1}^{2}[V_{i}]_{\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X}_{i})}

with the positive equivalence constants independent of
V
,
V_{1}
, and
V_{2}
.

Proof.

We also have
\mathfrak{X}^{*}=\mathfrak{X}_{1}^{*}\oplus\mathfrak{X}_{2}^{*}
and
V^{-*}=V_{1}^{-*}\oplus V_{2}^{-*}
. Thus

\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(Q,V^{-*})}(\mathfrak{e}^{*})\approx\sum_{i=1}^{2}\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(Q,V_{i}^{-*})}(\mathfrak{e}_{i}^{*})

and

\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,V)}(\mathfrak{e})\approx\sum_{i=1}^{2}\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,V_{i})}(\mathfrak{e}_{i}).

For the dual norm, we argue that

(7.1)

\begin{split}\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,V)}^{*}(\mathfrak{e}^{*})&\approx\sup_{\mathfrak{e}=(\mathfrak{e}_{1},\mathfrak{e}_{2})\in\mathfrak{X}\setminus\{0\}}\frac{|\sum_{i=1}^{2}\langle\mathfrak{e}_{i},\mathfrak{e}_{i}^{*}\rangle|}{\sum_{i=1}^{2}\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,V_{i})}(\mathfrak{e}_{i})}\\
&\approx\sum_{i=1}^{2}\sup_{\mathfrak{e}_{i}\in\mathfrak{X}_{i}\setminus\{0\}}\frac{|\langle\mathfrak{e}_{i},\mathfrak{e}_{i}^{*}\rangle|}{\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,V_{i})}(\mathfrak{e}_{i})}=\sum_{i=1}^{2}\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,V_{i})}^{*}(\mathfrak{e}_{i});\end{split}

indeed, in the last “
\approx
”, the “
\gtrsim
” follows by taking either one of
\mathfrak{e}_{i}=0
, while “
\leq
” is the general bound, for arbitrary positive functions
\lambda_{i},\mu_{i}
,

\sum_{i=1}^{2}\lambda_{i}(\mathfrak{e}_{i})\leq\sum_{i=1}^{2}C_{i}\mu_{i}(\mathfrak{e}_{i})\leq(C_{1}+C_{2})\sum_{i=1}^{2}\mu_{i}(\mathfrak{e}_{i}),\quad C_{i}:=\sup\frac{\lambda_{i}(\mathfrak{e}_{i})}{\mu_{i}(\mathfrak{e}_{i})},

specialised to
\lambda_{i}=\mathfrak{e}_{i}^{*}
and
\mu_{i}=\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,V_{i})}
.
Thus

\begin{split}\sup_{\mathfrak{e}^{*}\in\mathfrak{X}^{*}\setminus\{0\}}\frac{\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(Q,V^{-*})}(\mathfrak{e}^{*})}{\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,V)}^{*}(\mathfrak{e}^{*})}&\approx\sup_{(\mathfrak{e}_{1}^{*},\mathfrak{e}_{2}^{*})\in\mathfrak{X}^{*}\setminus\{0\}}\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{2}\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(Q,V_{i}^{-*})}(\mathfrak{e}_{i}^{*})}{\sum_{i=1}^{2}\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,V_{i})}^{*}(\mathfrak{e}_{i}^{*})}\\
&\approx\sum_{i=1}^{2}\sup_{\mathfrak{e}_{i}^{*}\in\mathfrak{X}_{i}^{*}\setminus\{0\}}\frac{\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(Q,V_{i}^{-*})}(\mathfrak{e}_{i}^{*})}{\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,V_{i})}^{*}(\mathfrak{e}_{i}^{*})},\end{split}

where the last “
\approx
” follows in the same way as the last “
\approx
” in (
7.1
). The lemma follows by taking the supremum over all cubes
Q
.
∎

Part II
Operator-weighted
L^{p}(V)
spaces

8.
Averaging and sparse operators

The following result is an analogue of
[
55
, Lemma 2.1]
and
[
1
, Lemma 2.2]
. The former deals with
p\in(1,\infty)
and
\mathfrak{X}=\mathbb{C}^{d}
, the latter with
p=2
and a general Hilbert space
\mathfrak{X}
. The former, like our present study, is also written in the formalism of the norms
\rho
, while the latter makes use of the explicit form of the reducing operators available for
p=2
. Nevertheless, our argument below, passing through certain quotient norms, is more closely modelled after the one in
[
1
]
.

Proposition 8.1
.

Let
p\in[1,\infty)
and
\mathfrak{X}
be a Banach space.
Let
V
be an
\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X})
-valued weight, and let
a,b\in L^{0}
be scalar-valued functions such that
aV\in L^{p}_{\operatorname{so}}(\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X}))
and
bV^{-*}\in L^{p^{\prime}}_{\operatorname{so}}(\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X}^{*}))
. Let

N:=\sup_{\mathfrak{e}\in\mathfrak{X}\setminus\{0\}}\frac{\rho_{L^{p}(aV)}(\mathfrak{e})}{\rho_{L^{p^{\prime}}(bV^{-*})}^{*}(\mathfrak{e})},

and consider the operator

(8.1)

Pf(x):=a(x)\int b(y)f(y)dy,

initially defined on

L^{p}_{b}(V;\mathfrak{X}):=\{f\in L^{p}(V;\mathfrak{X}):bf\in L^{1}(\mathfrak{X})\}.

Then

(i)

L^{p}_{b}(V;\mathfrak{X})\subset L^{p}(V;\mathfrak{X})
is dense.

(ii)

If
N<\infty
, then
P
extends to a bounded linear operator on
L^{p}(V;\mathfrak{X})
with

\|P\|_{L^{p}(V;\mathfrak{X})\to L^{p}(V;\mathfrak{X})}\leq N.

(iii)

If
\int ab=1
and
P
extends to a bounded linear operator on
L^{p}(V;\mathfrak{X})
, then

N=\|P\|_{L^{p}(V;\mathfrak{X})\to L^{p}(V;\mathfrak{X})}.

In particular,
if
V
is a
p
-weight and
V^{-*}
is a
p^{\prime}
-weight, then

(8.2)

\|f\mapsto\mathbf{1}_{Q}\langle f\rangle_{Q}\|_{L^{p}(V;\mathfrak{X})\to L^{p}(V;\mathfrak{X})}=\sup_{\mathfrak{e}\in\mathfrak{X}\setminus\{0\}}\frac{\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,V)}(\mathfrak{e})}{\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(Q,V^{-*})}^{*}(\mathfrak{e})}.

Proof.

The last claim (
8.2
) is recognised as the special case
a=b=|Q|^{-\frac{1}{2}}\mathbf{1}_{Q}
, so it remains to prove the other claims.

(
i
): Given
f\in L^{p}(V;\mathfrak{X})
, we have
bf\in L^{0}(\mathfrak{X})
. For
k\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\}
, let

f_{k}:=\mathbf{1}_{B(0,k)}\mathbf{1}_{\{\|bf\|_{\mathfrak{X}}\leq k\}}f.

Then
bf_{k}
is measurable and
\|bf\|_{\mathfrak{X}}\leq k\mathbf{1}_{B(0,k)}
, hence
bf_{k}\in L^{1}(\mathfrak{X})
. Since
f_{k}\to f
pointwise and
\|Vf_{k}\|_{\mathfrak{X}}\leq\|Vf\|_{\mathfrak{X}}
, we have
f_{k}\to f
in
L^{p}(V;\mathfrak{X})
by dominated convergence. This proves the density claimed in (
i
).

(
ii
): By (
i
), it is enough to prove an estimate for
f\in L^{p}_{b}(V;\mathfrak{X})
, where
Pf
is given by the explicit formula (
8.1
). Let
e_{f}:=\int bf\in\mathfrak{X}
. Then

\begin{split}\|Pf\|_{L^{p}(V;\mathfrak{X})}&=\|aV\mathfrak{e}_{f}\|_{L^{p}(\mathfrak{X})}=\rho_{L^{p}(aV)}(\mathfrak{e}_{f})\leq N\rho_{L^{p^{\prime}}(bV^{-*})}^{*}(\mathfrak{e}_{f})\\
&=N\sup_{\mathfrak{e}^{*}\in\mathfrak{X}^{*}\setminus\{0\}}\frac{|\langle\mathfrak{e}_{f},\mathfrak{e}^{*}\rangle|}{\rho_{L^{p^{\prime}}(bV^{-*})}(\mathfrak{e}^{*})},\end{split}

where

\begin{split}|\langle\mathfrak{e}_{f},\mathfrak{e}^{*}\rangle|&=\Big|\Big\langle\int bf,\mathfrak{e}^{*}\Big\rangle\Big|=\Big|\int\langle Vf,bV^{-*}\mathfrak{e}^{*}\rangle\Big|\\
&\leq\|Vf\|_{L^{p}(\mathfrak{X})}\|bV^{-*}\mathfrak{e}^{*}\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}(\mathfrak{X}^{*})}=\|f\|_{L^{p}(V;\mathfrak{X})}\cdot\rho_{L^{p^{\prime}}(bV^{-*})}(\mathfrak{e}^{*}).\end{split}

Substituting back, we obtain
\|Pf\|_{L^{p}(V;\mathfrak{X})}\leq N\|f\|_{L^{p}(V;\mathfrak{X})}
, and thus (
ii
).

(
iii
):
From (
8.1
), it is clear that
P
maps
L^{p}_{b}(V;\mathfrak{X})
into

\{a\mathfrak{e}:\mathfrak{e}\in\mathfrak{X}\}\subseteq L^{p}(V;\mathfrak{X}),

where the inclusion follows from the assumption that
aV\in L^{p}_{\operatorname{so}}(\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X}))
.
We check that this is a closed subspace: Indeed, if
f_{n}=a\mathfrak{e}_{n}\to f
in
L^{p}(V;\mathfrak{X})
, then
Vf_{n}=aV\mathfrak{e}_{n}\to Vf
in
L^{p}(\mathfrak{X})
; hence a subsequence converges almost everywhere. Since
V^{-1}
exists almost everywhere, it follows that
a\mathfrak{e}_{n}\to f
almost everywhere. Thus, a.e. on
\{a=0\}
, we have
f=0
, while
\mathfrak{e}_{n}\to a^{-1}f
a.e. on
\{a\neq 0\}
. Since
\mathfrak{e}_{n}
are constant functions, their a.e. limit must also be a constant, say
\mathfrak{e}
. Hence
a^{-1}f=\mathfrak{e}
a.e. on
a\neq 0
and
f=0
a.e. on
\{a=0\}
. These can be combined to the statement that
f=a\mathfrak{e}
almost everywhere, which confirms the closedness of
\{a\mathfrak{e}:\mathfrak{e}\in\mathfrak{X}\}
in
L^{p}(V;\mathfrak{X})
.

If
P:L^{p}_{b}(V;\mathfrak{X})\to\{a\mathfrak{e}:\mathfrak{e}\in\mathfrak{X}\}
extends to a bounded linear operator on
L^{p}(V;\mathfrak{X})
, the density proved in (
i
) shows that
P:L^{p}(V;\mathfrak{X})\to\{a\mathfrak{e}:\mathfrak{e}\in\mathfrak{X}\}
. Hence, for every
f\in L^{p}(V;\mathfrak{X})
, there is
\mathfrak{e}_{f}\in\mathfrak{X}
such that
Pf=a\mathfrak{e}_{f}
. Under the assumption that
\int ab=1
, it follows in particular that
a
cannot vanish almost everywhere, and hence such
\mathfrak{e}_{f}
is unique.

We then observe that

(8.3)

\begin{split}\|Pf\|_{L^{p}(V;\mathfrak{X})\to L^{p}(V;\mathfrak{X})}&=\sup_{f\in L^{p}(V;\mathfrak{X})\setminus\{0\}}\frac{\|aV\mathfrak{e}_{f}\|_{L^{p}(\mathfrak{X})}}{\|f\|_{L^{p}(V;\mathfrak{X})}}\\
&=\sup_{\mathfrak{e}\in\mathfrak{X}\setminus\{0\}}\sup_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{f\in L^p(V;\fX)}{\fx_f=\fx}}\frac{\rho_{L^{p}(aV)}(\mathfrak{e})}{\|f\|_{L^{p}(V;\mathfrak{X})}}.\end{split}

Given any
f\in L^{p}(V;\mathfrak{X})
with
\mathfrak{e}_{f}=\mathfrak{e}
, we consider
f^{\prime}:=f-a\mathfrak{e}
. For the second term, we have
b(a\mathfrak{e})\in L^{1}(\mathfrak{X})
since
\int ab=1
, and
V(a\mathfrak{e})\in L^{p}(\mathfrak{X})
since
aV\in L^{p}_{\operatorname{so}}(\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X}))
. Thus
a\mathfrak{e}\in L^{p}_{b}(V;\mathfrak{X})
, and
P(a\mathfrak{e})
can be computed by the explicit formula (
8.1
). It follows from the assumption
\int ab=1
and
\mathfrak{e}_{f}=\mathfrak{e}
that

P(a\mathfrak{e})=a\int ba\mathfrak{e}=a\mathfrak{e},\qquad Pf^{\prime}=Pf-P(a\mathfrak{e})=a\mathfrak{e}_{f}-a\mathfrak{e}=0.

Hence an arbitrary
f\in L^{p}(V;\mathfrak{X})
with
\mathfrak{e}_{f}=fx
can be written as
f=a\mathfrak{e}+f^{\prime}
, where
f^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Ker}P
. Thus the inner supremum on the right of (
8.3
) can be written as

(8.4)

\begin{split}\sup_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{f\in L^p(V;\fX)}{\fx_f=\fx}}\big(\|f\|_{L^{p}(V;\mathfrak{X})}\big)^{-1}&=\Big(\inf_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{f'\in L^p(V;\fX)}{P f'=0}}\|a\mathfrak{e}+f^{\prime}\|_{L^{p}(V;\mathfrak{X})}\Big)^{-1}\\
&=\big(\|a\mathfrak{e}\|_{L^{p}(V;\mathfrak{X})/\operatorname{Ker}P}\big)^{-1}.\end{split}

Moreover,

(8.5)

\|a\mathfrak{e}\|_{L^{p}(V;\mathfrak{X})/\operatorname{Ker}P}=\sup\Big\{|\Lambda(a\mathfrak{e})|:\|\Lambda\|_{(L^{p}(V;\mathfrak{X})/\operatorname{Ker}P)^{*}}\leq 1\Big\},

where, by general functional analysis (see e.g.
[
39
, Proposition B.1.4]
), the dual of the quotient is the annihilator

\begin{split}(L^{p}(V;\mathfrak{X})/\operatorname{Ker}P)^{*}=&\,(\operatorname{Ker}P)^{\perp}\\
:=&\,\{\Lambda\in(L^{p}(V;\mathfrak{X}))^{*}:\Lambda(f)=0\ \forall\ f\in\operatorname{Ker}P\}.\end{split}

We claim that

(8.6)

(\operatorname{Ker}P)^{\perp}=\{b\mathfrak{e}^{*}:\mathfrak{e}^{*}\in\mathfrak{X}^{*}\}\subset L^{p^{\prime}}(V^{-*};\mathfrak{X}^{*}),

where
L^{p^{\prime}}(V^{-*};\mathfrak{X}^{*})
is isometrically identified with a subspace of the dual
(L^{p}(V;\mathfrak{X}))^{*}
with respect to the duality

\langle f,g\rangle=\int\langle f(y),g(y)\rangle dy=\int\langle V(y)f(y),V(y)^{-*}g(y)\rangle dy.

Assuming (
8.6
) for the moment, we can continue from (
8.5
) with

\begin{split}\|a\mathfrak{e}\|_{L^{p}(V;\mathfrak{X})/\operatorname{Ker}P}&=\sup\Big\{\Big|\int\langle a\mathfrak{e},b\mathfrak{e}^{*}\rangle\Big|:\|b\mathfrak{e}^{*}\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}(V^{-*};\mathfrak{X}^{*})}\leq 1\Big\}\\
&=\sup\Big\{|\langle\mathfrak{e},\mathfrak{e}^{*}\rangle|:\rho_{L^{p^{\prime}}(bV^{-*})}(\mathfrak{e}^{*})\leq 1\Big\}=\rho_{L^{p^{\prime}}(bV^{-*})}^{*}(\mathfrak{e}).\end{split}

Substituting this into (
8.4
), and then (
8.4
) into (
8.3
), we obtain the identity claimed in (
iii
).

It only remains to verify claim (
8.6
).
We first observe that, for each
\Lambda\in(L^{p}(V;\mathfrak{X}))^{*}
, we can define
\mathfrak{e}^{*}_{\Lambda}\in\mathfrak{X}^{*}
by setting
\langle\mathfrak{e},\mathfrak{e}_{\Lambda}^{*}\rangle:=\Lambda(a\mathfrak{e})

for
\mathfrak{e}\in\mathfrak{X}
, and hence

\|\mathfrak{e}_{\Lambda}^{*}\|_{\mathfrak{X}^{*}}\leq\sup_{\|\mathfrak{e}\|_{\mathfrak{X}}\leq 1}\|\Lambda\|\|a\mathfrak{e}\|_{L^{p}(V;\mathfrak{X})}=\|\Lambda\|\|aV\|_{L^{p}_{\operatorname{so}}(\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X}))}.

For arbitrary
f\in L^{p}(V;\mathfrak{X})
we already checked that
f^{\prime}:=f-a\mathfrak{e}_{f}\in\operatorname{Ker}P
. Hence every
\Lambda\in(\operatorname{Ker}P)^{\perp}\subset(L^{p}(V;\mathfrak{X}))^{*}
satisfies

0=\Lambda(f^{\prime})=\Lambda(f)-\Lambda(a\mathfrak{e}_{f}).

In particular, for
f\in L^{p}_{b}(V;\mathfrak{X})
(for which
\mathfrak{e}_{f}=\int bf
), it follows that

\Lambda(f)=\Lambda(a\mathfrak{e}_{f})=\langle\mathfrak{e}_{f},\mathfrak{e}_{\Lambda}^{*}\rangle=\Big\langle\int bf,\mathfrak{e}_{\Lambda}^{*}\Big\rangle=\int\langle f,b\mathfrak{e}_{\Lambda}^{*}\rangle.

Note that both left and right-hand sides of the previous identity define continuous linear functionals on
f\in L^{p}(V;\mathfrak{X})
. Since they agree on the dense (by (
i
)) subspace
L^{p}_{b}(V;\mathfrak{X})
, they must agree on all
L^{p}(V;\mathfrak{X})
. This proves “
\subseteq
” in place of “
=
” in (
8.6
).

To check “
\supseteq
”, let
\mathfrak{e}^{*}\in\mathfrak{X}^{*}
and
f\in\operatorname{Ker}P\subseteq L^{p}(V;\mathfrak{X})
. Thus, there is a sequence
f_{n}\in L^{p}_{b}(V;\mathfrak{X})
with
f\to f_{n}
in
L^{p}(V;\mathfrak{X})
and
Pf_{n}\to Pf=0
in
L^{p}(V;\mathfrak{X})
, hence, for a subsequence, also almost everywhere. Since
Pf_{n}=a\mathfrak{e}_{f_{n}}
and
a
does not vanish a.e., it follows that
\mathfrak{e}_{f_{n}}\to 0
in
\mathfrak{X}
, where
\mathfrak{e}_{f_{n}}=\int bf_{n}
. Thus also
\int\langle bf_{n},\mathfrak{e}^{*}\rangle=\int\langle f_{n},b\mathfrak{e}^{*}\rangle\to 0
. But
b\mathfrak{e}^{*}\in L^{p^{\prime}}(V^{-*};\mathfrak{X}^{*})
and
f_{n}\to f
in
L^{p}(V;\mathfrak{X})
, hence
\int\langle f,b\mathfrak{e}^{*}\rangle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\int\langle f_{n},b\mathfrak{e}^{*}\rangle=0
. This confirms “
\subseteq
” in place of “
=
” in (
8.6
).

Thus, we have completed the verification of (
8.6
), and thereby of the proposition.
∎

Remark 8.2
.

A curious detail of the proof above is that we manage to identify
(\operatorname{Ker}P)^{\perp}
,
a priori
a subspace of
functionals

(L^{p}(V;\mathfrak{X}))^{*}
, with an explicit subspace of
functions

L^{p^{\prime}}(V^{-*},\mathfrak{X}^{*})
, without the need to know whether the full spaces
(L^{p}(V;\mathfrak{X}))^{*}
and
L^{p^{\prime}}(V^{-*},\mathfrak{X}^{*})
coincide. Indeed, in the generality of arbitrary Banach spaces that we consider, this coincidence in general fails, as it would require the so-called Radon–Nikodým property of
\mathfrak{X}^{*}
(see e.g.
[
39
, Section 1.3.b]
).

Corollary 8.3
.

Let
\mathfrak{X}
be a Banach space,
p\in(1,\infty)
, and
V
be an
\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X})
-valued
p
-weight such that
V^{-*}
is a
p^{\prime}
-weight. Then the averaging operators
f\mapsto\mathbf{1}_{Q}\langle f\rangle_{Q}
are bounded on
L^{p}(V;\mathfrak{X})
, uniformly over all cubes
Q\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
, if and only if
V\in\mathscr{A}_{p}
.

Proof.

By (
8.2
) of Proposition
8.1
, we find that

\|f\mapsto\mathbf{1}_{Q}\langle f\rangle_{Q}\|_{L^{p}(V;\mathfrak{X})\to L^{p}(V;\mathfrak{X})}\leq C

for all cubes
Q\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
if and only if

\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,V)}(\mathfrak{e})\leq C\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(Q,V^{-*})}^{*}(\mathfrak{e})

for all cubes
Q\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
and all
\mathfrak{e}\in\mathfrak{X}
. By Corollary
4.10
, this latter condition is equivalent to
V\in\mathscr{A}_{p}
.
∎

As a further corollary, we obtain the boundedness of a class of sparse operators, previously considered by
[
49
]
in the context of
\mathscr{A}_{2}
-weighted
L^{2}
spaces of Hilbert space -valued functions, in the more general setting of
\mathscr{A}_{p}
-weighted
L^{p}
spaces of Banach space-valued functions. We should note, however, that our Corollary
8.4
below is not strictly an extension of
[
49
, Theorem 2.1]
, since these authors obtain a somewhat sharper control, requiring the
\mathscr{A}_{2}
property only for the cubes that appear in the sparse collection. It might seem at first that this is the case in our argument as well, but the fact that we apply the reverse Hölder inequality (Proposition
5.1
) makes implicit use of the
\mathscr{A}_{p}
condition for the subcubes of these cubes as well.

Corollary 8.4
.

Let
p\in(1,\infty)
, let
V\in\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X}))
, let
\mathscr{S}\subset\mathscr{D}
be a sparse collection of dyadic cubes of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and for each
Q\in\mathscr{S}
, let
a_{Q}
and
b_{Q}
be scalar functions supported in
Q
and bounded in absolute value by
1
. Then

Tf:=\sum_{Q\in\mathscr{S}}a_{Q}\fint_{Q}b_{Q}f

defines a bounded operator on
L^{p}(V;\mathfrak{X})
.

Proof.

Let us begin by observing that each individual term in the series of
Tf
defines a bounded operator on
L^{p}(V)
by Proposition
8.1
. Hence, so does any finite sum of these terms. Our plan is to first prove that such finite sums satisfy uniform bounds that are independent of the number of terms, and then use this to deduce the convergence of the infinite series to a bounded operator.

A priori bounds for finite sums

Let first
\mathscr{S}
be finite, so that
Tf\in L^{p}(V)
is well defined. We estimate its norm by dualising with
g\in L^{p^{\prime}}(V^{-*})
; cf.
[
39
, Proposition 1.3.1.]
. Hence, we are led to study

\langle Tf,g\rangle=\sum_{Q\in\mathscr{S}}|Q|\Big\langle\fint_{Q}b_{Q}f,\fint_{Q}a_{Q}g\Big\rangle,

and we begin with the estimate

(8.7)

|\langle Tf,g\rangle|\leq\sum_{Q\in\mathscr{S}}|Q|\rho_{\textit{\L}^{v}(Q,V)}\Big(\fint_{Q}b_{Q}f\Big)\rho_{\textit{\L}^{v}(Q,V)}^{*}\Big(\fint_{Q}a_{Q}g\Big).

The last term is

\rho_{\textit{\L}^{v}(Q,V)}^{*}\Big(\fint_{Q}a_{Q}g\Big)=\sup_{\mathfrak{e}\in\mathfrak{X}\setminus\{0\}}\frac{1}{\rho_{\textit{\L}^{v}(Q,V)}(\mathfrak{e})}\Big|\Big\langle\mathfrak{e},\fint_{Q}a_{Q}g\Big\rangle\Big|,

where

\Big|\Big\langle\mathfrak{e},\fint_{Q}a_{Q}g\Big\rangle\Big|=\Big|\fint_{Q}a_{Q}\langle V\mathfrak{e},V^{-*}g\rangle\Big|\leq\|V\mathfrak{e}\|_{\textit{\L}^{v}(Q)}\|V^{-*}g\|_{\textit{\L}^{v^{\prime}}(Q)},

and hence

(8.8)

\rho_{\textit{\L}^{v}(Q,V)}^{*}\Big(\fint_{Q}a_{Q}g\Big)\leq\|V^{-*}g\|_{\textit{\L}^{v^{\prime}}(Q)}.

Let now
v=p+\varepsilon
. By Proposition
5.1
and (
5.2
), we have

\rho_{\textit{\L}^{v}(Q,V)}\Big(\fint_{Q}b_{Q}f\Big)\lesssim\rho_{\textit{\L}^{u}(Q,V)}\Big(\fint_{Q}b_{Q}f\Big)

for any
u\in(0,\infty)
.
On the other hand, by Proposition
8.1
,

\begin{split}\rho_{\textit{\L}^{u}(Q,V)}\Big(\fint_{Q}b_{Q}f\Big)&=\Big\|\frac{\mathbf{1}_{Q}}{|Q|^{\frac{1}{u}}}\int\frac{b_{Q}}{|Q|^{\frac{1}{u^{\prime}}}}\frac{f}{|Q|^{\frac{1}{u}}}\Big\|_{L^{u}(V)}\\
&\leq\sup_{\mathfrak{e}\in\mathfrak{X}\setminus\{0\}}\frac{\rho_{L^{u}(\mathbf{1}_{Q}|Q|^{-\frac{1}{u}}V)}(\mathfrak{e})}{\rho_{L^{u^{\prime}}(b_{Q}|Q|^{-\frac{1}{u^{\prime}}}V^{-*})}^{*}(\mathfrak{e})}\|\mathbf{1}_{Q}|Q|^{-\frac{1}{u}}f\|_{L^{u}(V)}\\
&=\sup_{\mathfrak{e}\in\mathfrak{X}\setminus\{0\}}\frac{\rho_{\textit{\L}^{u}(Q,V)}(\mathfrak{e})}{\rho_{\textit{\L}^{u^{\prime}}(Q,b_{Q}V^{-*})}^{*}(\mathfrak{e})}\|f\|_{\textit{\L}^{u}(Q,V)}.\\
\end{split}

Taking
u=(p^{\prime}+\varepsilon)^{\prime}
, thus
u^{\prime}=p^{\prime}+\varepsilon
, it follows that

\begin{split}\rho_{\textit{\L}^{u^{\prime}}(Q,b_{Q}V^{-*})}(\mathfrak{e}^{*})&\leq\rho_{\textit{\L}^{u^{\prime}}(Q,V^{-*})}(\mathfrak{e}^{*})\qquad\text{since }|b_{Q}|\leq\mathbf{1}_{Q},\\
&\lesssim\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(Q,V^{-*})}(\mathfrak{e}^{*})\qquad\text{by Proposition \ref {prop:RHI}},\end{split}

and hence
\rho_{\textit{\L}^{u^{\prime}}(Q,b_{Q}V^{-*})}^{*}(\mathfrak{e})\gtrsim\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(Q,V^{-*})}^{*}(\mathfrak{e})
. Thus

\frac{\rho_{\textit{\L}^{u}(Q,V)}(\mathfrak{e})}{\rho_{\textit{\L}^{u^{\prime}}(Q,b_{Q}V^{-*})}^{*}(\mathfrak{e})}\lesssim\frac{\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,V)}(\mathfrak{e})}{\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(Q,V^{-*})}^{*}(\mathfrak{e})}\lesssim 1

by the
\mathscr{A}_{p}
condition in the last step. Thus, we have checked that

(8.9)

\rho_{\textit{\L}^{v}(Q,V)}\Big(\fint_{Q}b_{Q}f\Big)\lesssim\|f\|_{\textit{\L}^{u}(Q,V)},\qquad\begin{cases}v=p+\varepsilon,\\
u=(p^{\prime}+\varepsilon)^{\prime}.\end{cases}

Substituting (
8.8
) and (
8.9
) into (
8.7
), and using the sparseness assumption
|Q|\lesssim|E(Q)|
for some disjoint subsets
E(Q)\subseteq Q
, we obtain

(8.10)

\begin{split}|\langle Tf,g\rangle|&\lesssim\sum_{Q\in\mathscr{S}}|E(Q)|\|f\|_{\textit{\L}^{u}(Q,V)}\|g\|_{\textit{\L}^{v^{\prime}}(Q,V^{-*})}\\
&\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}M_{u}(Vf)M_{v^{\prime}}(V^{-*}g)\\
&\leq\|M_{u}(Vf)\|_{L^{p}}\|M_{v^{\prime}}(V^{-*}g)\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}}\\
&\lesssim\|Vf\|_{L^{p}}\|V^{-*}g\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}}=\|f\|_{L^{p}(V)}\|g\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}(V^{-*})}\end{split}

by the maximal theorem, since
p>u
and
p^{\prime}>v^{\prime}
. This shows that
\|Tf\|_{L^{p}(V)}\lesssim\|f\|_{L^{p}(V)}
by duality.

Convergence of the infinite series

We then turn to the general case of infinite
\mathscr{S}
. We will show that the series defining
Tf
converges unconditionally in
L^{p}(V)
, i.e., that all

T_{k}f:=\sum_{Q\in\mathscr{S}_{k}}a_{Q}\fint_{Q}b_{Q}f

with
k\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\}

form a Cauchy sequence in
L^{p}(V)
whenever
\mathscr{S}_{k}\subseteq\mathscr{S}
are finite subsets with
\mathscr{S}_{k}\uparrow\mathscr{S}
. Thus, we need to show that
T_{j}f-T_{k}f=\sum_{Q\in\mathscr{S}_{j}\setminus\mathscr{S}_{k}}a_{Q}\fint_{Q}b_{Q}f
converges to zero in
L^{p}(V)
as
j>k\to\infty
.

For all
M,N\in\mathbb{N}
, there are only finitely many dyadic cubes
Q\in\mathscr{S}
such that
Q\subseteq[-2^{M},2^{M}]
and
2^{-N}\leq\ell(Q)\leq 2^{M}
. Hence, for all
k\geq k(M,N)
, all such cubes will be contained in
\mathscr{S}_{k}
and hence not contained in
\mathscr{S}_{j}\setminus\mathscr{S}_{k}
for
j>k\geq k(M,N)
. In other words,
\mathscr{S}_{j}\setminus\mathscr{S}_{k}
only contains cubes that are

(8.11)

\begin{cases}\text{large:}&\ell(Q)>2^{M},\quad\text{or}\\
\text{far:}&Q\subseteq[-2^{M},2^{M}]^{c},\quad\text{or}\\
\text{small:}&Q\subseteq[-2^{M},2^{M}]\quad\text{and}\quad\ell(Q)<2^{-N}.\\
\end{cases}

We now apply (
8.10
) with
\mathscr{S}_{j}\setminus\mathscr{S}_{k}
in place of
\mathscr{S}
, observing that we can replace the maximal function by a slightly smaller object, where we only take the supremum over the cubes that actually appear in the sum:

(8.12)

\begin{split}|\langle T_{j}f-T_{k}f,g\rangle|&\lesssim\sum_{Q\in\mathscr{S}_{j}\setminus\mathscr{S}_{k}}|E(Q)|\|f\|_{\textit{\L}^{u}(Q,V)}\|g\|_{\textit{\L}^{v^{\prime}}(Q,V^{-*})}\\
&\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}M_{u}^{\mathscr{S}_{j}\setminus\mathscr{S}_{k}}(Vf)M_{v^{\prime}}(V^{-*}g)\\
&\lesssim\|M_{u}^{\mathscr{S}_{j}\setminus\mathscr{S}_{k}}(Vf)\|_{L^{p}}\|g\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}(V^{-*})},\end{split}

where (writing simply
\phi:=Vf\in L^{p}
)

\begin{split}M_{u}^{\mathscr{S}_{j}\setminus\mathscr{S}_{k}}\phi(x)&:=\sup_{x\in Q\in\mathscr{S}_{j}\setminus\mathscr{S}_{k}}\|\phi\|_{\textit{\L}^{u}(Q)}\leq M_{u,M}^{\operatorname{large}}\phi(x)+M_{u,M}^{\operatorname{far}}\phi(x)+M_{u,M,N,\mathscr{S}}^{\operatorname{small}}\phi(x)\end{split}

and the last three maximal operators are defined by taking the supremum over the respective collections of cubes defined in (
8.11
). It is evident that each of them is pointwise dominated by
M_{u}\phi\in L^{p}
; hence, by dominated convergence, to show their
L^{p}
convergence to zero, it is enough to show pointwise convergence.

Concerning
M_{u,M}^{\operatorname{large}}\phi
, we note that

\|\phi\|_{\textit{\L}^{u}(Q)}\leq\|\phi\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q)}\leq|Q|^{-\frac{1}{p}}\|\phi\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq 2^{-\frac{nM}{p}}\|\phi\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}

for
\ell(Q)>2^{M}
, and hence

M_{u,M}^{\operatorname{large}}\phi(x)\leq 2^{-\frac{nM}{p}}\|\phi\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\to 0

as
M\to\infty
. For the “far” case, it is even simpler to see that

M_{u,M}^{\operatorname{far}}\phi(x)\leq\mathbf{1}_{[-2^{M},2^{M}]^{c}}(x)M_{u}\phi(x)\to 0.

Finally, we turn to the case of small cubes. This requires a little more care and in particular the fact that we are dealing with subsets of a fixed collection
\mathscr{S}
of sparse cubes only. (Note that the full maximal function over all cubes, when restricted to small cubes, converges to
|\phi(x)|
instead of
0
by Lebesgue’s differentiation theorem!)

The maximal function
M_{u,M,N,\mathscr{S}}^{\operatorname{small}}\phi
is supported in the union
U_{M,N}
of cubes
Q\in\mathscr{S}
with
Q\subseteq[-2^{M},2^{M}]
and
\ell(Q)<2^{-N}
. The measure of this union is dominated by

\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{Q\in\mathscr S: \ell(Q)<2^{-N}}{Q\subseteq[-2^M,2^M]}}|Q|\lesssim\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{Q\in\mathscr S}{Q\subseteq[-2^M,2^M]}}|E(Q)|\leq|[-2^{M},2^{M}]|=2^{n(M+1)},

and hence by the tail (with respect to the condition
\ell(Q)<2^{-N}
) of a convergent series. Thus,
|U_{M,N}|\to 0
as
N\to\infty
. Since clearly
U_{M,N}\supseteq U_{M,N+1}
, it follows that
U_{M,N}\downarrow U_{M,\infty}
as
N\to\infty
, where
|U_{M,\infty}|=0
. Hence

M_{u,M,N,\mathscr{S}}^{\operatorname{small}}\phi(x)\leq\mathbf{1}_{U_{M,N}}(x)M_{u}\phi(x)\to 0

almost everywhere as
N\to\infty
.

To summarise, given
\varepsilon>0
, we can first choose
M
so large that

\|M_{u,M}^{\operatorname{large}}\phi\|_{L^{p}}<\frac{\varepsilon}{3},\qquad\|M_{u,M}^{\operatorname{large}}\phi\|_{L^{p}}<\frac{\varepsilon}{3},

and then
N
so large that

\|M_{u,M,N,\mathscr{S}}^{\operatorname{small}}\phi\|_{L^{p}}<\frac{\varepsilon}{3}

to see that

\|M_{u}^{\mathscr{S}_{j}\setminus\mathscr{S}_{k}}\phi\|_{L^{p}}<\varepsilon

for all
j>k\geq k(M,N)
. In view of (
8.12
), we find that
\{T_{k}f\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\}}
is a Cauchy sequence in
L^{p}(V)
, which we wanted to prove.
∎

9.
Unbounded commutators

The aim of this section is to prove the following statement:

Theorem 9.1
.

Let
\mathfrak{X}
be an infinite-dimensional Banach space and let
p\in(1,\infty)
. Then there exists a function
B\in\operatorname{BMO}_{\operatorname{so}}(\mathbb{R};\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X}))
with
B^{*}\in\operatorname{BMO}_{\operatorname{so}}(\mathbb{R};\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X}^{*}))
such that its commutator
[B,\mathcal{H}]
with the Hilbert transform

\mathcal{H}f(x):=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{\pi}\Big(\int_{-\infty}^{x-\varepsilon}+\int_{x+\varepsilon}^{\infty}\Big)\frac{f(y)dy}{x-y}

is not bounded on
L^{p}(\mathbb{R};\mathfrak{X})
.

While not making direct reference to operator-weighted spaces, this result is closely related, and this connection will be elaborated in the following section, where we return to the setting of
L^{p}(V)
. In the special case that
p=2
and
\mathfrak{X}
is a Hilbert space, Theorem
9.1
is contained in
[
32
, Theorem 1.2]
,
[
54
, Theorem 1.2]
, or
[
34
, Theorem 1.3]
. Formally, the said results state a dimensional growth of the commutator norms in finite-dimensional Hilbert spaces, but it is easy to obtain the infinite-dimensional version from this by noting that an infinite-dimensional Hilbert space can be written as an
\ell^{2}
direct sum containing all the finite-dimensional versions.

To construct a function
B
as in Theorem
9.1
, we will use two different methods depending on the Banach space
\mathfrak{X}
. The first method works if
\mathfrak{X}
is
not
a UMD space
[
39
, Definition 4.2.1]
, while the second method requires
\mathfrak{X}
to be
K
-convex
[
39
, Definition 4.3.9]
. Since every UMD space is
K
-convex
[
39
, Proposition 4.3.10]
, the two cases cover all Banach spaces, even with some overlap, since not every
K
-convex space has UMD.

9.1.
Scalar commutators and the UMD property

For Banach spaces that fail the UMD property, Theorem
9.1
will be a consequence of the following:

Proposition 9.2
.

Let
\mathfrak{X}
be an infinite-dimensional Banach space and let
p\in(1,\infty)
. Then the following conditions are equivalent:

(i)

\mathfrak{X}
is a UMD space;

(ii)

[b,\mathcal{H}]
is bounded on
L^{p}(\mathbb{R};\mathfrak{X})
for all scalar-valued
b\in\operatorname{BMO}(\mathbb{R})
;

(iii)

[b,\mathcal{H}]
is bounded on
L^{p}(\mathbb{R};\mathfrak{X})
for all scalar-valued
b\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
.

The statement is perhaps expected, in view of the well-known characterisation of the UMD condition by the boundedness of the Hilbert transform on
L^{p}(\mathbb{R};\mathfrak{X})
. While this will be used in the proof below, observe that some work still needs to be done, since the (un)boundedness of
\mathcal{H}
is not the same as the (un)boundedness of a commutator
[b,\mathcal{H}]
, and their equivalence will in any case require some conditions on the function
b
. Proposition
9.2
continues the tradition of characterising the UMD condition by the boundedness of various operators, like the Hilbert transform
[
8
,
16
]
, imaginary powers
(-\triangle)^{is}
of the Laplacian
[
37
]
, or other Fourier multipliers with sufficient structure
[
31
]
. (The mentioned results can also be found in
[
39
, Theorem 5.1.1]
,
[
40
, Corollary 10.5.2]
, and
[
41
, Corollary 13.3.10]
, respectively.) In contrast to all these examples, the commutators
[b,\mathcal{H}]
in Proposition
9.2
are
not
translation-invariant operators.

Proof of Proposition
9.2
.

(
i
)
\Rightarrow
(
ii
): This can be proved by repeating the classical Cauchy integral trick of
[
19
]
, where the scalar-valuedness of
b
is used, while the vector-valuedness of
f
poses no problem. An easy identity followed by the Cauchy integral formula shows that

[b,\mathcal{H}]f=\frac{d}{dz}(e^{zb}\mathcal{H}(e^{-zb}f))\Big|_{z=0}=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{|z|=\varepsilon}e^{zb}\mathcal{H}(e^{-zb}f)\,\frac{dz}{z^{2}}.

The assumption (
i
) implies that
\mathcal{H}
is bounded on
L^{p}(v;\mathfrak{X})
for all scalar-valued weights
v\in\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R})
(see
[
41
, Corollary 11.3.27]
). If
\varepsilon>0
is small enough (depending on
\|b\|_{\operatorname{BMO}(\mathbb{R})}
), it is well known and easy to check that
e^{\Re(zb)}\in\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R})
, uniformly in
|z|=\varepsilon
, as already observed in
[
19
]
. Combining these observations, it follows that

\begin{split}\|[b,\mathcal{H}]f\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R};\mathfrak{X})}&\leq\frac{1}{2\pi}\oint_{|z|=\varepsilon}\|e^{\Re(zb)}\mathcal{H}(e^{-zb}f)\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R};\mathfrak{X})}\,\frac{|dz|}{|z|^{2}}\\
&\lesssim\oint_{|z|=\varepsilon}\|e^{\Re(zb)}(e^{-zb}f)\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R};\mathfrak{X})}\,\frac{|dz|}{|z|^{2}}\\
&=\oint_{|z|=\varepsilon}\|f\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R};\mathfrak{X})}\,\frac{|dz|}{|z|^{2}}\\
&\lesssim\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\|f\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R};\mathfrak{X})}\approx\|b\|_{\operatorname{BMO}(\mathbb{R})}\|f\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R};\mathfrak{X})}.\end{split}

(
ii
)
\Rightarrow
(
iii
) is obvious. Let us also note that (
i
)
\Rightarrow
(
iii
) can be seen directly without the need for the Cauchy integral trick or the weighted theory, using only the unweighted boundedness of
\mathcal{H}
under the UMD condition:

\begin{split}\|[b,\mathcal{H}]f\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R};\mathfrak{X})}&\leq\|b\mathcal{H}f\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R};\mathfrak{X})}+\|\mathcal{H}(bf)\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R};\mathfrak{X})}\lesssim\|b\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}\|f\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R};\mathfrak{X})}.\end{split}

(
iii
)
\Rightarrow
(
i
):
Let us fix a function
b
such that

(9.1)

b(x)=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }x\in[m-\frac{1}{4},m+\frac{1}{4}]\text{ for an even }m\in\mathbb{Z},\\
-1&\text{if }x\in[m-\frac{1}{4},m+\frac{1}{4}]\text{ for an odd }m\in\mathbb{Z},\end{cases}

where
\mathbb{Z}
denotes the set of all integers.
Let us fix a bump
\varphi
of integral one supported in
[-\frac{1}{4},\frac{1}{4}]
, and consider

f(y)=\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{j\in\Z}{\text{odd}}}a_{j}\varphi(y-j),\quad g(x)=\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{k\in\Z}{\text{even}}}c_{k}\varphi(x-k),

so that
\|f\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R};\mathfrak{X})}\approx\|\{a_{j}\}\|_{\ell^{p}(\mathbb{Z}_{\textup{odd}};\mathfrak{X})}
and
\|g\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R};\mathfrak{X})}\approx\|\{c_{j}\}\|_{\ell^{p^{\prime}}(\mathbb{Z}_{\textup{even}};\mathfrak{X}^{*})}
.

Then
bf=-f
and
bg=g
, hence

(9.2)

\begin{split}\langle[b,\mathcal{H}]f,g\rangle&=\langle\mathcal{H}f,bg\rangle-\langle\mathcal{H}(bf),g\rangle\\
&=2\langle\mathcal{H}f,g\rangle=\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{j\in\Z_{\text{odd}}}{k\in\Z_{\text{even}}}}2d_{k-j}\langle a_{j},c_{k}\rangle,\\
\end{split}

where the factor

\begin{split}d_{k-j}:=\iint\frac{1}{x-y}\varphi(y-j)\varphi(x-k)\,dx\,dy=\iint\frac{\varphi(v)\varphi(u)}{(k+u)-(j+v)}\,du\,dv\end{split}

differs from
(k-j)^{-1}=(k-j)^{-1}\iint\varphi(u)\varphi(v)dudv
by

\Big|\iint\frac{v-u}{(k-j+u-v)(k-j)}\varphi(u)\varphi(v)dudv\Big|\lesssim\frac{1}{(k-j)^{2}}.

By (
9.2
) and assumption (
iii
), the mapping

\{a_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}_{\text{odd}}}\mapsto\Big\{\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}_{\text{odd}}}d_{k-j}a_{j}\Big\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\text{even}}}

is bounded from
\ell^{p}(\mathbb{Z}_{\text{odd}};\mathfrak{X})
to
\ell^{p}(\mathbb{Z}_{\text{even}};\mathfrak{X})
. Since the same is clearly true with
(k-j)^{-2}
in place of
d_{k-j}
, it is also true with
(k-j)^{-1}
in place of
d_{k-j}
, by the above estimate on their difference.

By a change of variables, it follows that the discrete convolution by
\{(k+\frac{1}{2})^{-1}\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}
is bounded on
\ell^{p}(\mathbb{Z};\mathfrak{X})
. The difference of this kernel from
\{\mathbf{1}_{\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}}k^{-1}\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}
is dominated by
\{(1+k^{2})^{-1}\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\ell^{1}(\mathbb{Z})
, which clearly induces a bounded convolution operator on
\ell^{p}(\mathbb{Z};\mathfrak{X})
. Hence, the same is true of
\{\mathbf{1}_{\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}}k^{-1}\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}
. But the convolution with this sequence is the discrete Hilbert transform, whose boundedness on
\ell^{p}(\mathbb{Z};\mathfrak{X})
is equivalent to the UMD property of
\mathfrak{X}
by
[
6
, Theorem 2.8]
. This completes the proof.
∎

Remark 9.3
.

The proof of (
iii
)
\Rightarrow
(
i
) shows that (
iii
) could be further weakened by requiring only that
[b,\mathcal{H}]
is bounded for a single function
b
satisfying (
9.1
); in particular
b
may be taken to be
C^{\infty}
and periodic, or alternatively piecewise constant and periodic. On the other hand, it
cannot
be taken significantly simpler than this, as shown by the following:

Proposition 9.4
.

Suppose that
b:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{C}
satisfies the following properties for finitely many points
a_{1}<a_{2}<\ldots<a_{n}
of
\mathbb{R}
:

(i)

b\in C^{1}((a_{i},a_{i+1}))
with one-sided limits at the end-points, for each
i=1,\ldots,n-1
;

(ii)

b
has (possibly different) constant values on each of the unbounded intervals
(-\infty,a_{0})
and
(a_{n},\infty)
.

Then
[b,\mathcal{H}]
is bounded on
L^{p}(\mathbb{R};\mathfrak{X})
for every
p\in(1,\infty)
and every Banach space
\mathfrak{X}
.

Proof.

Let
I_{0}:=(-\infty,a_{0})
,
I_{i}:=(a_{i},a_{i+1})
for
i\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}
, and
I_{n}:=(a_{n},\infty)
. Moreover, let
c_{i}
be the constant value of
b
on
I_{i}
for
i\in\{0,n\}
. Then

\begin{split}[b,\mathcal{H}]f&=\sum_{i,j=0}^{n}\mathbf{1}_{I_{i}}[b,\mathcal{H}](\mathbf{1}_{I_{j}}f).\end{split}

For the terms with
i=j\in\{0,n\}
, we note that

\mathbf{1}_{I_{i}}[b,\mathcal{H}](\mathbf{1}_{I_{i}}f)=\mathbf{1}_{I_{i}}[c_{i},\mathcal{H}](\mathbf{1}_{I_{i}}f)=0.

For
i=j\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}
, we note that
[b,\mathcal{H}]
is an integral operator with kernel
(b(x)-b(y))/(x-y)
; for
x,y\in I_{i}
, this is uniformly bounded by
\|\mathbf{1}_{I_{i}}b^{\prime}\|_{\infty}<\infty
. Hence

\begin{split}\|\mathbf{1}_{I_{i}}[b,\mathcal{H}](\mathbf{1}_{I_{i}}f)\|_{p}&\leq|I_{i}|^{\frac{1}{p}}\|[b,\mathcal{H}](\mathbf{1}_{I_{i}}f)\|_{\infty}\\
&\leq|I_{i}|^{\frac{1}{p}}\|\mathbf{1}_{I_{i}}b^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\|\mathbf{1}_{I_{i}}f\|_{1}\leq|I_{i}|\|\mathbf{1}_{I_{i}}b^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\|f\|_{p}.\end{split}

For the remaining terms
i\neq j
, we note that the restriction of the Hilbert transform
f\mapsto\mathbf{1}_{I_{i}}\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{1}_{I_{j}}f)
on complementary intervals
I_{i}\cap I_{j}=\varnothing
has a kernel that does not change sign, and hence it extends boundedly from
L^{p}(\mathbb{R})
to
L^{p}(\mathbb{R};\mathfrak{X})
for an arbitrary Banach space
\mathfrak{X}
(see
[
39
, Theorem 2.1.3]
). Thus,

\|\mathbf{1}_{I_{i}}b\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{1}_{I_{j}}f)\|_{p}\leq\|b\|_{\infty}\|\mathbf{1}_{I_{i}}\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{1}_{I_{j}}f)\|_{p}\lesssim\|b\|_{\infty}\|f\|_{p}

and the estimate with
\mathcal{H}b
in place of
b\mathcal{H}
is similar. Also note that

\|b\|_{\infty}=\max\{|c_{0}|,|c_{n}|,\|\mathbf{1}_{I_{i}}b\|_{\infty}:i=1,\ldots,n-1\}

is finite for the functions of the type that we consider.
∎

9.2.
Operator commutators and
K
-convexity

We now turn to the more delicate part in the proof of Theorem
9.1
. While Proposition
9.2
provides us with a required function
B=b
for non-UMD spaces, it also shows that a scalar-valued function will not suffice in the case of UMD spaces, and hence a genuinely operator-valued construction will be necessary. While additional steps will be needed, it turns out that we can make use of the examples already available in finite-dimensional Hilbert spaces. For concreteness, we recall the following precise statement:

Theorem 9.5

(
[
54
, Theorem 1.2]
)
.

Let
E
be a
d
-dimensional Hilbert space. Then there exists a function
B=B^{*}
with

\|B\|_{\operatorname{BMO}_{\operatorname{so}}(\mathbb{R};\mathscr{L}(E))}\leq 1,\qquad\|[B,\mathcal{H}]\|_{\mathscr{L}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R};E))}\gtrsim\log d,

where
\mathcal{H}
is the Hilbert transform and the implied constant is absolute.

Corollary 9.6
.

In the situation of Theorem
9.5
, we also have

\|[B,\mathcal{H}]\|_{\mathscr{L}(L^{p}(\mathbb{R};E))}\gtrsim\log d\qquad\forall\ p\in(1,\infty).

Proof.

The adjoint of
[B,\mathcal{H}]
with respect to the standard duality of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R};E)
is

[B,\mathcal{H}]^{*}=[\mathcal{H}^{*},B^{*}]=[-\mathcal{H},B]=[B,\mathcal{H}];

thus, this operator is self-adjoint. Since
(L^{p}(\mathbb{R};E))^{*}=L^{p^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R};E)
, we find that

\|[B,\mathcal{H}]\|_{\mathscr{L}(L^{p}(\mathbb{R};E))}=\|[B,\mathcal{H}]^{*}\|_{\mathscr{L}((L^{p}(\mathbb{R};E)^{*})}=\|[B,\mathcal{H}]\|_{\mathscr{L}(L^{p^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R};E))}.

Since
\frac{1}{2}=\frac{1}{2}(\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{p^{\prime}})
, it follows from the Riesz–Thorin interpolation theorem that

\begin{split}\|[B,\mathcal{H}]\|_{\mathscr{L}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R};E))}&\leq\|[B,\mathcal{H}]\|_{\mathscr{L}(L^{p}(\mathbb{R};E))}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|[B,\mathcal{H}]\|_{\mathscr{L}(L^{p^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R};E))}^{\frac{1}{2}}\\
&=\|[B,\mathcal{H}]\|_{\mathscr{L}(L^{p}(\mathbb{R};E))}.\end{split}

The claim of the corollary is now immediate from Theorem
9.5
.
∎

To proceed, we will need to embed the Hilbert space examples into a given Banach space
\mathfrak{X}
in a quantitatively controlled manner. This will be accomplished with the help of the following result, which depends on the notion of the
K
-convexity
(see
[
39
, Definition 4.3.9]
or
[
40
, Section 7.4.a]
) of a Banach space.

Theorem 9.7

(
[
23
]
)
.

Let
\mathfrak{X}
be a
K
-convex normed space. Then there are positive constants
c,C
depending only on
\mathfrak{X}
with the following properties.
Every finite-dimensional subspace
F\subset\mathfrak{X}
with
\dim F=n
has a further subspace
G\subset F
such that
\dim G\geq cn^{c}
and

(i)

there is a Hilbert space
H
and an isomorphism
\iota:G\to H
with

\|\iota\|\|\iota^{-1}\|\leq 2;

(ii)

there is a projection
P
of
\mathfrak{X}
onto
G
with
\|P\|\leq C
.

Proof.

While this is implicitly contained in
[
23
]
, we indicate the required details to obtain the above formulation from the results explicitly stated in
[
23
]
.
First,
[
23
, Corollary 8.3]
contains a similar statement except that the
K
-convexity is replaced by the assumption that both
\mathfrak{X}
and
\mathfrak{X}^{*}
have some finite
cotype

q,q^{*}<\infty
(see
[
40
, Definition 7.1.1]
) and conclusion (
ii
) is replaced by
\|P\|\leq 27\mathscr{K}_{n}(\mathfrak{X})
for a quantity
\mathscr{K}_{n}(\mathfrak{X})
defined at the beginning of
[
23
, Section 8]
.

Now, suppose that
\mathfrak{X}
is
K
-convex, as assumed in Theorem
9.7
. Then
\mathfrak{X}
has some non-trivial type
p>1
and some finite cotype
q<\infty
by
[
40
, Proposition 7.4.12]
, and then also
\mathfrak{X}^{*}
has finite cotype
q^{*}=p^{\prime}<\infty
by
[
40
, Proposition 7.1.13]
. Thus
\mathfrak{X}
satisfies the assumptions, and hence the conclusions, of
[
23
, Corollary 8.3]
. Moreover,
[
23
, Proposition 9.1]
shows that the
K
-convexity is equivalent to
\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathscr{K}_{n}(\mathfrak{X})<\infty
. Thus, the conclusions of
[
23
, Corollary 8.3]
take exactly the form stated in Theorem
9.7
, with
C=27\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathscr{K}_{n}(\mathfrak{X})<\infty
depending only on
\mathfrak{X}
.
∎

Corollary 9.8
.

Let
\mathfrak{X}
be a
K
-convex normed space with
\dim\mathfrak{X}=\infty
.
Then it has subspaces
G
of any given finite dimension with properties (
i
) and (
ii
).

Proof.

Since we can take any
n
in Theorem
9.7
, the said theorem guarantees that we can find
G
with
\dim G\geq cn^{c}

larger than
any given number. It remains to see that we can find
G
with
\dim G

equal to
any given number
m
. To see this, let
G\subset\mathfrak{X}
and
H
be the spaces provided by Theorem
9.7
with
\dim G=\dim H>m
. Let
H^{\prime}\subset H
be a Hilbert space with
\dim H^{\prime}=m
, and let
Q
be the orthogonal projection of
H
onto
H^{\prime}
. If
\iota:G\to H
is the isomorphism provided by Theorem
9.7
, then
G^{\prime}:=\iota^{-1}H^{\prime}\subset\iota^{-1}H=G\subset\mathfrak{X}
is a subspace with
\dim G^{\prime}=\dim H^{\prime}=m
, and the restriction
\iota^{\prime}:=\iota|_{G^{\prime}}
is an isomorphism of
\iota^{\prime}:G^{\prime}\to H^{\prime}
with

\|\iota^{\prime}\|\|(\iota^{\prime})^{-1}\|\leq\|\iota\|\|\iota^{-1}\|\leq 2.

Moreover,
P^{\prime}:=\iota^{-1}\circ Q\circ\iota\circ P:\mathfrak{X}\to G^{\prime}
is a projection of norm

\|P^{\prime}\|\leq\|\iota^{-1}\|\|Q\|\|\iota\|\|P\|\leq\|\iota^{-1}\|\cdot 1\cdot\|\iota\|\cdot C\leq 2C.

Hence
G^{\prime}\subset\mathfrak{X}
with the given dimension
\dim G^{\prime}=m
satisfies (
i
) and (
ii
) of Theorem
9.7
with
2C
in place of
C
, but this is just another constant that depends only on
\mathfrak{X}
.
∎

In the following lemma, we embed a sequence of finite-dimensional examples into a given
K
-convex space
\mathfrak{X}
, taking
\mathfrak{X}_{1}=\mathfrak{X}_{2}=\mathfrak{X}
in the statement. We prove the result slightly more generally with two spaces
\mathfrak{X}_{j}
, since this requires almost no additional effort but will be convenient for a subsequent discussion.

Lemma 9.9
.

For
j=1,2
, let
\mathfrak{X}_{j}
be infinite-dimensional
K
-convex Banach spaces. Then there exist functions
B_{k}\in\operatorname{BMO}_{\operatorname{so}}(\mathbb{R};\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X}_{1},\mathfrak{X}_{2}))
with
B_{k}^{*}\in\operatorname{BMO}_{\operatorname{so}}(\mathbb{R};\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X}_{2}^{*},\mathfrak{X}_{1}^{*}))
such that

\|B_{k}\|_{\operatorname{BMO}_{\operatorname{so}}(\mathbb{R};\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X}_{1},\mathfrak{X}_{2}))}\leq 1,\qquad\|B_{k}^{*}\|_{\operatorname{BMO}_{\operatorname{so}}(\mathbb{R};\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X}_{2}^{*},\mathfrak{X}_{1}^{*}))}\leq 1,

and

\|[B_{k},\mathcal{H}]\|_{\mathscr{L}(L^{p}(\mathbb{R};\mathfrak{X}_{1}),L^{p}(\mathbb{R};\mathfrak{X}_{2}))}>k.

Proof.

By Corollary
9.8
, both spaces
\mathfrak{X}_{j}
have subspaces
G_{j}
with the properties (
i
) and (
ii
) of Theorem
9.7
, and
\dim G_{1}=\dim G_{2}=N
can be taken equal and as large as we like. Let
\iota_{j}
,
H_{j}
,
P_{j}
, and
C_{j}
have the same meaning as in the said properties with
G_{j}
in place of
G
. By redefining the norm on
H_{j}
with a multiplicative constant, we may assume, in place of
\|\iota_{j}\|\|\iota_{j}^{-1}\|\leq 2
, that
\|\iota_{j}\|=1
and
\|\iota_{j}^{-1}\|\leq 2
. Since two Hilbert spaces of the same dimension are isometrically isomorphic, we may further take
H:=H_{1}=H_{2}
. Let
C:=\max(C_{1},C_{2})
.

By Corollary
9.6
, there is a function
B\in\operatorname{BMO}_{\operatorname{so}}(\mathbb{R};\mathscr{L}(H))
with
B^{*}(=B)\in\operatorname{BMO}_{\operatorname{so}}(\mathbb{R};\mathscr{L}(H))
of norm at most one in these spaces such that

\|[B,\mathcal{H}]\|_{\mathscr{L}(L^{p}(\mathbb{R};H))}\gtrsim\log\dim H=\log\dim G_{j}=\log N.

For all
x\in\mathbb{R}
, we define

\tilde{B}(x):=J_{2}\circ\iota^{-1}_{2}\circ B(x)\circ\iota_{1}\circ P_{1}\in\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X}_{1},\mathfrak{X}_{2}),

where
J_{2}:G_{2}\to\mathfrak{X}_{2}
is the injection
\mathfrak{e}\mapsto\mathfrak{e}
.
The adjoint is

\tilde{B}(x)^{*}=P_{1}^{*}\circ\iota_{1}^{*}\circ B(x)^{*}\circ\iota_{2}^{-*}\circ J_{2}^{*}\in\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X}_{2}^{*},\mathfrak{X}_{1}^{*}).

For
\mathfrak{e}\in\mathfrak{X}_{1}
,

\begin{split}\|\tilde{B}(\cdot)\mathfrak{e}\|_{\operatorname{BMO}(\mathbb{R};\mathfrak{X}_{2})}&\leq\|J_{2}\|\|\iota_{2}^{-1}\|\|B(\cdot)(\iota_{1}(P_{1}\mathfrak{e}))\|_{\operatorname{BMO}(\mathbb{R};H)}\\
&\leq 1\cdot 2\cdot\|B\|_{\operatorname{BMO}_{\operatorname{so}}(\mathbb{R};\mathscr{L}(H))}\|\iota_{1}(P_{1}\mathfrak{e}))\|_{H},\end{split}

where
\|\iota_{1}(P_{1}\mathfrak{e}))\|_{H}\leq\|\iota_{1}\|\|P_{1}\|\|\mathfrak{e}\|_{\mathfrak{X}_{1}}\leq C\|\mathfrak{e}\|_{\mathfrak{X}_{1}}
.
Thus

(9.3)

\|\tilde{B}\|_{\operatorname{BMO}_{\operatorname{so}}(\mathbb{R};\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X}_{1},\mathfrak{X}_{2}))}\leq 2C\|B\|_{\operatorname{BMO}_{\operatorname{so}}(\mathbb{R};\mathscr{L}(H))}.

Similarly, for
\mathfrak{e}^{*}\in\mathfrak{X}_{2}^{*}
,

\begin{split}\|\tilde{B}(\cdot)^{*}\mathfrak{e}^{*}\|_{\operatorname{BMO}(\mathbb{R};\mathfrak{X}_{1}^{*})}&\leq\|P_{1}^{*}\|\|\iota_{1}^{*}\|\|\tilde{B}(\cdot)^{*}(\iota_{2}^{-*}(J_{2}^{*}\mathfrak{e}^{*}))\|_{\operatorname{BMO}(\mathbb{R};H)}\\
&\leq\|P_{1}\|\|\iota_{1}\|\|\tilde{B}^{*}\|_{\operatorname{BMO}_{\operatorname{so}}(\mathbb{R};\mathscr{L}(H))}\|\iota_{2}^{-*}(J_{2}^{*}\mathfrak{e}^{*}))\|_{H},\end{split}

where
\|P_{1}\|\|\iota_{1}\|\leq C\cdot 1
, and

\|\iota_{2}^{-*}(J_{2}^{*}\mathfrak{e}^{*}))\|_{H}\leq\|\iota_{2}^{-*}\|\|J_{2}^{*}\|\|\mathfrak{e}^{*}\|_{\mathfrak{X}_{2}^{*}}=\|\iota_{2}^{-1}\|\|J_{2}\|\|\mathfrak{e}^{*}\|_{\mathfrak{X}_{2}^{*}}\leq 2\|\mathfrak{e}^{*}\|_{\mathfrak{X}^{*}}.

Thus

(9.4)

\|\tilde{B}^{*}\|_{\operatorname{BMO}_{\operatorname{so}}(\mathbb{R};\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X}_{1}^{*}))}\leq 2C\|B^{*}\|_{\operatorname{BMO}_{\operatorname{so}}(\mathbb{R};\mathscr{L}(H))}.

Moreover,

\begin{split}\|[B,\mathcal{H}]\|_{\mathscr{L}(L^{p}(\mathbb{R};H))}&=\|\iota_{2}\circ\iota_{2}^{-1}[B,\mathcal{H}]\iota_{1}\circ\iota_{1}^{-1}\|_{\mathscr{L}(L^{p}(\mathbb{R};H))}\\
&\leq\|\iota_{2}\|\|\iota_{2}^{-1}[B,\mathcal{H}]\iota\|_{\mathscr{L}(L^{p}(\mathbb{R};G_{1}),L^{p}(\mathbb{R};G_{2}))}\|\iota_{1}^{-1}\|\\
&\leq 1\cdot\|J_{2}\iota_{2}^{-1}[B,\mathcal{H}]\iota_{1}P_{1}\|_{\mathscr{L}(L^{p}(\mathbb{R};\mathfrak{X}_{1}),L^{p}(\mathbb{R};\mathfrak{X}_{2}))}\cdot 2,\end{split}

where the last estimate follows from the fact that
\iota_{2}^{-1}[B,\mathcal{H}]\iota_{1}
is the restriction of
J_{2}\iota^{-1}_{2}[B,\mathcal{H}]\iota_{1}P_{1}
to
L^{p}(\mathbb{R};G_{1})
. Since the operators
\iota_{j}
,
J_{j}
, and
P_{j}
commute with
\mathcal{H}
, it follows that

J_{2}\iota_{2}^{-1}[B,\mathcal{H}]\iota_{1}P_{1}=[J_{2}\iota_{2}^{-1}B\iota_{1}P_{1},\mathcal{H}]=[\tilde{B},\mathcal{H}].

Thus, we obtain

(9.5)

\log N\lesssim\|[B,\mathcal{H}]\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R};H)}\leq 2\|[\tilde{B},\mathcal{H}]\|_{\mathscr{L}(L^{p}(\mathbb{R};\mathfrak{X}_{1}),L^{p}(\mathbb{R};\mathfrak{X}_{2}))}.

From (
9.3
), (
9.4
), and (
9.5
), it follows that
B_{k}:=\frac{1}{2C}\tilde{B}
has the required properties, as soon as
N=\dim G_{1}=\dim G_{2}
is chosen large enough, as we may by Corollary
9.8
.
∎

We next wish to combine together the different functions
B_{k}
. For this, we will use the following construction. Given a sequence of integrable functions
\{b_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}
, where each
b_{k}
is defined (at least) on
[4k,4k+1]
, we define a new function
b
on
\mathbb{R}
as follows, where
k\in\mathbb{Z}
and
j\geq 0
are arbitrary and
I_{j}:=[2^{-j-1},2^{-j})
:

(9.6)

b(x):=\begin{cases}b_{k}(x),&x\in 4k+[0,1],\\
\langle b_{k}\rangle_{4k+I_{j}},&x\in 4k-I_{j},\\
\langle b_{k}\rangle_{4k+1-I_{j}},&x\in 4k+1+I_{j},\\
0,&x\in 4k+[2,3].\end{cases}

Lemma 9.10
.

Equation (
9.6
) defines
b
uniquely at every
x\in\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

Clearly, the first case of (
9.6
) defines
b
on
[4k,4k+1]
, and the last one on
[4k+2,4k+3]
.
Noting that
\bigcup_{j\geq 0}I_{j}=(0,1)
, the second case defines
b
on
(4k-1,4k)
, the third one on
(4k+1,4k+2)
.
As
k\in\mathbb{Z}
varies, this uniquely defines
b
at every point
x\in\mathbb{R}
.
∎

Note that the values of
b
on
(4k-1,4k)
and
(4k+1,4k+2)
are Whitney-type extensions of
b_{k}
over the boundary points
4k
and
4k+1
, respectively. While definition (
9.6
) can be made for any integrable functions
b_{k}
, it is particularly useful under the additional assumption that
\langle b_{k}\rangle_{[4k,4k+1]}=0
, in which case the definition of
b
on
[4k+2,4k+3]
is not as arbitrary as it might otherwise seem.

Lemma 9.11
.

For any
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, let
J_{k}:=[4k,4k+1]
and let
b_{k}\in\operatorname{BMO}(J_{k};\mathfrak{X})
satisfy
\langle b_{k}\rangle_{J_{k}}=0
and
\|b_{k}\|_{\operatorname{BMO}(J_{k};\mathfrak{X})}\leq 1
. Let
b
be a function on
\mathbb{R}
defined by (
9.6
). Then
b\in\operatorname{BMO}(\mathbb{R};\mathfrak{X})
and
\|b\|_{\operatorname{BMO}(\mathbb{R};\mathfrak{X})}\lesssim 1

with the implicit positive constant independent of
b
.

Proof.

We need to estimate
\inf_{c\in\mathfrak{X}}\fint_{I}\|b(x)-c\|\,dx
for all finite intervals
I\subset\mathbb{R}
. We split this into several cases.

Case
I\subseteq[4k,4k+1]
for some
k\in\mathbb{Z}
is clear, since this is just the condition that
\|b_{k}\|_{\operatorname{BMO}(I_{k};\mathfrak{X})}\leq 1
.

Case
I\subseteq 4k-[2^{-j-1},2^{-j}]
for some
k\in\mathbb{Z}
and
j\geq-1
is also clear, as
b_{k}
is essentially constant on
I
. Note that we intentionally include the case
j=-1
here, i.e.,
I\subseteq[4k-2,4k-1]=[4(k-1)+2,4(k-1)+3]
, in which case
b=0
on
I
.

Case:
I\subseteq(4k-2,4k)
meets exactly two intervals
4k-I_{j}
, where
j\geq-1
(again, intentionally starting from this value). Let
k=0
(without loss of generality), and
j=i,i+1
. To simplify writing, in the special case
j=-1
, we abuse notation and define
\langle b_{0}\rangle_{I_{-1}}:=0
. (Recall that
I_{-1}=[1,2)
, but
b_{0}
is only defined on
[0,1]
, so this abuse should not cause any ambiguity.) With this convention, let
c=\langle b_{0}\rangle_{I_{i}}
. Then

\begin{split}\fint_{I}\|b-c\|&=\frac{1}{|I|}\sum_{j=i}^{i+1}|I\cap(-I_{j})|\|\langle b_{0}\rangle_{I_{j}}-c\|\\
&=\frac{|I\cap(-I_{j+1})|}{|I|}\|\langle b_{0}\rangle_{I_{i+1}}-\langle b_{0}\rangle_{I_{i}}\|\lesssim 1\cdot\|b_{0}\|_{\operatorname{BMO}(J_{0};\mathfrak{X})}\leq 1\end{split}

by a well-known estimate for averages over close-by, comparable intervals.

Case:
I\subseteq(4k-2,4k)
meets more than two intervals
4k-I_{j}
with
j\geq-1
. Let again
k=0
, and let
i
be the minimal index such that
I
meets
-I_{i}
. Then

[2^{-i-2},2^{-i-1})=-I_{i+1}\subseteq I\subseteq\bigcup_{j=i}^{\infty}(-I_{j})=-(0,2^{-i})=:-\hat{I}_{i}.

Thus
|I|\geq|I_{i+1}|=\frac{1}{4}|\hat{I}_{i}|
.

If
i=-1
, we again abuse notation and define
\langle b_{0}\rangle_{\hat{I}_{-1}}:=0
, keeping also the earlier abuse
\langle b_{0}\rangle_{I_{-1}}:=0
.
Then, with
c=\langle b_{0}\rangle_{\hat{I}_{i}}
, it follows that

\fint_{I}\|b-c\|\leq 4\fint_{-\hat{I}_{i}}\|b-c\|=\frac{4}{|\hat{I}_{i}|}\sum_{j=i}^{\infty}|I_{j}|\|\langle b_{0}\rangle_{I_{j}}-c\|=:A_{i}.

By the conventions that we made, if
i=-1
, then
\|\langle b_{0}\rangle_{I_{-1}}-c\|=\|0-0\|=0
, and hence the term
j=-1
does not contribute. Moreover, we have
|\hat{I}_{-1}|\geq|\hat{I}_{0}|
. Hence
A_{-1}\leq A_{0}
, and so it suffices to estimate
A_{i}
with
i\geq 0
. In this case, all
\langle b_{0}\rangle_{I_{j}}
have their usual meaning and
\hat{I}_{i}\subseteq[0,1]
; thus, we can estimate

A_{i}\leq\frac{4}{|\hat{I}_{i}|}\sum_{j=i}^{\infty}|I_{j}|\fint_{I_{j}}\|b_{0}-c\|=4\fint_{\hat{I}_{i}}\|b_{0}-c\|\leq 4\|b_{0}\|_{\operatorname{BMO}(J_{0};\mathfrak{X})}\leq 4.

Thus we have verified the case under consideration.

Case
I\subseteq[4k-2,4k]
is now covered by the previous three cases.

Case
I\subseteq[4k+1,4k+3]
is symmetric.

Case:
I\subseteq[4k-1,4k+1]
. We may assume that
I
meets both
(4k-1,4k)
and
[4k,4k+1]
, since otherwise this is already covered. Let
k=0
(without loss of generality). Let
\tilde{I}:=I\cup(-I)
so that
I\subseteq\tilde{I}
and
|\tilde{I}|\leq|I|+|-I|=2|I|
. Let
i
be the smallest index such that
\tilde{I}
meets
I_{i}
. Then

\tilde{I}_{i+1}=[-2^{-i-1},2^{-i-1}]\subseteq\tilde{I}\subseteq[-2^{-i},2^{-i}]=:\tilde{I}_{i},

and hence
|\tilde{I}_{i}|=2|\tilde{I}_{i+1}|\leq 2|\tilde{I}|\leq 4|I|
. Hence, with
c=\langle b_{0}\rangle_{[0,2^{-i}]}
,

\begin{split}\fint_{I}\|b-c\|&\leq 4\fint_{\tilde{I}_{i}}\|b-c\|\\
&=\frac{4}{|\tilde{I}_{i}|}\Big(\int_{0}^{2^{-i}}\|b_{0}-c\|+\sum_{j=i}^{\infty}|I_{j}|\|\langle b_{0}\rangle_{I_{j}}-c\|\Big)\\
&\leq\frac{4}{|\tilde{I}_{i}|}\Big(\int_{0}^{2^{-i}}\|b_{0}-c\|+\sum_{j=i}^{\infty}|I_{j}|\fint_{I_{j}}\|b_{0}-c\|\Big)\\
&=\frac{8}{|\tilde{I}_{i}|}\int_{0}^{2^{-i}}\|b_{0}-c\|=4\fint_{0}^{2^{-i}}\|b_{0}-c\|\\
&\leq 4\|b_{0}\|_{\operatorname{BMO}(J_{0};\mathfrak{X})}\leq 4.\end{split}

Case
I\subseteq[4k,4k+2]
is symmetric to the previous one.

Since each
h\in\mathbb{Z}
has one of the forms
4k-2
,
4k-1
,
4k
, and
4k+1
, we have now covered all cases, where
I
is contained in an interval of the form
[h,h+2]
for some
h\in\mathbb{Z}
. Clearly, every interval of length
|I|\leq 1
is of this type. (If
h\leq\inf I<h+1
, then
I\subset[h,h+2]
.) Thus, it only remains to consider:

Case:
|I|>1
. Let
m:=\lfloor\inf I\rfloor
,
n:=\lceil\sup I\rceil
, and
J:=[m,n]
. Then
|J|\leq|I|+2\leq 3|I|
. With
c=0
, we have

\fint_{I}\|b-c\|\leq 3\fint_{J}\|b\|=\frac{3}{|J|}\sum_{i=m}^{n-1}\int_{i}^{i+1}\|b\|.

Recall that each
b_{k}
has
\langle b_{k}\rangle_{[4k,4k+1]}=0
by assumption, and then
b
has
\langle b\rangle_{[i,i+1]}=0
for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
by its construction (
9.6
). Hence

\int_{i}^{i+1}\|b\|=\fint_{[i,i+1]}\|b-\langle b\rangle_{[i,i+1]}\|\leq 2\inf_{c\in\mathfrak{X}}\fint_{[i,i+1]}\|b-c\|\lesssim 1

by the cases of short intervals that we have already handled. Substituting back, it follows that

\fint_{I}\|b-c\|\lesssim\frac{1}{|J|}\sum_{i=m}^{n-1}1=\frac{1}{|J|}(n-m)=1,

recalling that
J=[m,n]
.

We have now completed the proof of the lemma.
∎

We are now ready to present the function that proves Theorem
9.1
for any
K
-convex space
\mathfrak{X}
.

Proposition 9.12
.

For
j=1,2
, let
\mathfrak{X}_{j}
be infinite-dimensional
K
-convex Banach spaces. Then there exists a function

B\in\operatorname{BMO}_{\operatorname{so}}(\mathbb{R};\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X}_{1},\mathfrak{X}_{2}))

with
B^{*}\in\operatorname{BMO}_{\operatorname{so}}(\mathbb{R};\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X}_{2}^{*},\mathfrak{X}_{1}^{*}))
such that
[B,\mathcal{H}]
is not bounded from
L^{p}(\mathbb{R};\mathfrak{X}_{1})
to
L^{p}(\mathbb{R};\mathfrak{X}_{2})
.

Proof.

Let
B_{k}\in\operatorname{BMO}_{\operatorname{so}}(\mathbb{R};\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X}_{1},\mathfrak{X}_{2}))
be the functions provided by Lemma
9.9
. By the estimate
\|[B_{k},\mathcal{H}]\|_{\mathscr{L}(L^{p}(\mathbb{R};\mathfrak{X}_{1}),L^{p}(\mathbb{R};\mathfrak{X}_{2}))}>k
, we can find compactly supported
f_{k}\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R};\mathfrak{X}_{1})
and
g_{k}\in L^{p^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R};\mathfrak{X}_{2}^{*})
such that

\|f_{k}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R};\mathfrak{X}_{1})}=\|g_{k}\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R};\mathfrak{X}_{2}^{*})}=1

and

|\langle[B_{k},\mathcal{H}]f_{k},g_{k}\rangle|>k.

By the translation and the dilation invariances of the Hilbert transform, it follows that

\langle[B_{k},\mathcal{H}]f_{k},g_{k}\rangle=\langle[\tilde{B}_{k},\mathcal{H}]\tilde{f}_{k},\tilde{g}_{k}\rangle,

where

\tilde{f}_{k}(x):=r_{k}^{-\frac{1}{p}}f_{k}\Big(\frac{x-x_{k}}{r_{k}}\Big),\quad\tilde{g}_{k}(x):=r_{k}^{-\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}}g_{k}\Big(\frac{x-x_{k}}{r_{k}}\Big),

\tilde{B}_{k}(x):=B_{k}\Big(\frac{x-x_{k}}{r_{k}}\Big),

and the
x_{k}
and
r_{k}
are arbitrary. These satisfy
\|\tilde{f}_{k}\|_{p}=\|f_{k}\|_{p}
and
\|\tilde{g}_{k}\|_{p^{\prime}}=\|g_{k}\|_{p^{\prime}}
, and also
\tilde{B}_{k}
and
\tilde{B}_{k}^{*}
have the same
\operatorname{BMO}_{\operatorname{so}}
norms as
B_{k}
and
B_{k}^{*}
, respectively.

Let
x_{k}:=4k+\frac{1}{2}
. Since
f_{k}
and
g_{k}
are compactly supported, a sufficiently small choice of
r_{k}
guarantees that
\tilde{f}_{k}
and
\tilde{g}_{k}
are supported in
[4k,4k+1]
. After fixing these
x_{k}
and
r_{k}
, we will finally replace
\tilde{B}_{k}
by
\tilde{B}_{k}-\langle\tilde{B}_{k}\rangle_{[4k,4k+1]}
, noting that this affects neither the
\operatorname{BMO}_{\operatorname{so}}
norms nor the commutator
[\tilde{B}_{k},\mathcal{H}]
. Thus, without loss of generality, we may assume that

(9.7)

\langle\tilde{B}_{k}\rangle_{[4k,4k+1]}=0.

We then define a function
B
by applying the algorithm (
9.6
) to the functions
\tilde{B}_{k}|_{[4k,4k+1]}
, where we may define simply
\tilde{B}_{k}:=0
for
k\leq 0
. Since the algorithm (
9.6
) is linear, it is clear that, for each
\mathfrak{e}\in\mathfrak{X}_{1}
, the function
B(\cdot)\mathfrak{e}
is the result of the same algorithm applied to the functions
\tilde{B}_{k}(\cdot)\mathfrak{e}|_{[4k,4k+1]}
, which satisfy

\|\tilde{B}_{k}(\cdot)\mathfrak{e}\|_{\operatorname{BMO}([4k,4k+1];\mathfrak{X}_{2})}\leq\|\tilde{B}_{k}(\cdot)\mathfrak{e}\|_{\operatorname{BMO}_{\operatorname{so}}(\mathbb{R};\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X}_{2}))}\|\mathfrak{e}\|_{\mathfrak{X}_{1}}\leq 1

for
\|\mathfrak{e}\|_{\mathfrak{X}_{1}}\leq 1
. Hence Lemma
9.11
(here we need (
9.7
)) guarantees that
\|B(\cdot)\mathfrak{e}\|_{\operatorname{BMO}(\mathbb{R};\mathfrak{X}_{2})}\lesssim 1

for the same
\mathfrak{e}
, and thus
\|B\|_{\operatorname{BMO}_{\operatorname{so}}(\mathbb{R};\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X}_{1},\mathfrak{X}_{2}))}\lesssim 1
. Similarly, we also obtain

\|B^{*}\|_{\operatorname{BMO}_{\operatorname{so}}(\mathbb{R};\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X}_{2}^{*},\mathfrak{X}_{1}^{*}))}\lesssim 1.

It remains to check the unboundedness of
[B,\mathcal{H}]
. Testing with
\tilde{f}_{k},\tilde{g}_{k}
from above, we find that

\begin{split}\|[B,\mathcal{H}]\|_{\mathscr{L}(L^{p}(\mathbb{R};\mathfrak{X}_{1}),L^{p}(\mathbb{R};\mathfrak{X}_{2}))}&\geq|\langle[B,\mathcal{H}]\tilde{f}_{k},\tilde{g}_{k}\rangle|\\
&=|\langle[\tilde{B}_{k},\mathcal{H}]\tilde{f}_{k},\tilde{g}_{k}\rangle|>k,\end{split}

where the identity follows from the fact that
\tilde{f}_{k}
and
\tilde{g}_{k}
are supported in
[4k,4k+1]
, where
B=B_{k}
by construction. Since the previous estimate is valid for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we see that
[B,\mathcal{H}]
cannot be bounded.
∎

Proof of Theorem
9.1
.

If
\mathfrak{X}
is non-UMD (resp.
K
-convex), then the required
B
is produced by Proposition
9.2
(resp.
9.12
, taking
\mathfrak{X}_{1}=\mathfrak{X}_{2}=\mathfrak{X}
). Since every UMD space is
K
-convex (see
[
39
, Proposition 4.3.10]
), these two cases cover all Banach spaces.
∎

10.
Unbounded Hilbert transforms

The main result of this section is the following theorem, which shows the insufficiency of the
\mathscr{A}_{p}
condition to guarantee the boundedness of the Hilbert transform on the operator-weighed
L^{p}(V;\mathfrak{X})
space, whenever
p\in(1,\infty)
and
\mathfrak{X}
is an arbitrary Banach space. In the special case that
p=2
and
\mathfrak{X}
is an infinite-dimensional Hilbert space, this is contained in
[
32
, Theorem 1.3]
or
[
34
, Theorem 1.1]
. Formally, these results show the existence of a sequence of finite-dimensional weights, whose
\mathscr{A}_{2}
constants remain bounded while the norms of the weighted Hilbert transforms grow without limit as the dimension increases to infinity. However, since an infinite-dimensional Hilbert space can be written as an
\ell^{2}
direct sum containing all the finite-dimensional versions, the infinite-dimensional counterexample readily follows.

Theorem 10.1
.

Let
p\in(1,\infty)
and
\mathfrak{X}
be an arbitrary Banach space.
Then there exists
V\in\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R};\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X}))
such that the Hilbert transform is unbounded on
L^{p}(V;\mathfrak{X})
.

As in Theorem
9.1
, we will consider two cases for the Banach space
\mathfrak{X}
. However, this time the case that
\mathfrak{X}
is not UMD is entirely trivial: in this case, the Hilbert transform is unbounded on
L^{p}(\mathbb{R};\mathfrak{X})
, and hence we can simply take
V\equiv I
, the identity operator on
\mathfrak{X}
.

In the case that
\mathfrak{X}
is
K
-convex, the proof of Theorem
10.1
will make use of the constructions in the proof of Theorem
9.1
through a close link of the two types of (un)boundedness results that goes back to
[
33
]
, and will be reviewed shortly. However, while the essence of this link remains in general Banach spaces, there is a devil in the details, which prevents a soft deduction of Theorem
10.1
from Theorem
9.1
that one can do in a Hilbert space (and more generally, but not always), and seems to necessitate revisiting also several details of the proof.

The following lemma gives the basic construction of
\mathscr{A}_{p}
weights from BMO functions. The case that
p=2
and
\mathfrak{X}_{1}=\mathfrak{X}_{2}
is a Hilbert space goes back to
[
33
, Lemma 2.1]
. To streamline the statement of the lemma, we introduce the
L^{p}
seminorm modulo constant functions,

\|b\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}_{0}(Q;\mathfrak{X})}:=\Big(\fint_{Q}\|b(x)-\langle b\rangle_{Q}\|_{\mathfrak{X}}^{p}\,dx\Big)^{\frac{1}{p}},

and its strong-operator version

\|B\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}_{0,\operatorname{so}}(Q,\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X}_{1},\mathfrak{X}_{2}))}:=\sup_{\|\mathfrak{e}_{1}\|_{\mathfrak{X}_{1}}\leq 1}\|B(\cdot)\mathfrak{e}_{1}\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}_{0}(Q;\mathfrak{X}_{2})}.

Lemma 10.2
.

Let
p\in(1,\infty)
. For
j=1,2
, let
\mathfrak{X}_{j}
be Banach spaces and

B\in L^{p}_{\operatorname{so}}(Q;\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X}_{1},\mathfrak{X}_{2}))

be a function whose pointwise adjoint satisfies
B^{*}\in L^{p^{\prime}}_{\operatorname{so}}(Q;\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X}_{2}^{*},\mathfrak{X}_{1}^{*}))
. Let
\mathfrak{X}:=\mathfrak{X}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{X}_{2}
, and consider the
\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X})
-valued weight

(10.1)

V(x)=\begin{pmatrix}I&0\\
B(x)&I\end{pmatrix}.

Then, for all
\mathfrak{e}^{*}\in\mathfrak{X}^{*}
and
\mathfrak{e}\in\mathfrak{X}
, the following bounds are valid:

(10.2)

\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(Q,V^{-*})}(\mathfrak{e}^{*})\leq(1+\|B^{*}\|_{\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}_{\operatorname{so}}(Q;\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X}_{2}^{*},\mathfrak{X}_{1}^{*}))})\|\mathfrak{e}^{*}\|_{\mathfrak{X}^{*}},

(10.3)

\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,V)}(\mathfrak{e})\leq(1+\|B\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}_{\operatorname{so}}(Q,\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X}_{1},\mathfrak{X}_{2}))})\|\mathfrak{e}\|_{\mathfrak{X}},

and

(10.4)

\frac{\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(Q,V^{-*})}(\mathfrak{e}^{*})}{\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,V)}^{*}(\mathfrak{e}^{*})}\leq 1+\|B\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}_{0,\operatorname{so}}(Q;\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X}_{1},\mathfrak{X}_{2}))}+\|B^{*}\|_{\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}_{0,\operatorname{so}}(Q;\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X}_{2}^{*},\mathfrak{X}_{1}^{*}))}.

In particular, if

B\in\operatorname{BMO}_{\operatorname{so}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X}_{1},\mathfrak{X}_{2})),\quad B^{*}\in\operatorname{BMO}_{\operatorname{so}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X}_{2}^{*},\mathfrak{X}_{1}^{*}))

then
V\in\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X}))
and

[V]_{\mathscr{A}_{p}}\lesssim 1+\|B\|_{\operatorname{BMO}_{\operatorname{so}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X}_{1},\mathfrak{X}_{2}))}+\|B^{*}\|_{\operatorname{BMO}_{\operatorname{so}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X}_{2}^{*},\mathfrak{X}_{1}^{*}))}.

Note that (
10.2
) and (
10.3
) have
\textit{\L}^{p}
norms, while (
10.4
) has
\textit{\L}^{p}_{0}
.

Proof.

Note that the last assertion concerning
\mathscr{A}_{p}
and
\operatorname{BMO}
is immediate from (
10.4
), the relevant definitions, and the John–Nirenberg inequality. Thus we concentrate on proving (
10.2
), (
10.3
), and (
10.4
).

For the matrix weight
V
as defined, it is immediate to compute

V^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}I&0\\
-B&I\end{pmatrix},\qquad V^{-*}=\begin{pmatrix}I&-B^{*}\\
0&I\end{pmatrix}.

The assumptions on
B
and
B^{*}
imply in particular the qualitative conditions

V\in L^{p}_{\operatorname{so}}(Q;\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X})),\qquad V^{-*}\in L^{p^{\prime}}_{\operatorname{so}}(Q;\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X}^{*})),

so that the left-hand side of (
10.4
) is well defined, as well as

(10.5)

V^{-1}\in L^{p}_{\operatorname{so}}(Q;\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X})),

which will be used shortly.

The bound (
10.2
) follows simply by taking the
\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(Q)
norms of

\|V^{-*}(x)\mathfrak{e}^{*}\|_{\mathfrak{X}^{*}}\leq\|\mathfrak{e}^{*}\|_{\mathfrak{X}^{*}}+\|B^{*}(x)\mathfrak{e}_{2}^{*}\|_{\mathfrak{X}_{1}^{*}},

and noting that
\|\mathfrak{e}_{2}^{*}\|_{\mathfrak{X}_{2}^{*}}\leq\|\mathfrak{e}^{*}\|_{\mathfrak{X}^{*}}
.
Similarly, (
10.3
) is obtained by taking the
\textit{\L}^{p}(Q)
norms

\|V(x)\mathfrak{e}\|_{\mathfrak{X}}\leq\|\mathfrak{e}\|_{\mathfrak{X}}+\|B(x)\mathfrak{e}_{1}\|_{\mathfrak{X}_{2}}.

Thus (
10.2
) and (
10.3
) are proved, and we turn to the proof of (
10.4
).

The numerator of (
10.4
) can be expressed as

\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(Q,V^{-*})}(\mathfrak{e}^{*})=\Big(\fint_{Q}\|V^{-*}(\cdot)\mathfrak{e}^{*}\|^{p^{\prime}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}}=\sup_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{\Norm{f}{\aveL^p(Q;\fX)}\leq 1}{f\text{ simple}}}\Big|\fint_{Q}\langle V^{-*}(\cdot)\mathfrak{e}^{*},f\rangle\Big|.

Thanks to (
10.5
), the integral on the right-hand side may be written as

\fint_{Q}\langle V^{-*}(\cdot)\mathfrak{e}^{*},f\rangle=\fint_{Q}\langle\mathfrak{e}^{*},V^{-1}f\rangle=\Big\langle\mathfrak{e}^{*},\fint_{Q}V^{-1}f\Big\rangle=:\langle\mathfrak{e}^{*},\mathfrak{e}_{f}\rangle.

Hence, by the definition of the dual norm,

\Big|\fint_{Q}\langle V^{-*}(\cdot)\mathfrak{e}^{*},f\rangle\Big|\leq\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,V)}^{*}(\mathfrak{e}^{*})\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,V)}(\mathfrak{e}_{f}),

where

\begin{split}\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,V)}(\mathfrak{e}_{f})&=\Big(\fint_{Q}\|V(x)\mathfrak{e}_{f}\|^{p}\,dx\Big)^{\frac{1}{p}}\\
&=\Big(\fint_{Q}\|V(x)\fint_{Q}V(y)^{-1}f(y)dy\|^{p}\,dx\Big)^{\frac{1}{p}}\\
&=\sup_{\|g\|_{\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(Q;\mathfrak{X}^{*})}\leq 1}\Big|\fint_{Q}\fint_{Q}\langle V(x)V(y)^{-1}f(y),g(x)\rangle\,dy\,dx\Big|.\end{split}

For
V
as in the assumptions, we have

\begin{split}V(x)V(y)^{-1}&=\begin{pmatrix}I&0\\
B(x)&I\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}I&0\\
-B(y)&I\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}I&0\\
B(x)-B(y)&I\end{pmatrix}\\
&=\begin{pmatrix}I&0\\
0&I\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
B(x)-\langle B\rangle_{Q}&0\end{pmatrix}-\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
B(y)-\langle B\rangle_{Q}&0\end{pmatrix}.\end{split}

Hence

\begin{split}\fint_{Q}&\fint_{Q}\langle V(x)V(y)^{-1}f(y),g(x)\rangle\,dy\,dx\\
&=\fint_{Q}\fint_{Q}\langle f(y),g(x)\rangle\,dy\,dx+\fint_{Q}\fint_{Q}\langle(B(x)-\langle B\rangle_{Q})f_{1}(y),g_{2}(x)\rangle\,dy\,dx\\
&\qquad-\fint_{Q}\fint_{Q}\langle f_{1}(y),(B^{*}(y)-\langle B^{*}\rangle_{Q})g_{2}(x)\rangle\,dy\,dx\\
&=:{\rm I}+{\rm II}-{\rm III},\end{split}

where
f=(f_{1},f_{2})
and
g=(g_{1},g_{2})
. We can estimate

\begin{split}|\rm II|&\leq\fint_{Q}\Big(\fint_{Q}\|[B(x)-\langle B\rangle_{Q}]f_{1}(y)\|_{\mathfrak{X}_{2}}^{p}\,dx\Big)^{\frac{1}{p}}\|g_{2}\|_{\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(Q;\mathfrak{X}_{2}^{*})}\,dy\\
&\leq\fint_{Q}\|B\|_{\textit{\L}_{0,\operatorname{so}}^{p}(Q;\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X}_{1},\mathfrak{X}_{2}))}\|f_{1}(y)\|_{\mathfrak{X}_{1}}\|g_{2}\|_{\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(Q;\mathfrak{X}_{2}^{*})}\,dy\\
&=\|B\|_{\textit{\L}_{0,\operatorname{so}}^{p}(Q;\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X}_{1},\mathfrak{X}_{2}))}\|f_{1}\|_{\textit{\L}^{1}(Q;\mathfrak{X}_{1})}\|g_{2}\|_{\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(Q;\mathfrak{X}_{2}^{*})}.\end{split}

Similarly,

|\mathrm{III}|\leq\|B^{*}\|_{\textit{\L}_{0,\operatorname{so}}^{p^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X}_{2}^{*},\mathfrak{X}_{1}^{*}))}\|f_{1}\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q;\mathfrak{X}_{1})}\|g_{2}\|_{\textit{\L}^{1}(Q;\mathfrak{X}_{2}^{*})}

and of course

|\mathrm{I}|\leq\|f\|_{\textit{\L}^{1}(Q;\mathfrak{X})}\|g\|_{\textit{\L}^{1}(Q;\mathfrak{X}^{*})}.

Substituting back and using Hölder’s inequality, we find that

\begin{split}\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,V)}(\mathfrak{e}_{f})&\leq(1+\|B\|_{\textit{\L}_{0,\operatorname{so}}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X}_{1},\mathfrak{X}_{2}))}+\|B^{*}\|_{\textit{\L}_{0,\operatorname{so}}^{p^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X}_{2}^{*}\mathfrak{X}_{1}^{*}))})\|f\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q;\mathfrak{X})}\|g\|_{\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(Q;\mathfrak{X}^{*})},\end{split}

where the last factor is bounded by
1
by the conditions on
f
and
g
. Substituting further back, this proves the claimed bound (
10.4
).
∎

Remark 10.3
.

Before proceeding with the proof of Theorem
10.1
for a general
K
-convex Banach space
\mathfrak{X}
,
let us indicate an easier argument under an additional assumption that the Banach space
\mathfrak{X}
is
decomposable
, meaning that it admits a representation
\mathfrak{X}=\mathfrak{X}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{X}_{2}
as a direct sum of two
infinite-dimensional
Banach spaces
\{\mathfrak{X}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{2}
. Let
B\in\operatorname{BMO}_{\operatorname{so}}(\mathbb{R};\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X}_{1},\mathfrak{X}_{2}))
with
B^{*}\in\operatorname{BMO}_{\operatorname{so}}(\mathbb{R};\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X}_{2}^{*},\mathfrak{X}_{1}^{*}))
be a function, provided by Proposition
9.12
, such that
[B,\mathcal{H}]
is not bounded from
L^{p}(\mathbb{R};\mathfrak{X}_{1})
to
L^{p}(\mathbb{R};\mathfrak{X}_{2})
. Let
V:\mathbb{R}\to\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X})
be defined by (
10.1
); thus
V\in\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R};\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X}))
by Lemma
10.2
.

Note that
\mathcal{H}
is bounded on
L^{p}(V;\mathfrak{X})
if and only if
V\mathcal{H}V^{-1}
is bounded on
L^{p}(\mathbb{R};\mathfrak{X})
. Relative to the direct sum splitting
\mathfrak{X}=\mathfrak{X}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{X}_{2}
and the induced splitting
L^{p}(\mathbb{R};\mathfrak{X})=L^{p}(\mathbb{R};\mathfrak{X}_{1})\oplus L^{p}(\mathbb{R};\mathfrak{X}_{2})
, this latter operator is given by

V\mathcal{H}V^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}I&0\\
B&I\end{pmatrix}\mathcal{H}\begin{pmatrix}I&0\\
-B&I\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathcal{H}&0\\
[B,\mathcal{H}]&\mathcal{H}\end{pmatrix}.

Thus, a necessary condition for the boundedness of
\mathcal{H}:L^{p}(V;\mathfrak{X})\to L^{p}(V;\mathfrak{X})
is the boundedness of
[B,\mathcal{H}]:L^{p}(\mathbb{R};\mathfrak{X}_{1})\to L^{p}(\mathbb{R};\mathfrak{X}_{2})
, which was specifically excluded by Proposition
9.12
. Hence, the proof of Theorem
10.1
can be completed in this soft way in the special case of
decomposable
Banach spaces.

While this property holds for most classical Banach spaces, it does not hold in general. Indeed, there even exist
hereditarily indecomposable
(H.I.) Banach spaces
\mathfrak{X}
, meaning that not only
\mathfrak{X}
itself but all of its infinite-dimensional closed subspaces are indecomposable (i.e., not decomposable). The first such example is due to Gowers and Maurey
[
36
]
, and others have been found since then. Notably, Ferenczi
[
22
]
has constructed an H.I. space
\mathfrak{X}
that is
uniformly convex
and hence in particular
K
-convex. (See the end of this Remark for this implication.) Thus, the above construction of
V
is not possible in arbitrary
K
-convex spaces, and not even in the smaller class of uniformly convex spaces.

To complete the proof of Theorem
10.1
, we would actually only need this construction in the still smaller class of UMD spaces. Whether every UMD space is decomposable appears to be open. If this turns out to be the case, then a simpler proof of Theorem
10.1
will be available, and we completed it already. However, in the lack of knowing such a result, we adopt a different approach below, which does not require decomposability.

The idea is the following: Instead of using the finite-dimensional functions
B_{k}
to build an infinite-dimensional
B
and then using this to build the weight
V
by Lemma
10.2
(as we did above, assuming decomposability), we use each finite-dimensional function
B_{k}
to build a finite-dimensional weight
V_{k}
by Lemma
10.2
, and then we use the finite-dimensional weights
V_{k}
to build an infinite-dimensional weight
V
in a similar way as the functions
B_{k}
were used to build
B
. This turns out to work, as we will see below.

[The implication from uniform convexity to
K
-convexity, which we mentioned above, is well known, but seems difficult to quote as a single result. It can be obtained, e.g., from the following chain: if
\mathfrak{X}
is uniformly convex, then
\mathfrak{X}^{*}
is
uniformly smooth
(by
[
57
, Proposition 10.21]
), thus
