where the implicit positive constant is independent of
f
.

Remark 15.8
.

The converse inequality

\|f\|_{\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho,\varphi)}\leq\|f\|_{\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho,\sup,\varphi)}

is trivial without any assumptions on
\rho
.

Proof of Proposition
15.7
.

Let
x,y\in Q\in\mathscr{D}_{j}
. We apply Lemma
13.3

with the
u
-norm
\rho_{Q}
. Since every
u
-norm is also a
\tilde{u}
-norm for all
\tilde{u}\in(0,u)
, we assume that
u\in(0,\min\{p,q\})
to begin with. (This is only needed later in the proof.) This gives

\rho_{Q}(\varphi_{j}*f(x))^{u}\lesssim\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\phi_{j}^{\prime}(x-z)\rho_{Q}(\varphi_{j}*f(z))^{u}\,dz,

where
\phi_{j}^{\prime}(z)=(1+|z|)^{-M^{\prime}}
, and
M^{\prime}>0
can be taken as large as we like. Moreover,
by (
14.5
), we obtain

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\phi_{j}^{\prime}(x-z)\rho_{Q}(\varphi_{j}*f(z))^{u}\,dz\lesssim\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\phi_{j}(y-z)\rho_{\mathscr{D}_{j}}(\varphi*f)(z)^{u}\,dz,

where
\phi(z):=\phi^{\prime}(z)(1+|z|)^{Nu}=(1+|z|)^{-M}
, and
M=M^{\prime}-Nu
can still be chosen as large as we like by taking
M^{\prime}
large enough. Since the previous bound holds for all
x,y\in Q\in\mathscr{D}_{j}
, the claim (
15.4
) follows.

For the second claim, we multiply both sides of (
15.4
) by
2^{js}
and take the
(L^{p}\ell^{q})_{A}
quasi-norms of both sides. This gives

\begin{split}\|f\|_{\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho,\sup,\varphi)}&=\Big\|\Big\{2^{js}\|\rho_{\mathscr{D}_{j}}(\varphi_{j}*f)\|_{\textit{\L}^{\infty}(\mathscr{D}_{j})}\Big\}\Big\|_{(L^{p}\ell^{q})_{A}}\lesssim\Big\|\Big\{2^{js}\big(\phi_{j}*\rho_{\mathscr{D}_{j}}(\varphi_{j}*f)^{u}\big)^{\frac{1}{u}}\Big\}\Big\|_{(L^{p}\ell^{q})_{A}}\\
&=\Big\|\Big\{2^{jsu}\phi_{j}*\rho_{\mathscr{D}_{j}}(\varphi_{j}*f)^{u}\Big\}\Big\|_{(L^{\frac{p}{u}}\ell^{\frac{q}{u}})_{A}}^{\frac{1}{u}}\lesssim\Big\|\Big\{2^{jsu}\rho_{\mathscr{D}_{j}}(\varphi_{j}*f)^{u}\Big\}\Big\|_{(L^{\frac{p}{u}}\ell^{\frac{q}{u}})_{A}}^{\frac{1}{u}}\\
&=\Big\|\Big\{2^{js}\rho_{\mathscr{D}_{j}}(\varphi_{j}*f)\Big\}\Big\|_{(L^{p}\ell^{q})_{A}}=\|f\|_{\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho,\varphi)},\end{split}

where the last inequality follows from the Fefferman–Stein vector-valued maximal inequality, noting that
|\phi_{j}*f|\lesssim Mf
and
\frac{p}{u},\frac{q}{u}>1
.
∎

Lemma 15.9
.

For
\varphi,\psi\in\mathscr{S}_{\infty}
, we have the estimates

|\varphi_{i}*\psi_{j}(x)|\lesssim 2^{-|i-j|M}\phi_{\min(i,j)}(x),

where
\phi(x):=(1+|x|)^{-M}
,
\phi_{i}(x)=2^{in}\phi(2^{i}x)
,
and the implicit positive constant is independent of
i
,
j
, and
x
, and

|\langle\varphi_{Q},\psi_{R}\rangle|\lesssim B_{-M}(Q,R):=B_{-M,-M,-M}(Q,R),

where the right hand side is defined in (
6.6
)
and the implicit positive constant is independent of
Q
and
R
.
In each case,
M>0
can be taken as large as we like.

Proof.

The first estimate is
[
69
, Lemma 2.2]
, which is also reproduced as
[
10
, Lemma 3.31]
, and the second one is
[
10
, Corollary 3.32]
. In the cited sources, more precise forms of the upper bound are given; we have omitted the details that are irrelevant to us.
∎

Lemma 15.10
.

Suppose that
\rho=\{\rho_{Q}\}_{Q\in\mathscr{D}}
is a family of quasi-norms on
\mathfrak{X}
, each qualitatively equivalent to
\|\ \|_{\mathfrak{X}}
, and that (
15.2
) holds for some
a,b,c\geq 0
. Then for all
t\in\dot{a}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho)
, the series
T_{\psi}t
as in (
15.1
) converges in
\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})
.

Proof.

The claim means that the series

\sum_{R\in\mathscr{D}}\|\langle\varphi,\psi_{R}\rangle t_{R}\|_{\mathfrak{X}}

converges for all
\varphi\in\mathscr{S}_{\infty}
. If
Q_{0}:=[0,1)^{n}
, we note that
\varphi=\varphi_{Q_{0}}
. Then

\begin{split}\|\langle\varphi,\psi_{R}\rangle t_{R}\|_{\mathfrak{X}}&=|\langle\varphi,\psi_{R}\rangle|\sup_{\|\mathfrak{e}^{*}\|_{\mathfrak{X}^{*}}\leq 1}|\langle t_{R},\mathfrak{e}^{*}\rangle|\leq B_{-M}(Q_{0},R)\sup_{\|\mathfrak{e}^{*}\|_{\mathfrak{X}^{*}}\leq 1}\rho_{R}(t_{R})\rho_{R}^{*}(\mathfrak{e}^{*}),\end{split}

where

\begin{split}\rho_{R}(t_{R})&\leq\ell(R)^{s}|R|^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p}}\|t\|_{\dot{a}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho)}\approx B_{\sigma,-\sigma,0}(Q_{0},R)\|t\|_{\dot{a}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho)},\qquad\sigma:=s+n\Big(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p}\Big),\end{split}

by Lemma
15.5
, and

\rho_{R}^{*}(\mathfrak{e}^{*})\lesssim B_{a,b,c}(Q_{0},R)\rho_{Q_{0}}^{*}(\mathfrak{e}^{*})

by assumption (
15.2
) and the definition of the dual norms. Moreover,

\rho_{Q_{0}}^{*}(\mathfrak{e}^{*})=\sup_{\mathfrak{e}\in\mathfrak{X}\setminus\{0\}}\frac{|\langle\mathfrak{e}^{*},\mathfrak{e}\rangle|}{\rho_{Q_{0}}(\mathfrak{e})}\approx\sup_{\mathfrak{e}\in\mathfrak{X}\setminus\{0\}}\frac{|\langle\mathfrak{e}^{*},\mathfrak{e}\rangle|}{\|\mathfrak{e}\|_{\mathfrak{X}}}=\|\mathfrak{e}^{*}\|_{\mathfrak{X}^{*}}\leq 1

by the assumed equivalence of
\rho_{Q_{0}}
and
\|\ \|_{\mathfrak{X}}
.

Hence,

\begin{split}\sum_{R\in\mathscr{D}}&\|\langle\varphi,\psi_{R}\rangle t_{R}\|_{\mathfrak{X}}\lesssim\sum_{R\in\mathscr{D}}B_{-M}(Q_{0},R)B_{\sigma,-\sigma,0}(Q_{0},R)B_{a,b,c}(Q_{0},R)\|t\|_{\dot{a}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho)},\end{split}

and this series converges as soon as
M>0
is chosen large enough.
∎

Lemma 15.11
.

Suppose that
\rho=\{\rho_{Q}\}_{Q\in\mathscr{D}}
is
a family of
(u,a,b,c)
-norms for some

u\in(0,1]
and
a,b,c\in(0,\infty)
.
For
t\in\dot{a}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho)
and two Littlewood–Paley functions
\varphi,\psi
, we have

\|\rho_{\mathscr{D}_{i}}(\varphi_{i}*T_{\psi}t)\|_{\textit{\L}^{\infty}(\mathscr{D}_{i})}\lesssim\sum_{j:|j-i|\leq 3}\big(\phi_{j}*\rho_{\mathscr{D}_{j}}(t_{j})^{u}\big)^{\frac{1}{u}},

where
\phi_{j}(x):=2^{jn}\phi(2^{j}x)
,
\phi(x):=(1+|x|)^{-M}
with
M>0
as large as we like, and
the implicit positive constant is independent of

i
and
t
.

Proof.

By Lemma
15.10
, we can meaningfully consider

\varphi_{i}*T_{\psi}t(x)=\sum_{R\in\mathscr{D}}\varphi_{i}*\psi_{R}(x)t_{R},

where

\psi_{R}(x)=\frac{1}{|R|^{\frac{1}{2}}}\psi\Big(\frac{x-x_{R}}{\ell(R)}\Big)=|R|^{\frac{1}{2}}\psi_{j_{R}}(x-x_{R})

and
j_{R}:=-\log_{2}\ell(R)
.
The convolution
\varphi_{i}*\psi_{R}
can be non-zero only if
\operatorname{supp}\hat{\varphi}_{i}
and
\operatorname{supp}\hat{\psi}_{R}=\operatorname{supp}\hat{\psi}_{j_{R}}
intersect,
which requires
\pi^{-2}<2^{i}/2^{j_{R}}<\pi^{2}
, i.e.,

|i-j_{R}|<\log_{2}\pi^{2}<4.

Then, for
x\in Q\in\mathscr{D}_{i}
,

\begin{split}\rho_{Q}(\varphi_{i}*T_{\psi}t(x))^{u}&\leq\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{R\in\mathscr D}{\abs{j_R-i}<4}}\big(|R|^{\frac{1}{2}}|\varphi_{i}*\psi_{j_{R}}(x-x_{R})|\cdot\rho_{Q}(t_{R})\big)^{u}\\
&\lesssim\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{R\in\mathscr D}{\abs{j_R-i}<4}}\big(|R|^{\frac{1}{2}}\phi_{i}^{\prime}(x-x_{R})\cdot(1+2^{i}|x_{Q}-x_{R}|)^{c}\rho_{R}(t_{R})\big)^{u},\end{split}

using Lemma
15.9
to produce the factor with
\phi^{\prime}(z)=(1+|z|)^{-M^{\prime}}
, where
M^{\prime}>0

can be as large as we like, and assumption (
15.2
) to produce the factor
(1+2^{i}|x_{Q}-x_{R}|)^{c}
. The fact that
|j_{R}-i|<4
allows us to ignore several factors involving
\ell(Q)/\ell(R)
or
|i-j_{R}|
, and use the numbers
i
and
j_{R}
interchangeably in the upper bound. We can then continue the estimate with

\begin{split}\rho_{Q}(\varphi_{i}*T_{\psi}t(x))^{u}&\lesssim\sum_{\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{R\in\mathscr D}{\abs{j_R-i}<4}}\Big(1+2^{i}|x_{Q}-x_{R}|\Big)^{(c-M^{\prime})u}\Big(\frac{\rho_{R}(t_{R})}{|R|^{\frac{1}{2}}}\Big)^{u}\\
&\approx\sum_{|j-i|<4}\sum_{R\in\mathscr{D}_{j}}\int_{R}2^{jn}\Big(1+2^{j}|x-y|\Big)^{(c-M^{\prime})u}(\rho_{\mathscr{D}_{j}}t_{j})(y)^{u}\,dy\\
&=\sum_{|j-i|<4}\phi_{j}*(\rho_{\mathscr{D}_{j}}t_{j})^{u}(x),\end{split}

where
\phi(x):=(1+|x|)^{-M}
and
M:=(M^{\prime}-c)u
may still be as large as we like, by taking
M^{\prime}
sufficiently large.
∎

Proposition 15.12
.

Suppose that
\rho=\{\rho_{Q}\}_{Q\in\mathscr{D}}
is
a family of
(u,a,b,c)
-norms for some

u\in(0,1]
and
a,b,c\in(0,\infty)
.
Then for all
t\in\dot{a}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho)
, the series
T_{\psi}t
converges in

\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})
, and

\|T_{\psi}t\|_{\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho,\sup,\varphi)}\lesssim\|t\|_{\dot{a}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho)}.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we may take
u\in(0,\min\{p,q\})
.
The convergence of the series was already established in Lemma
15.10
, and Lemma
15.11
gives the pointwise estimate

\|\rho_{\mathscr{D}_{i}}(\varphi_{i}*T_{\psi}t)\|_{\textit{\L}^{\infty}(\mathscr{D}_{i})}\lesssim\sum_{k=-3}^{3}\big(\phi_{i+k}*\rho_{\mathscr{D}_{i+k}}(t_{i+k})^{u}\big)^{\frac{1}{u}}.

Multiplying by
2^{is}
, taking the
(L^{p}\ell^{q})_{A}
quasi-norms of both sides, and proceeding with the usual estimates, we obtain

\begin{split}\|T_{\psi}t\|_{\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho,\sup,\varphi)}&=\Big\|\Big\{2^{is}\|\rho_{\mathscr{D}_{i}}(\varphi_{i}*T_{\psi}t)\|_{\textit{\L}^{\infty}(\mathscr{D}_{i})}\Big\}\Big\|_{(L^{p}\ell^{q})_{A}} \\
&\lesssim\sum_{k=-3}^{3}\Big\|\Big\{2^{is}\big(\phi_{i+k}*\rho_{\mathscr{D}_{i+k}}(t_{i+k})^{u}\big)^{\frac{1}{u}}\Big\}\Big\|_{(L^{p}\ell^{q})_{A}} \\
&\approx\Big\|\Big\{2^{isu}\phi_{i}*\rho_{\mathscr{D}_{i}}(t_{i})^{u}\Big\}\Big\|_{(L^{\frac{p}{u}}\ell^{\frac{q}{u}})_{A}}^{\frac{1}{u}}\lesssim\Big\|\Big\{2^{isu}\rho_{\mathscr{D}_{i}}(t_{i})^{u}\Big\}\Big\|_{(L^{\frac{p}{u}}\ell^{\frac{q}{u}})_{A}}^{\frac{1}{u}} \\
&\approx\Big\|\Big\{2^{is}\rho_{\mathscr{D}_{i}}(t_{i})\Big\}\Big\|_{(L^{p}\ell^{q})_{A}}=\|t\|_{\dot{a}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho)};\end{split}

in the last estimate removing the convolutions
\phi_{i}
, we note that
|\phi_{i}*f|\lesssim Mf
, so that this can be deduced from the Fefferman–Stein vector-valued maximal inequality, given that
\frac{p}{u},\frac{q}{u}>1
.
∎

Lemma 15.13
.

Let
\varphi,\psi
be two Littlewood–Paley functions that satisfy (
15.3
).
Then for all
f\in\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, we have

f=\sum_{Q\in\mathscr{D}}\langle f,\varphi_{Q}\rangle\psi_{Q}.

Proof.

This is
[
24
, Lemma 2.1]
(replacing
\varphi(z)
by
\varphi(-z)
).
∎

We now have everything to give:

Proof of Theorem
15.3
.

We already know from Proposition
15.7
and Remark
15.8
that

\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho,\sup,\varphi)=\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho,\varphi),

so we will use these spaces interchangeably and omit the “
\sup
”.
By Propositions
15.6
and
15.12
, we know that

S_{\varphi}:\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho,\tilde{\varphi})\to\dot{a}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho)\quad\text{and}\quad T_{\psi}:\dot{a}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho)\to\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho,\chi)

are bounded for all Littlewood–Paley functions
\varphi,\psi,\chi
. Hence

(15.5)

T_{\psi}\circ S_{\varphi}:\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho,\tilde{\varphi})\to\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho,\chi)

is bounded, and Lemma
15.10
informs us that

T_{\psi}(S_{\varphi}f)=\sum_{Q\in\mathscr{D}}\langle f,\varphi_{Q}\rangle\psi_{Q}

converges in
\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})
. Hence

(15.6)

\langle T_{\psi}(S_{\varphi}f),\mathfrak{e}^{*}\rangle=\sum_{Q\in\mathscr{D}}\langle\langle f,\mathfrak{e}^{*}\rangle,\varphi_{Q}\rangle\psi_{Q}=T_{\psi}(S_{\varphi}\langle f,\mathfrak{e}^{*}\rangle)

converges in
\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, where
\langle f,\mathfrak{e}^{*}\rangle\in\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is the distribution defined by

\langle\langle f,\mathfrak{e}^{*}\rangle,\phi\rangle:=\langle\langle f,\phi\rangle,\mathfrak{e}^{*}\rangle

for all
\phi\in\mathscr{S}_{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

Given a Littlewood–Paley function
\varphi
, it is well known how to construct another Littlewood–Paley function
\psi
so that (
15.3
) holds: indeed, we can take

\hat{\psi}(\xi):=\frac{\overline{\hat{\varphi}(-\xi)}}{\sum\limits_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}|\hat{\varphi}(2^{k}\xi)|^{2}},

where the denominator is strictly positive since the
k
-th term is strictly positive for
|\xi|\in[\alpha^{-1},\alpha]2^{-k}
and the condition that
\alpha>\sqrt{2}
guarantees that the consecutive intervals overlap. For such
\psi
, Lemma
15.13
guarantees that the right-hand side of (
15.6
) is simply
\langle f,\mathfrak{e}^{*}\rangle
. Since this is true for all
\mathfrak{e}^{*}\in\mathfrak{X}^{*}
, (
15.6
) implies that
T_{\psi}(S_{\varphi}f)=f
for all
f\in\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho,\tilde{\varphi})
, and the boundedness (
15.5
) shows that

\|f\|_{\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho,\chi)}\lesssim\|f\|_{\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho,\tilde{\varphi})}.

Since this is true for any two
\varphi,\chi
, we have proved claim (
i
). The other claims then follow since we already proved them for some particular choice of
\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho,\chi)
, and by (
i
), they are all equal.
∎

Since, under the assumptions of
Theorems
15.3
and
15.4

respectively, the spaces
\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho,\varphi)

and
\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(V,\varphi)

do not depend on the choice of

\varphi
, in what follows we denote them simply by

\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho)
and
\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(V)
,
respectively, under these assumptions.

At the end of this section, we provide some useful consequences
of the
\varphi
-transform characterization.
We first consider the embeddings between
Besov–Triebel–Lizorkin spaces
and Schwartz (distribution) spaces.
For any linear space
D
of functions on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we define

(15.7)

\displaystyle D\otimes\mathfrak{X}:=\Big\{\sum_{k=1}^{N}f_{k}\mathfrak{e}_{k}:N\in\mathbb{N},\ f_{k}\in D,\ \mathfrak{e}_{k}\in\mathfrak{X}\Big\}.

Corollary 15.14
.

Let
p\in(0,\infty)
,
q\in(0,\infty]
,
and
s\in\mathbb{R}
.

(i)

If
\rho=\{\rho_{Q}\}_{Q\in\mathscr{D}}
is a family
of
(u,a,b,c)
-norms for some
u\in(0,1]
and

a,b,c\in[0,\infty)
, then
\mathscr{S}_{\infty}\otimes\mathfrak{X}\hookrightarrow\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho)\hookrightarrow\mathscr{S}^{\prime}_{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})
.

(ii)

If
V\in\mathscr{A}_{r}
with
r\in[p,\infty)

and if
q\in(0,r+\varepsilon_{V})

when
A=F
, where
\varepsilon_{V}
is
the optimal reverse Hölder lifting index of
V
, then

\mathscr{S}_{\infty}\otimes\mathfrak{X}\hookrightarrow\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(V)\hookrightarrow\mathscr{S}^{\prime}_{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})
.

Proof.

By Theorems
14.3
and
14.7
,
we only need to prove (
i
).
Indeed, this argument is completely analogous to the proof in the
scalar or the matrix-weighted case: the embedding

\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho)\hookrightarrow\mathscr{S}^{\prime}_{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})

follows from repeating the proof of
[
10
, Proposition 3.39]
with
Lemma 3.33 and Theorem 3.29 therein replaced, respectively,
by Lemma
15.10
and Theorem
15.3
;
the embedding
\mathscr{S}_{\infty}\otimes\mathfrak{X}\hookrightarrow\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho)

follows from repeating the proof of
[
69
, Proposition 3.2(viii)]

and using Definition
15.1
(
iii
).
∎

Finally, we give the following boundedness of the Hilbert transform and the Riesz potential,
which is in sharp contrast with a related result for
L^{p}(V)
spaces given in Theorem
10.1
.

Corollary 15.15
.

Let
p\in(0,\infty)
,
q\in(0,\infty]
,
and
s\in\mathbb{R}
.

(i)

If
\rho=\{\rho_{Q}\}_{Q\in\mathscr{D}}
is a family
of
(u,a,b,c)
-norms for some
u\in(0,1]
and

a,b,c\in[0,\infty)
, then,

(a)

for
n=1
, the Hilbert transform is an isomorphism of
\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho)
onto itself, and

(b)

for all
n\geq 1
, the operator
(-\Delta)^{\frac{t}{2}}
is an isomorphism of

\dot{A}^{s+t}_{p,q}(\rho)
onto
\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho)
.

(ii)

If
V\in\mathscr{A}_{r}
with
r\in[p,\infty)

and if
q\in(0,r+\varepsilon_{V})

when
A=F
, where
\varepsilon_{V}
is
the optimal reverse Hölder lifting index of
V
, then
the conclusions of (
i
) hold
with
\rho
replaced by
V
.

Proof.

By Theorems
14.3
and
14.7
, we only need to
prove (
i
).
We note that both operators are Fourier multipliers
T_{m}f=(m\hat{f})^{\vee}
with symbols
m(\xi)=-i\operatorname{sgn}(\xi)
and
m(\xi)=|\xi|^{t}
, respectively, that are smooth on
\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{0\}
, with at most moderate blow-up as
|\xi|\to 0
or
\infty
. Hence they act on
\mathscr{S}_{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, and thus on
\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})
. For the quantities featuring in the
\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}
norm, we have

(\varphi_{j}*T_{m}f)^{\wedge}=\hat{\varphi}(2^{-j}\cdot)m\hat{f}=(m(2^{j}\cdot)\hat{\varphi})(2^{-j})\hat{f}.

In the case of
(-\Delta)^{\frac{t}{2}}
resp. the Hilbert transform, we have
m(2^{j}\cdot)=2^{jt}m
resp.
m(2^{j}\cdot)=m=2^{j\cdot 0}m
; hence

(\varphi_{j}*T_{m}f)^{\wedge}=2^{jt}(m\hat{\varphi})(2^{-j}\cdot)\hat{f}=2^{jt}(\psi_{j}*f)^{\wedge},

where
\psi:=(m\hat{\varphi})^{\vee}=T_{m}\varphi
, and we take
t=0
in the case of the Hilbert transform.

In either case, since
m
is smooth and bounded away from zero on compact subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{0\}
, it follows that
\psi=T_{m}\varphi
is another Littlewood–Paley function. Thus

\begin{split}\|T_{m}f\|_{\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho,\varphi)}&=\big\|\big\{2^{js}\varphi_{j}*T_{m}f\big\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\big\|_{(L^{p}\ell^{q})_{A}}\\
&=\big\|\big\{2^{j(s+t)}\psi_{j}*f\big\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\big\|_{(L^{p}\ell^{q})_{A}}=\|f\|_{\dot{A}^{s+t}_{p,q}(\rho,\psi)},\end{split}

where
t=0
in the case of the Hilbert transform. Since the norms on the left and the right are independent (up to constants) of the Littlewood–Paley functions, the claims follow.
∎

16.
Relation of Triebel–Lizorkin spaces to
L^{p}(V)

In the classical theory of function spaces, one of the key features of Triebel–Lizorkin spaces is that they contain the usual
L^{p}
spaces as the special case
\dot{F}^{0}_{p,2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})=L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
for
p\in(1,\infty)
. Under the
\mathscr{A}_{p}
condition, this remains true even for matrix-weighted spaces, as shown in slightly different versions in
[
28
,
42
,
66
]
.
However, this cannot possibly extend to the level of generality that we consider: By Corollary
15.15
,
the Hilbert transform is bounded on
\dot{F}^{0}_{p,q}(\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(\mathscr{D},V)})
for all
p\in(0,\infty)
and
q\in(0,\infty]
, and on
\dot{F}^{0}_{p,q}(V)
for all
0<q\leq p<\infty
, whenever
V\in\mathscr{A}_{p}
.
On the other hand, Theorem
10.1
shows that this condition is insufficient for the boundedness on
L^{p}(V)
. Thus
V\in\mathscr{A}_{p}
does not guarantee the coincidence of
L^{p}(V)
with any of the Triebel–Lizorkin spaces
\dot{F}^{0}_{p,q}(\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(\mathscr{D},V)})
or
\dot{F}^{0}_{p,q}(V)
with
q
in the indicated ranges.

On the other hand, for functions taking values in a Banach space, even in the unweighted case, the best that one can say in general is the “sandwich” result

(16.1)

\dot{F}^{0}_{p,1}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})\subset L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})\subset\dot{F}^{0}_{p,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X}),\qquad p\in(1,\infty);

see
[
41
, Propositions 14.6.13 and 14.7.8]
for the positive result and its sharpness, respectively. We will here prove that (
16.1
) remains valid for the operator-weighted versions if the weight satisfies
V\in\mathscr{A}_{p}
.

Proposition 16.1
.

Let
p,r\in(1,\infty)

and
V\in\mathscr{A}_{\max\{p,r\}}
. If

f\in\dot{F}^{0}_{p,1}(\rho_{\textit{\L}^{r}(\mathscr{D},V)})=\dot{F}^{0}_{p,1}(V)
, then
f\in L^{p}(V)
and

\|f\|_{L^{p}(V)}\lesssim\|f\|_{\dot{F}^{0}_{p,1}(V)}\approx\|f\|_{\dot{F}^{0}_{p,1}(\rho_{\textit{\L}^{r}(\mathscr{D},V)})}

with the implicit positive constants independent of
f
.

Proof.

The equality of the two
\dot{F}^{0}_{p,1}
spaces and the related norm equivalence is a special case of Theorem
14.7
with
q=1\in(0,\max\{p,r\})
. To show that this space embeds into
L^{p}(V)
, we first note that

\|f\|_{\dot{F}^{0}_{p,1}(V)}=\Big\|\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\|V(\varphi_{j}*f)\|\Big\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}

In particular, the partial sums of
\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}V(\varphi_{j}*f)
form a Cauchy sequence in
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})
, which converges to some
g\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})
. Equivalently,
\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\varphi_{j}*f
converges to
h:=V^{-1}g\in L^{p}(V;\mathfrak{X})
, where

\|h\|_{L^{p}(V;\mathfrak{X})}=\|g\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})}\leq\Big\|\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\|V(\varphi_{j}*f)\|\Big\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}=\|f\|_{\dot{F}^{0}_{p,1}(V)}.

Recalling from Theorem
15.3
or
15.4
that
\dot{F}^{0}_{p,1}(\rho_{\textit{\L}^{r}(\mathscr{D},V)})=\dot{F}^{0}_{p,1}(V)
is independent of the choice of the Littlewood–Paley function implicit in its definition, we can choose a special Littlewood–Paley function with
\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\hat{\varphi}(2^{j}\xi)\equiv 1
for
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{0\}
. Then
\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\varphi_{j}*f=f
in
\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})
, and hence
f=h
in the sense of
\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})
. Thus the previous display is the claimed embedding.
∎

Proposition 16.2
.

Let
p\in(1,\infty)
,

r\in(0,p]
, and
V\in\mathscr{A}_{p}
. If
f\in L^{p}(V)
, then
f\in\dot{F}^{0}_{p,\infty}(\rho_{\textit{\L}^{r}(\mathscr{D},V)})
and

\|f\|_{\dot{F}^{0}_{p,\infty}(\rho_{\textit{\L}^{r}(\mathscr{D},V)})}\lesssim\|f\|_{L^{p}(V)}

with the implicit positive constant independent of
f
.

Proof.

Let
x\in Q\in\mathscr{D}_{j}
and
\mathfrak{e}^{*}\in\mathfrak{X}^{*}
. Then

\begin{split}|\langle\varphi_{j}*f(x),\mathfrak{e}^{*}\rangle|&=\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\varphi_{j}(x-y)\langle f(y),\mathfrak{e}^{*}\rangle\,dy\Big|\\
&\lesssim\sum_{R\in\mathscr{D}_{j}}|R|\phi_{j}^{\prime}(x-x_{R})\fint_{R}|\langle V(y)f(y),V^{-*}(y)\mathfrak{e}^{*}\rangle|\,dy\\
&\leq\sum_{R\in\mathscr{D}_{j}}|R|\phi_{j}^{\prime}(x-x_{R})\|Vf\|_{\textit{\L}^{(p^{\prime}+\eta)^{\prime}}(R:\mathfrak{X})}\|V^{-*}\mathfrak{e}^{*}\|_{\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}+\eta}(R;\mathfrak{X}^{*})},\end{split}

where
\phi_{j}^{\prime}(z)=2^{j}\phi(2^{j}z)
with
\phi(z):=(1+|z|)^{-M^{\prime}}
dominates
\varphi_{j}\in\mathscr{S}_{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
for any
M^{\prime}>0
and where
\eta>0
is a number guaranteed by the reverse Hölder property of Proposition
5.1
. Thus

\begin{split}\|V^{-*}\mathfrak{e}^{*}\|_{\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}+\eta}(R;\mathfrak{X}^{*})}&=\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}+\eta}(R,V^{-*})}(\mathfrak{e}^{*})\\
&\lesssim\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(R,V^{-*})}(\mathfrak{e}^{*})\qquad\text{by Proposition \ref {prop:RHI}}\\
&\lesssim(1+2^{j}|x_{Q}-x_{R}|)^{c}\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(Q,V^{-*})}(\mathfrak{e}^{*}).\end{split}

Substituting back and denoting
\phi(z):=\phi(z)(1+|z|)^{c}=(1+|z|)^{-M}
, where
M:=M^{\prime}-c
is still as large as we like, we obtain

\begin{split}\frac{|\langle\varphi_{j}*f(x),\mathfrak{e}^{*}\rangle|}{\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(Q,V^{-*})}(\mathfrak{e}^{*})}&\lesssim\sum_{R\in\mathscr{D}_{j}}|R|\phi_{j}(x-x_{R})\|Vf\|_{\textit{\L}^{(p^{\prime}+\eta)^{\prime}}(R;\mathfrak{X})}\\
&\lesssim\Big(\sum_{R\in\mathscr{D}_{j}}|R|\phi_{j}(x-x_{R})\|Vf\|_{\textit{\L}^{(p^{\prime}+\eta)^{\prime}}(R;\mathfrak{X})}^{(p^{\prime}+\eta)^{\prime}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{(p^{\prime}+\eta)^{\prime}}}\\
&\approx(\phi_{j}*\|Vf(\cdot)\|^{(p^{\prime}+\eta)^{\prime}})^{\frac{1}{(p^{\prime}+\eta)^{\prime}}}(x).\end{split}

Thus, by Hölder’s inequality, the
\mathscr{A}_{p}
condition, and the definition of the dual norm,

\begin{split}\rho_{\textit{\L}^{r}(Q,V)}(\varphi_{j}\ast f(x))&\leq\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,V)}(\varphi_{j}*f(x))\lesssim\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(Q,V^{-*})}^{*}(\varphi_{j}*f(x))\\
&\lesssim(\phi_{j}*\|Vf(\cdot)\|^{(p^{\prime}+\eta)^{\prime}})^{\frac{1}{(p^{\prime}+\eta)^{\prime}}}(x)\lesssim M_{(p^{\prime}+\eta)^{\prime}}(Vf)(x)\end{split}

for all
x\in Q\in\mathscr{D}_{j}
. Therefore

\begin{split}\|f\|_{\dot{F}^{0}_{p,\infty}(\rho_{\textit{\L}^{r}(\mathscr{D},V)})}&=\big\|\big\{\rho_{\textit{\L}^{r}(\mathscr{D}_{j},V)}(\varphi_{j}*f)\big\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\big\|_{L^{p}\ell^{\infty}}\\
&\lesssim\|M_{(p^{\prime}+\eta)^{\prime}}(Vf)\|_{L^{p}}\lesssim\|Vf\|_{L^{p}}=\|f\|_{L^{p}(V)},\end{split}

where the last estimate was based on the maximal inequality and the fact that
p^{\prime}+\eta>p^{\prime}
and hence
(p^{\prime}+\eta)^{\prime}<(p^{\prime})^{\prime}=p
for
p\in(1,\infty)
.
∎

17.
Almost diagonal operators

In this section, we study the boundedness of the following class of
almost diagonal operators on Besov and Triebel–Lizorkin spaces.
This is a cornerstone for developing further properties of these spaces
in the subsequent sections.

Definition 17.1
.

A matrix
B=\{b_{QR}\}_{Q,R\in\mathscr{D}}
is said to be

(D,E,F)
-almost diagonal
if

|b_{QR}|\lesssim B_{-E,-F,-D}(Q,R)

where the right-hand side is defined in (
6.6
).

Remark 17.2
.

Definition
17.1
is equivalent to
[
11
, Definition 4.1]
, although it was there formulated slightly differently. The said
[
11
, Definition 4.1]
seems to be the first place where this condition was formulated with generic parameters
D,E,F\in\mathbb{R}
. With specific choices of parameters, the definition goes back to
[
25
, p. 53]
, and reappears in
[
59
, Definition 8.1]
and
[
28
, Definition 2.5]
, each time with different specific parameters for the particular context.

Before proceeding to our main results,
we first recall the classical result.
Recall that the scalar sequence space
\dot{a}^{s}_{p,q}
is defined to be
the set of all
t=\{t_{Q}\}_{Q\in\mathscr{D}}
with each
t_{Q}\in\mathbb{C}
such that

\displaystyle\|t\|_{\dot{a}^{s}_{p,q}}:=\Big\|\Big\{2^{js}t_{j}\Big\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\Big\|_{(L^{p}\ell^{q})_{A}}<\infty,

where
t_{j}
is as in (
12.1
).

Theorem 17.3

(
[
25
, Theorem 3.3]
)
.

Let
B
be
(D,E,F)
-almost diagonal with

(17.1)

D>J,\qquad E>\frac{n}{2}+s,\qquad F>J-\frac{n}{2}-s,

where

(17.2)

J:=\begin{cases}n/\min(1,p,q)&\textup{(Triebel--Lizorkin case)},\\
n/\min(1,p)&\textup{(Besov case)}.\end{cases}

Then
B
is bounded on
\dot{a}^{s}_{p,q}
.

Proof.

Strictly speaking,
[
25
, Theorem 3.3]
covers only the Triebel–Lizorkin case, but the Besov case is similar and easier.
∎

Remark 17.4
.

As showed in
[
11
, Sections 7 and 9]
,
the assumption (
17.1
) on
(D,E,F)

is optimal.
More precisely,
if
a=b
or
a=f
and
q\geq\min\{1,p\}
, and if
every
(D,E,F)
-almost diagonal operator
B
is bounded
on
\dot{a}^{s}_{p,q}
, then, by

[
11
, Sections 7 and 9]
, the triple
(D,E,F)
satisfies
(
17.1
).

In this section, we deal with the boundedness of
B

on average and pointwise-weighted spaces separately.
First, in Subsection
17.1
, we establish the boundedness of
almost diagonal operators on average-weighted spaces
\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho)

under very mild assumptions on
\rho
.
But, in this setting,
the optimal assumption on
(D,E,F)
is still unknown
(see Remark
17.7
).
Next, in Subsection
17.2
,
we improve the assumption on
(D,E,F)
for pointwise-weighted spaces

\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(V)
under
V\in\bigcup_{r\geq p}\mathscr{A}_{r}
,
and we prove that this assumption is optimal in some sense
(see Remark
17.13
).

17.1.
Average-weighted spaces with abstract seminorms

The main result of this subsection is the following
boundedness result.

Theorem 17.5
.

Let
p\in(0,\infty)
,
q\in(0,\infty]
,
and
s\in\mathbb{R}
.
Suppose
\rho=\{\rho_{Q}\}_{Q\in\mathscr{D}}
is a family of

(u,a,b,c)
-norms with some
u\in(0,1]
and
a,b,c\in[0,\infty)
.
If
B
is
(D,E,F)
-almost diagonal with

D>J^{(u)}+c,\qquad E>\frac{n}{2}+s+a,\qquad F>J^{(u)}-\frac{n}{2}-s+b,

where

(17.3)

J^{(u)}:=\begin{cases}n/\min\{p,q,u\}&\textup{(Triebel--Lizorkin case)},\\
n/\min\{p,u\}&\textup{(Besov case)},\end{cases}

then
B
is bounded on
\dot{a}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho)
.

Before going to the proof of Theorem
17.5
,
we give some applications to concrete examples of
\rho
as follows.

Corollary 17.6
.

Let
p,r\in(0,\infty)
,
q\in(0,\infty]
, and
s\in\mathbb{R}
.
Suppose that
B
is a
(D,E,F)
-almost diagonal operator.
If one of the following conditions holds:

(i)

V
is an
(r,\beta)
-doubling weight and

D>J^{(\min\{1,r\})}+\frac{\beta}{r},\qquad E>\frac{n}{2}+s+\frac{n}{r},\qquad F>J^{(\min\{1,r\})}-\frac{n}{2}-s+\frac{\beta-n}{r};\qquad\text{or}

(ii)

V\in\mathscr{A}_{w}
with
w\geq r
has doubling
dimension
\beta\in[n,\infty)
, and

D>J^{(\min\{1,r\})}+\frac{\beta}{w},\qquad E>\frac{n}{2}+s+\frac{n}{w},\qquad F>J^{(\min\{1,r\})}-\frac{n}{2}-s+\frac{\beta-n}{w},

then
B
is bounded on
\dot{a}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho_{\textit{\L}^{r}(\mathscr{D},V)})
.

Proof.

By Remark
15.2
, we find that
(
i
) or (
ii
)
implies that all the assumptions of
Theorem
17.5
are satisfied.
Thus, the claim of the present corollary is precisely the conclusion of Theorem
17.5
.
∎

Remark 17.7
.

Now we give some comments
on the assumptions to
(D,E,F)
in Theorem
17.5

and Corollary
17.6
.
Note that

J^{(\min\{1,r\})}=J\quad\text{if}\quad r=p,

where
J^{(\min\{1,r\})}
is defined in (
17.3
) and appears in Corollary
17.6
, while
J
is defined in (
17.2
).
In particular, specializing Corollary
17.6
(
ii
)
to
w=r=p
and the case of Triebel–Lizorkin spaces

\dot{a}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(V;\mathscr{D})})=\dot{f}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(V;\mathscr{D})}),

Corollary
17.6
(
ii
) has the same assumptions as

[
28
, Theorem 2.6]
in the matrix-weighted case,
and hence provides its natural extension to the operator-weighted case.
However, a sharper condition is known for matrix-
\mathscr{A}_{p}
-weights
from
[
11
, Theorem 5.1]
.
In Subsection
17.2
below, we will
obtain a corresponding result in the operator-weighted setting.
For a general family
\rho
of quasi-norms,
the optimal assumptions on
(D,E,F)

for Theorem
17.5
to hold are still unknown.

Now, we turn to show Theorem
17.5
.
In analogy with the familiar rescaling property of the
L^{p}
norms,

\|f\|_{L^{p}}=\||f|^{r}\|_{L^{\frac{p}{r}}}^{\frac{1}{r}}
,
we first record the following version for the
\dot{a}^{s}_{p,q}
norms:

Lemma 17.8
.

Let
p\in(0,\infty)
,
q\in(0,\infty]
, and
s\in\mathbb{R}
.
If
\rho=\{\rho_{Q}\}_{Q\in\mathscr{D}}

is a family of quasi-norms and
u\in(0,\infty)
, then,
for any
t=\{t_{Q}\}_{Q\in\mathscr{D}}
of vectors
in
\mathfrak{X}
,

\|t\|_{\dot{a}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho)}=\big\|\big\{\rho_{Q}(t_{Q})^{u}\ell(Q)^{\frac{n}{2}(1-u)}\big\}_{Q\in\mathscr{D}}\big\|_{\dot{a}^{su}_{\frac{p}{u},\frac{q}{u}}}^{\frac{1}{u}}.

Proof.

By the definitions of the norms under consideration,

\begin{split}\|t\|_{\dot{a}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho)}&=\Big\|\Big\{\sum_{Q\in\mathscr{D}_{j}}\frac{\rho_{Q}(t_{Q})}{\ell(Q)^{s+\frac{n}{2}}}\mathbf{1}_{Q}\Big\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\Big\|_{(L^{p}\ell^{q})_{A}}=\Big\|\Big\{\sum_{Q\in\mathscr{D}_{j}}\frac{\rho_{Q}(t_{Q})^{u}}{\ell(Q)^{(s+\frac{n}{2})u}}\mathbf{1}_{Q}\Big\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\Big\|_{(L^{\frac{p}{u}}\ell^{\frac{q}{u}})_{A}}^{\frac{1}{u}}\\
&=\Big\|\Big\{\sum_{Q\in\mathscr{D}_{j}}\frac{\rho_{Q}(t_{Q})^{u}\ell(Q)^{\frac{n}{2}(1-u)}}{\ell(Q)^{(su+\frac{n}{2})}}\mathbf{1}_{Q}\Big\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\Big\|_{(L^{\frac{p}{u}}\ell^{\frac{q}{u}})_{A}}^{\frac{1}{u}}=\big\|\big\{\rho_{Q}(t_{Q})^{u}\ell(Q)^{\frac{n}{2}(1-u)}\big\}_{Q\in\mathscr{D}}\big\|_{\dot{a}^{su}_{\frac{p}{u},\frac{q}{u}}}^{\frac{1}{u}},\end{split}

which completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Lemma 17.9
.

Let an infinite matrix
B=\{b_{QR}\}_{Q,R\in\mathscr{D}}
of scalars act on sequences
t=\{t_{Q}\}_{Q\in\mathscr{D}}
by

(Bt)_{Q}:=\sum_{R\in\mathscr{D}}b_{QR}t_{R}.

If
\rho=\{\rho_{Q}\}_{Q\in\mathscr{D}}
is a family of
u
-norms, then

\|B\|_{\mathscr{L}(\dot{a}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho))}\leq\|\tilde{B}\|_{\mathscr{L}(\dot{a}^{su}_{\frac{p}{u},\frac{q}{u}})}^{\frac{1}{u}},

where

(17.4)

\tilde{B}:=\{\tilde{b}_{QR}\}_{Q,R\in\mathscr{D}},\quad\tilde{b}_{QR}:=|b_{QR}|^{u}\big\|\frac{\rho_{Q}}{\rho_{R}}\big\|^{u}\Big(\frac{|Q|}{|R|}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}(1-u)},

and

\big\|\frac{\rho_{Q}}{\rho_{R}}\big\|:=\sup_{\mathfrak{e}\in\mathfrak{X}\setminus\{0\}}\frac{\rho_{Q}(\mathfrak{e})}{\rho_{R}(\mathfrak{e})}

Proof.

By Lemma
17.8
and a direct calculation, we obtain

(17.5)

\begin{split}\|Bt\|_{\dot{a}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho)}&=\big\|\big\{\rho_{Q}((Bt)_{Q})^{u}|Q|^{\frac{1}{2}(1-u)}\big\}_{Q\in\mathscr{D}}\big\|_{\dot{a}^{su}_{\frac{p}{u},\frac{q}{u}}}^{\frac{1}{u}}\\
&\leq\Big\|\Big\{\sum_{R\in\mathscr{D}}\rho_{Q}(b_{QR}t_{R})^{u}|Q|^{\frac{1}{2}(1-u)}\Big\}_{Q\in\mathscr{D}}\Big\|_{\dot{a}^{su}_{\frac{p}{u},\frac{q}{u}}}^{\frac{1}{u}}\\
&\leq\Big\|\Big\{\sum_{R\in\mathscr{D}}|b_{QR}|^{u}\big\|\frac{\rho_{Q}}{\rho_{R}}\big\|^{u}\Big(\frac{|Q|}{|R|}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}(1-u)}\rho_{R}(t_{R})^{u}|R|^{\frac{1}{2}(1-u)}\Big\}_{Q\in\mathscr{D}}\Big\|_{\dot{a}^{su}_{\frac{p}{u},\frac{q}{u}}}^{\frac{1}{u}}.\\
\end{split}

Denoting

\tilde{t}_{R}:=\rho_{R}(t_{R})^{u}|R|^{\frac{1}{2}(1-u)}

and recalling the notation (
17.4
), it follows that

(17.6)

\|Bt\|_{\dot{a}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho)}\leq\|\tilde{B}\tilde{t}\|_{\dot{a}^{su}_{\frac{p}{u},\frac{q}{u}}}^{\frac{1}{u}}\leq\|\tilde{B}\|_{\mathscr{L}(\dot{a}^{su}_{\frac{p}{u},\frac{q}{u}})}^{\frac{1}{u}}\|\tilde{t}\|_{\dot{a}^{su}_{\frac{p}{u},\frac{q}{u}}}^{\frac{1}{u}}=\|\tilde{B}\|_{\mathscr{L}(\dot{a}^{su}_{\frac{p}{u},\frac{q}{u}})}^{\frac{1}{u}}\|t\|_{\dot{a}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho)}

by Lemma
17.8
again in the last step.
∎

Proof of Theorem
17.5
.

Let

\tilde{u}:=\begin{cases}\min\{p,q,u\}&\text{(Triebel--Lizorkin case)},\\
\min\{p,u\}&\text{(Besov case)}.\end{cases}

Then
\rho
is also a family of
\tilde{u}
-norms, and Lemma
17.9
shows that

(17.7)

\|B\|_{\mathscr{L}(\dot{a}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho))}\leq\|\tilde{B}\|_{\mathscr{L}(\dot{a}^{s\tilde{u}}_{\frac{p}{\tilde{u}},\frac{q}{\tilde{u}}})}^{\frac{1}{\tilde{u}}},

where
\tilde{B}
is defined in (
17.4
). The parameter
J
in (
17.2
), but corresponding to the space on the right of (
17.7
), is then

\tilde{J}=\left.\begin{cases}n/\min\{1,\frac{p}{\tilde{u}},\frac{q}{\tilde{u}}\}=n{\tilde{u}}/\min\{p,q,\tilde{u}\}=n{\tilde{u}}/{\tilde{u}}\\
n/\min(1,\frac{p}{\tilde{u}})=n{\tilde{u}}/\min\{p,\tilde{u}\}=n{\tilde{u}}/{\tilde{u}}\end{cases}\right\}=n

in the Triebel–Lizorkin and Besov cases, respectively, but both of them actually result in the same value in the end.

Thus, by Theorem
17.3
, the right-hand side of (
17.7
) is finite provided that
\tilde{B}
is
(\tilde{D},\tilde{E},\tilde{F})
-almost diagonal with

\tilde{D}>n,\qquad\tilde{E}>\frac{n}{2}+s{\tilde{u}},\qquad\tilde{F}>n-\frac{n}{2}-s{\tilde{u}}=\frac{n}{2}-s{\tilde{u}}.

Recalling the definition of
\tilde{B}
from (
17.4
), this means that we need

|b_{QR}|^{\tilde{u}}\big\|\frac{\rho_{Q}}{\rho_{R}}\big\|^{\tilde{u}}\Big(\frac{|Q|}{|R|}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}(1-{\tilde{u}})}\lesssim B_{-\frac{n}{2}-s{\tilde{u}}-\varepsilon,-\frac{n}{2}+s{\tilde{u}}-\varepsilon,-n-\varepsilon}(Q,R),

or, taking
\tilde{u}
-th roots of both sides, and denoting

\frac{\varepsilon}{\tilde{u}}
again simply by
\varepsilon
,

(17.8)

|b_{QR}|\big\|\frac{\rho_{Q}}{\rho_{R}}\big\|\Big(\frac{|Q|}{|R|}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}(\frac{1}{\tilde{u}}-1)}\lesssim B_{-\frac{n}{2{\tilde{u}}}-s-\varepsilon,-\frac{n}{2{\tilde{u}}}+s-\varepsilon,-\frac{n}{\tilde{u}}-\varepsilon}(Q,R).

Using the assumption that

\big\|\frac{\rho_{Q}}{\rho_{R}}\big\|\lesssim B_{a,b,c}(Q,R),

and noting that

\frac{|Q|}{|R|}=\Big(\frac{\ell(Q)}{\ell(R)}\Big)^{n}\approx\Big(1+\frac{\ell(R)}{\ell(Q)}\Big)^{-n}\Big(1+\frac{\ell(Q)}{\ell(R)}\Big)^{n}\approx B_{-n,n,0}(Q,R),

we find that

\operatorname{LHS}\eqref{eq:ad2show}\lesssim|b_{QR}|B_{a-\frac{n}{2}(\frac{1}{\tilde{u}}-1),b+\frac{n}{2}(\frac{1}{\tilde{u}}-1),c}(Q,R).

This is dominated by the right-hand side of (
17.8
) provided that

|b_{QR}|\lesssim B_{-\frac{n}{2}-s-a-\varepsilon,-\frac{n}{\tilde{u}}+\frac{n}{2}+s-b-\varepsilon,-\frac{n}{\tilde{u}}-c-\varepsilon}(Q,R).

Observing that
\frac{n}{\tilde{u}}=J^{(u)}
,
this is precisely the almost diagonality condition that we assumed.
∎

Remark 17.10
.

The proofs of Lemma
17.9
and
Theorem
17.5
tell us a stronger conclusion:
under the assumptions of Theorem
17.5
,
the series
\sum_{R\in\mathscr{D}}b_{QR}t_{R}

is absolutely convergent in
\mathfrak{X}
for all

Q\in\mathscr{D}
and all sequences

t=\{t_{R}\}_{R\in\mathscr{D}}\in\dot{a}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho)
.

Indeed, suppose that the notation is as in the proof of Theorem
17.5
.
Then, from the proof of (
17.7
)
(more precisely, from (
17.5
) and
(
17.6
) in the proof of Lemma
17.9
),
we deduce that

\displaystyle\Big\|\Big\{\sum_{R\in\mathscr{D}}\rho_{Q}(b_{QR}t_{R})^{\tilde{u}}|Q|^{\frac{1}{2}(1-{\tilde{u}})}\Big\}_{Q\in\mathscr{D}}\Big\|_{\dot{a}^{s{\tilde{u}}}_{\frac{p}{\tilde{u}},\frac{q}{\tilde{u}}}}^{\frac{1}{\tilde{u}}}\leq\|\tilde{B}\|_{\mathscr{L}(\dot{a}^{s{\tilde{u}}}_{\frac{p}{\tilde{u}},\frac{q}{\tilde{u}}})}^{\frac{1}{\tilde{u}}}\|t\|_{\dot{a}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho)}.

Therefore, from the assumption that
\rho
is a family of
(\tilde{u},a,b,c)
-norms (see in particular Definition
15.1
(
ii
)),
we obtain

\displaystyle\Big(\sum_{R\in\mathscr{D}}|b_{QR}|\|t_{R}\|_{\mathfrak{X}}\Big)^{\tilde{u}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{R\in\mathscr{D}}\|b_{QR}t_{R}\|_{\mathfrak{X}}^{\tilde{u}}\sim_{Q}\sum_{R\in\mathscr{D}}\rho_{Q}(b_{QR}t_{R})^{\tilde{u}}

\displaystyle\leq|Q|^{\frac{1}{2}(\tilde{u}-1)}\|\tilde{B}\|_{\mathscr{L}(\dot{a}^{s{\tilde{u}}}_{\frac{p}{\tilde{u}},\frac{q}{\tilde{u}}})}^{\frac{1}{\tilde{u}}}\|t\|_{\dot{a}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho)}<\infty.

This shows the absolute convergence of
\sum_{R\in\mathscr{D}}b_{QR}t_{R}
. This conclusion will be used later. (Since absolute convergence is a qualitative property, the fact that the implicit constant above, inherited from Definition
15.1
(
ii
), is allowed to depend on
Q
, is of no concern.)

17.2.
Muckenhoupt-weighted spaces

Now, we aim to improve the assumptions on
(D,E,F)

for weighted spaces under the operator
\mathscr{A}_{p}
condition.
To this end, we need the following technical lemma
about the almost diagonal operators and ball average operators,
which was proved in the case
\mathfrak{X}=\mathbb{C}^{m}
in

[
11
, Lemma 5.4]
.
But the proof of
[
11
, Lemma 5.4]
does not
use any special property of finite-dimensional spaces
and hence this estimate still holds for general

\mathfrak{X}
.

Lemma 17.11
.

Let
p\in(0,\infty)
,
q\in(0,\infty]
,
and
B
be
(D,E,F)
-almost diagonal for some
D,E,F\in\mathbb{R}
.
Then there exists a positive constant
C
such that,
for any
t:=\{t_{Q}\}_{Q\in\mathscr{D}}
in
\mathfrak{X}

satisfying that
Bt
is well defined, any
u\in(0,1]
,
and any sequence
\{H_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}

in
L^{p}_{\mathrm{loc},\operatorname{so}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X}))
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Big\|\Big\{\|H_{j}\left(Bt\right)_{j}\|_{\mathfrak{X}}\Big\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\Big\|_{(L^{p}\ell^{q})_{A}}^{v}&\leq C\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\Bigg[2^{-(E-\frac{n}{2})k_{-}}2^{-k_{+}(F+\frac{n}{2}-\frac{n}{u})}2^{-(D-\frac{n}{u})l}\\
&\quad\times\Bigg\|\Bigg\{\Bigg[\fint_{B(\cdot,2^{l+k_{+}-i})}\|H_{i-k}(\cdot)t_{i}(y)\|_{\mathfrak{X}}^{u}\,dy\Bigg]^{\frac{1}{u}}\Bigg\}_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\Bigg\|_{(L^{p}\ell^{q})_{A}}\Bigg]^{v},\end{split}

where
t_{i}
and
(Bt)_{j}
are defined as in (
12.1
)
and
v:=\min\{1,p,q\}
.

Theorem 17.12
.

Let
p\in(0,\infty)
,
V\in\mathscr{A}_{r}
with
r\in[p,\infty)

have doubling dimension
\beta
, and
s\in\mathbb{R}
.
Suppose that
B
is a
(D,E,F)
-almost diagonal operator
satisfying

(17.9)

D>J+\frac{\beta-n}{r},\qquad E>\frac{n}{2}+s,\qquad F>J-\frac{n}{2}-s+\frac{\beta-n}{r},

where
J
is as in (
17.2
).

(i)

If
q\in(0,\infty]
,
then
B
is bounded on
\dot{b}^{s}_{p,q}(V)=\dot{b}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho_{\textit{\L}^{r}(\mathscr{D},V)})
.

(ii)

If
q\in(0,r+\varepsilon_{V})
,
where
\varepsilon_{V}
is
the optimal reverse Hölder lifting index of
V
, then

B
is bounded on
\dot{f}^{s}_{p,q}(V)=\dot{f}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho_{\textit{\L}^{r}(\mathscr{D},V)})
.

Remark 17.13
.

The assumptions on
(D,E,F)
in Theorem
17.12

improve those in Corollary
17.6
(
ii
) for
w=r
.
Moreover, when
V\in\mathscr{A}_{p}
,
the assumptions on
(D,E,F)
in Theorem
17.12
are optimal
in the following sense.

(i)

If
p\in(0,1]
, then, by Corollary
6.3
,
one can choose the doubling dimension
\beta=n
.
In this case, (
17.9
) reduces to
(
17.1
) and hence, by Remark
17.4
,
is optimal even in the scalar case.

(ii)

If
p\in(1,\infty)
, then, compared with (
17.1
),
the assumptions on
D
and
F
in (
17.9
) contain an additional
term “
\frac{\beta-n}{p}
”. This is mainly due
to our use of (
17.11
) in the proof of Theorem
17.12
.
Indeed, we later show that the estimate
(
17.11
) cannot be improved in the
infinite-dimensional case; see Proposition
17.15
.

Proof of Theorem
17.12
.

The equalities of
\dot{a}^{s}_{p,q}(V)=\dot{a}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho_{\textit{\L}^{r}(\mathscr{D},V)})
follow from Corollary
14.2
in the Besov case and Theorem
14.7
in the Triebel–Lizorkin case; note that we now assume
r\in[p,\infty)
, and hence
\max\{p,r\}=r
. Indeed, the coincidence of the spaces is important, since our proof will estimate the pointwise-weighted norm
\|Bt\|_{\dot{a}^{s}_{p,q}(V)}
by the average-weighted norm
\|t\|_{\dot{a}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho_{\textit{\L}^{r}(\mathscr{D},V)})}
.

Similarly to
[
11
, Lemma 5.3]
,
we only need to consider the case
s=0
.
More precisely, if we define

(J_{s}t)_{Q}:=\ell(Q)^{-s}t_{Q}
for any
t:=\{t_{Q}\}_{Q\in\mathscr{D}}

and any
Q\in\mathscr{D}
,
and define

\widetilde{B}:=\Big\{\Big[\frac{\ell(R)}{\ell(Q)}\Big]^{s}b_{QR}\Big\}_{Q,R\in\mathscr{D}}

for
B:=\{b_{QR}\}_{Q,R\in\mathscr{D}}
,
then, from definitions, it immediately follows
that
J_{s}:\dot{a}^{s}_{p,q}(V)\to\dot{a}^{0}_{p,q}(V)

is an isometric isomorphism,
\widetilde{B}

is a
(D,E-s,F+s)
-almost diagonal operator, and

J_{s}\circ B=\widetilde{B}\circ J_{s}
. Therefore,
once we show that, for a certain triple
(D,E,F)
,
every
(D,E,F)
-almost diagonal operator
is bounded on
\dot{a}^{0}_{p,q}(V)
,
we can use the above
transformations to further obtain the
boundedness of all
(D,E+s,F-s)
-almost diagonal operators
on
\dot{a}^{s}_{p,q}(V)
.

In the remainder of the proof, we always assume
s=0
.
We deal with (
i
) and (
ii
) in
a unified way. By the assumptions on
(D,E,F)
, we can choose

u\in(0,\frac{n}{J})
satisfying

D>\frac{n}{u}+\frac{\beta-n}{r}
,
E>\frac{n}{2}
, and
F>\frac{n}{u}-\frac{n}{2}+\frac{\beta-n}{r}
.
Applying Lemma
17.11
with this
u
and with
H_{j}:=V
for all
j\in\mathbb{Z}
, we find that

(17.10)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|Bt\|_{\dot{a}^{0}_{p,q}(V)}^{v}&\lesssim\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\Bigg[2^{-(E-\frac{n}{2})k_{-}}2^{-(F+\frac{n}{2}-\frac{n}{u})k_{+}}2^{-(D-\frac{n}{u})l}\\
&\quad\times\Bigg\|\Bigg\{\Bigg[\fint_{B(\cdot,2^{l+k_{+}-i})}\|V(\cdot)t_{i}(y)\|_{\mathfrak{X}}^{u}\,dy\Bigg]^{\frac{1}{u}}\Bigg\}_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\Bigg\|_{(L^{p}\ell^{q})_{A}}\Bigg]^{v}.\end{split}

Hence, if we can prove that, for any
M\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
,

(17.11)

\displaystyle\Bigg\|\Bigg\{\Bigg[\fint_{B(\cdot,2^{M-i})}\|V(\cdot)t_{i}(y)\|_{\mathfrak{X}}^{u}\,dy\Bigg]^{\frac{1}{u}}\Bigg\}_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\Bigg\|_{(L^{p}\ell^{q})_{A}}\lesssim 2^{\frac{M(\beta-n)}{r}}\|t\|_{\dot{a}^{0}_{p,q}(\rho_{\textit{\L}^{r}(\mathscr{D},V)})},

then, by (
17.10
) and the assumptions
on
a
and
(D,E,F)
, we further obtain the boundedness of

B
on
\dot{a}^{0}_{p,q}(V)=\dot{a}^{0}_{p,q}(\rho_{\textit{\L}^{r}(\mathscr{D},V)})
, recalling that we already observed the coincidence of the two spaces under the assumptions of the theorem that we are proving.

For an arbitrary fixed
i_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}
, let us denote

\|t\|_{(L^{p}\ell^{q})_{\hat{A}}}=\|\{t_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\|_{(L^{p}\ell^{q})_{\hat{A}}}:=\begin{cases}\|t_{i_{0}}\|_{L^{p}}&\text{if }A=B,\\
\|\{t_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\|_{L^{p}\ell^{q}}&\text{if }A=F.\end{cases}

Thus
(L^{p}\ell^{q})_{\hat{B}}=L^{p}
, we simply ignore all
i
except
i=i_{0}
.

To prove (
17.11
), it suffices to show that

(17.12)

\displaystyle\Bigg\|\Bigg\{\Bigg[\fint_{B(\cdot,2^{M-i})}\|V(\cdot)t_{i}(y)\|_{\mathfrak{X}}^{u}\,dy\Bigg]^{\frac{1}{u}}\Bigg\}_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\Bigg\|_{(L^{p}\ell^{q})_{\hat{A}}}\lesssim 2^{\frac{M(\beta-n)}{r}}\Big\|\Big\{\rho_{\textit{\L}^{r}(\mathscr{D}_{i},V)}(t_{i})\Big\}_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\Big\|_{(L^{p}\ell^{q})_{\hat{A}}}.

Indeed, this is precisely a restatement of (
17.11
) when
A=F
, while for
A=B
we obtain (
17.11
) by summing (
17.12
) over
i_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}
.

We now show (
17.12
). Since

u<\frac{n}{J}=\begin{cases}\min\{1,p\}&\text{if $A=B$},\\
\min\{1,p,q\}&\text{if $A=F$},\end{cases}

we have the duality
(L^{(\frac{p}{u})^{\prime}}\ell^{(\frac{q}{u})^{\prime}})_{\hat{A}}^{\prime}=(L^{\frac{p}{u}}\ell^{\frac{q}{u}})_{\hat{A}}
.

Hence, from the Hahn–Banach theorem, we deduce

(17.13)

\begin{split}\operatorname{LHS}\eqref{bdd-AD-ple1-e5b}&=\Bigg\|\Bigg\{\fint_{B(\cdot,2^{M-i})}\|V(\cdot)t_{i}(y)\|_{\mathfrak{X}}^{u}\,dy\Bigg\}_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\Bigg\|_{(L^{\frac{p}{u}}\ell^{\frac{q}{u}})_{A}}^{\frac{1}{u}}\\
&=\sup\Bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\fint_{B(x,2^{M-i})}\|V(x)t_{i}(y)\|_{\mathfrak{X}}^{u}\,dyg_{i}(x)\,dx\Bigg)^{\frac{1}{u}},\end{split}

where the supremum is taken over all
\{g_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}

such that
\|\{g_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\|_{(L^{(\frac{p}{u})^{\prime}}\ell^{(\frac{q}{u})^{\prime}})_{A}}\leq 1
. (When
A=B
, we can simply replace the sum over
i\in\mathbb{Z}
by the single term
i=i_{0}
and take the supremum over
\|g_{i}\|_{L^{(\frac{p}{u})^{\prime}}}\leq 1
.)

We now choose some
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{V})
, where we also demand that

(17.14)

q-r<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{V}\quad\text{if}\quad A=F,

which is possible, since
\varepsilon_{V}>0
and
q<r+\varepsilon_{V}
in that case.

Then

(17.15)

\displaystyle\rho_{\textit{\L}^{r+\varepsilon}(Q,V)}(\mathfrak{e})\lesssim\rho_{\textit{\L}^{r}(Q,V)}(\mathfrak{e})\quad\forall\,Q\in\mathscr{D},\forall\,\mathfrak{e}\in\mathfrak{X}.

Note that, for any
i\in\mathbb{Z}
,
Q\in\mathscr{D}_{i-M}
, and
x\in Q
,
we have
B(x,2^{M-i})\subset 3Q
.
Thus, for any
i\in\mathbb{Z}
,

(17.16)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\fint_{B(x,2^{M-i})}\|V(x)t_{i}(y)\|_{\mathfrak{X}}^{u}\,dyg_{i}(x)\,dx\\
&\quad\lesssim\sum_{Q\in\mathscr{D}_{i-M}}\int_{3Q}\fint_{3Q}\|V(x)t_{i}(y)\|_{\mathfrak{X}}^{u}g_{i}(x)\,dx\,dy\quad\text{by Tonelli's Theorem}\\
&\quad\leq\sum_{Q\in\mathscr{D}_{i-M}}\int_{3Q}\rho_{\textit{\L}^{r+\varepsilon}(3Q,V)}\big(t_{i}(y)\big)^{u}\Bigg[\fint_{3Q}g_{i}(x)^{(\frac{r+\varepsilon}{u})^{\prime}}\,dx\Bigg]^{1-\frac{u}{r+\varepsilon}}\,dy\end{split}

by Hölder’s inequality.
For any
R\in\mathscr{D}_{i}
and
Q\in\mathscr{D}_{i-M}
satisfying
R\cap 3Q\neq\emptyset
,
it holds that
R\subset 5Q
, which further implies

\displaystyle\begin{split}\rho_{\textit{\L}^{r}(3Q,V)}(t_{i})&\lesssim\rho_{\textit{\L}^{r}(5Q,V)}(t_{i})\\
&\lesssim\Big(\frac{\ell(5Q)}{\ell(R)}\Big)^{\frac{\beta-n}{r}}\rho_{\textit{\L}^{r}(R,V)}(t_{i})\quad\text{by Definition \ref {def:dbDim}}\\
&\approx 2^{\frac{M(\beta-n)}{r}}\rho_{\textit{\L}^{r}(R,V)}(t_{i}).\end{split}

Hence, for any
Q\in\mathscr{D}_{i-M}
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\rho_{\textit{\L}^{r+\varepsilon}(3Q,V)}(t_{i}){\bf 1}_{3Q}&\lesssim\rho_{\textit{\L}^{r}(3Q,V)}(t_{i}){\bf 1}_{3Q}\quad\text{by \eqref{eq:RHIepse2}}\\
&=\sum_{R\in\mathscr{D}_{i},R\cap 3Q\neq\emptyset}\rho_{\textit{\L}^{r}(3Q,V)}(t_{i}){\bf 1}_{R\cap 3Q}\\
&\lesssim 2^{\frac{M(\beta-n)}{r}}\rho_{\textit{\L}^{r}(\mathscr{D}_{i},V)}(t_{i}){\bf 1}_{3Q}.\end{split}

From the previous two displays, we find that

(17.17)

\begin{split}\operatorname{RHS}\eqref{bdd-AD-ple1-e7}&\lesssim 2^{\frac{M(\beta-n)u}{r}}\sum_{Q\in\mathscr{D}_{i-M}}\int_{3Q}\rho_{\textit{\L}^{r}(\mathscr{D}_{i},V)}(t_{i}(y))^{u}M_{(\frac{r+\varepsilon}{u})^{\prime}}g_{i}(y)\,dy\\
&\lesssim 2^{\frac{M(\beta-n)u}{r}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\rho_{\textit{\L}^{r}(\mathscr{D}_{i},V)}(t_{i}(y))^{u}M_{(\frac{r+\varepsilon}{u})^{\prime}}g_{i}(y)\quad\text{since }\sum_{Q\in\mathscr{D}_{i-M}}{\bf 1}_{3Q}\lesssim 1,\end{split}

where
M_{s}g_{i}(y):=\sup_{Q\owns y}\|g_{i}\|_{\textit{\L}^{s}(Q)}
.
Therefore, from
r+\varepsilon>r\geq p
and (
17.14
), it follows that

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &\displaystyle{\Big(\frac{r+\varepsilon}{u}\Big)^{\prime}<\Big(\frac{p}{u}\Big)^{\prime}}\qquad\qquad\qquad\text{if $A=B$},\\
&\displaystyle{\Big(\frac{r+\varepsilon}{u}\Big)^{\prime}<\min\Big\{\Big(\frac{p}{u}\Big)^{\prime},\Big(\frac{q}{u}\Big)^{\prime}\Big\}}\quad\hskip 4.83691pt\text{if $A=F$}.\end{aligned}\right.

Thus, from the boundedness of the maximal operator (for the case
A=B
)
or the Fefferman–Stein vector-valued maximal inequality (for the case
A=F
),
we further obtain

\displaystyle\operatorname{LHS}\eqref{bdd-AD-ple1-e5b}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sup 2^{\frac{M(\beta-n)}{r}}\Bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\rho_{\textit{\L}^{r}(\mathscr{D}_{i},V)}(t_{i}(y))^{u}M_{(\frac{r+\varepsilon}{u})^{\prime}}g_{i}(y)\,dy\Bigg)^{\frac{1}{u}}

by (
17.13
), (
17.16
), and (
17.17
)

\displaystyle\leq\sup 2^{\frac{M(\beta-n)}{r}}\Big\|\Big\{\rho_{\textit{\L}^{r}(\mathscr{D}_{i},V)}(t_{i})^{u}\Big\}_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\Big\|_{(L^{\frac{p}{u}}\ell^{\frac{q}{u}})_{\hat{A}}}^{\frac{1}{u}}\Big\|\Big\{M_{(\frac{r+\varepsilon}{u})^{\prime}}g_{i}\Big\}_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\Big\|_{(L^{(\frac{p}{u})^{\prime}}\ell^{(\frac{q}{u})^{\prime}})_{\hat{A}}}^{\frac{1}{u}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sup 2^{\frac{M(\beta-n)}{r}}\Big\|\Big\{\rho_{\textit{\L}^{r}(\mathscr{D}_{i},V)}(t_{i})\Big\}_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\Big\|_{(L^{p}\ell^{q})_{\hat{A}}}\|\{g_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\|_{(L^{(\frac{p}{u})^{\prime}}\ell^{(\frac{q}{u})^{\prime}})_{\hat{A}}}^{\frac{1}{u}}

by the relevant maximal inequality in each case

\displaystyle=2^{\frac{M(\beta-n)}{r}}\Big\|\Big\{\rho_{\textit{\L}^{r}(\mathscr{D}_{i},V)}(t_{i})\Big\}_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\Big\|_{(L^{p}\ell^{q})_{\hat{A}}},

since the supremum coming from (
17.13
) is over
\|\{g_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\|_{(L^{(\frac{p}{u})^{\prime}}\ell^{(\frac{q}{u})^{\prime}})_{\hat{A}}}\leq 1
. The previous inequality is precisely (
17.12
), which completes the proof of Theorem
17.12
.
∎

Remark 17.14
.

Combining
[
11
, Remark 5.5]
and
the proof of Theorem
17.12
, we can actually obtain
the following stronger conclusion:
under the assumptions of Theorem
17.12
,
the series
\sum_{R\in\mathscr{D}}b_{QR}t_{R}

is absolutely convergent in
\mathfrak{X}
for all

Q\in\mathscr{D}
and all sequences

t=\{t_{R}\}_{R\in\mathscr{D}}\in\dot{a}^{s}_{p,q}(V)
.

To prove this, suppose that the notation
is as in the proof of Theorem
17.12
.

[
11
, Remark 5.5]
claims that, when
\mathfrak{X}=\mathbb{C}^{m}
,

(
17.10
)
can be improved with the
left-hand side replaced by

(17.18)

\displaystyle\Big\|\Bigg\{\sum_{Q\in\mathscr{D}_{j}}\sum_{R\in\mathscr{D}}\|Vb_{QR}t_{R}\|_{\mathfrak{X}}\frac{{\bf 1}_{Q}}{|Q|^{\frac{1}{2}}}\Bigg\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\Big\|_{(L^{p}\ell^{q})_{A}}^{\nu}.

But the proof of this claim does not
use any special property of finite-dimensional spaces
and hence this estimate still holds for general

\mathfrak{X}
. Hence, by the proof of Theorem
17.12
,
we find that (
17.18
) is estimated by

\|t\|_{\dot{a}^{s}_{p,q}(V)}^{v}<\infty.

This means that, for almost every
x\in Q
,

\sum_{R\in\mathscr{D}}\|V(x)b_{QR}t_{R}\|_{\mathfrak{X}}<\infty
.
From this, we further infer that, for almost every
x\in Q
,

\displaystyle\sum_{R\in\mathscr{D}}\|b_{QR}t_{R}\|_{\mathfrak{X}}\leq\|V^{-1}(x)\|_{\mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{X})}\sum_{R\in\mathscr{D}}\|V(x)b_{QR}t_{R}\|_{\mathfrak{X}}<\infty,

which shows the absolute convergence of
\sum_{R\in\mathscr{D}}b_{QR}t_{R}
.
We will use this conclusion later.

17.3.
Sharpness of the assumptions

We now claim that the assumptions on

(D,E,F)
in Theorem
17.12
are optimal in some sense.
Note that the key to show Theorem
17.12
is
(
17.11
).
Motivated by Example
11.1
, we now
prove that the estimate (
17.11
)
cannot be improved in the infinite-dimensional case
and hence, in this sense,
the assumptions in Theorem
17.12
are optimal.

Proposition 17.15
.

Let
p\in(1,\infty)
and
u\in(0,1]
. If
there exists
a\in\mathbb{R}
such that, for any

V\in\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R};\mathscr{L}(\ell^{p}))

with doubling dimension
\beta\in(1,p)
and for any
t\in\dot{b}^{0}_{p,p}(V,\mathbb{R})
and
M\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
,

(17.19)

\displaystyle\Bigg\|\Bigg[\fint_{B(\cdot,2^{M-i})}\|V(\cdot)t_{i}(y)\|_{\ell^{p}}^{u}\,dy\Bigg]^{\frac{1}{u}}\Bigg\|_{L^{p}\ell^{p}}\lesssim 2^{Ma}\|t\|_{\dot{b}^{0}_{p,p}(V,\mathbb{R})},

then
a\geq\frac{\beta-1}{p}
.

Proof.

Let
v(x):=|x|^{\frac{\beta-1}{p}}
for any
x\in\mathbb{R}
.
Then, since
\beta\in(1,p)
, we infer
v\in\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R})
.
For any
j\in\mathbb{N}
and
k=0,\ldots,2^{j}-1
, define

\displaystyle x_{2^{j}+k}:=2^{-j}k\quad\text{and}\quad\lambda_{2^{j}+k}:=2^{\frac{j(\beta-1)}{p}}j^{-1}.

Let
v_{i}(\cdot):=v(\cdot-x_{i})
and
V:=\operatorname{diag}(v_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
.
Then, by (
11.2
), we find that

V\in\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R};\mathscr{L}(\ell^{p}))
.
Moreover, using
[
10
, Lemma 2.40]
, we conclude that,
for any
i\in\mathbb{N}
,
x\in\mathbb{R}
, and
r\in(0,\infty)
,

\displaystyle\int_{B(x,r)}v_{i}(y)^{p}\,dy\approx r(r+|x-x_{i}|)^{\beta-1}.

Since
\beta>1
, this
implies that, for any
\lambda\in[1,\infty)
,

\displaystyle\int_{B(x,\lambda r)}v_{i}(y)^{p}\,dy

\displaystyle\approx\lambda r(\lambda r+|x-x_{i}|)^{\beta-1}\leq\lambda^{\beta}r(r+|x-x_{i}|)^{\beta-1}

\displaystyle\approx\lambda^{\beta}\int_{B(x,r)}v_{i}(y)^{p}\,dy.

Hence, by (
6.5
),
v_{i}
has doubling dimension
\beta
.
Then it follows that, for any cubes
Q\subset S
and any
\mathfrak{e}=\{e_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\in\ell^{p}

\displaystyle\int_{S}\|V(y)\mathfrak{e}\|_{\ell^{p}}^{p}\,dy

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}|e_{i}|^{p}\int_{S}v_{i}(y)^{p}\,dy\lesssim\Big(\frac{\ell(S)}{\ell(Q)}\Big)^{\beta}\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}|e_{i}|^{p}\int_{Q}v_{i}(y)^{p}\,dy

\displaystyle=\Big(\frac{\ell(S)}{\ell(Q)}\Big)^{\beta}\int_{Q}\|V(y)\mathfrak{e}\|_{\ell^{p}}^{p}\,dy,

which implies that
V
also has doubling dimension
\beta
.

For any
i\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\mathfrak{e}_{i}:=(0,\ldots,0,1,0,\ldots)
,
where the
i
-th element is 1 and all the others are 0.
For any
Q\in\mathscr{D}
, if
Q=Q_{j,k}:=2^{-j}[k,k+1)
for some
j\geq 3
and
k=0,\ldots,2^{j}-1
,
define

\displaystyle t_{Q}:=2^{-\frac{j}{2}}\lambda_{2^{j}+k}\sum_{\ell=0,\ldots,2^{j}-1,|\ell-k|\leq 4}\mathfrak{e}_{2^{j}+\ell};

otherwise, define
t_{Q}:={\bf 0}
(the zero element of
\ell^{p}
).
Then
t:=\{t_{Q}\}_{Q\in\mathscr{D}}\in\dot{b}^{0}_{p,p}(V,\mathbb{R})
.
Indeed,

(17.20)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|t\|_{\dot{b}^{0}_{p,p}(V,\mathbb{R})}^{p}&=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{Q\in\mathscr{D}_{j}}|Q|^{-\frac{p}{2}}\int_{Q}\|V(x)t_{Q}\|_{\ell^{p}}^{p}\,dx\\
&=\sum_{j=3}^{\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{2^{j}-1}\lambda_{2^{j}+k}^{p}\sum_{\ell=0,\ldots,2^{j}-1,|\ell-k|\leq 4}\int_{2^{-j}k}^{2^{-j}(k+1)}v(x-2^{-j}\ell)^{p}\,dx\\
&=\sum_{j=3}^{\infty}2^{j(\beta-1)}j^{-p}\sum_{k=0}^{2^{j}-1}\sum_{\ell=0,\ldots,2^{j}-1,|\ell-k|\leq 4}\int_{2^{-j}k}^{2^{-j}(k+1)}|x-2^{-j}\ell|^{p}\,dx.\end{split}

Observe that, for any
k,\ell=0,\ldots,2^{j}-1
with
|k-\ell|\leq 4
,

\displaystyle\int_{2^{-j}k}^{2^{-j}(k+1)}|x-2^{-j}\ell|^{p}\,dx\lesssim\int_{2^{-j}k}^{2^{-j}(k+1)}[|x-2^{-j}k|^{\beta-1}+2^{-j(\beta-1)}|k-\ell|^{\beta-1}]\,dx\lesssim 2^{-j\beta}.

Thus, by (
17.20
), we obtain

\displaystyle\|t\|_{\dot{b}^{0}_{p,p}(V,\mathbb{R})}^{p}\lesssim\sum_{j=3}^{\infty}2^{j(\beta-1)}j^{-p}2^{j}2^{-j\beta}=\sum_{j=3}^{\infty}j^{-p}<\infty,

which implies
t\in\dot{b}^{0}_{p,p}(V,\mathbb{R})
.

On the other hand, for every
M\in\mathbb{N}\cap[3,\infty)
,

(17.21)

\operatorname{LHS}(\ref {sharp-ade1})\gtrsim\Bigg\{\int_{0}^{1}\Bigg[\int_{0}^{1}\|V(x)t_{M}(y)\|_{\ell^{p}}^{u}\,dy\Bigg]^{\frac{p}{u}}\,dx\Bigg\}^{\frac{1}{p}}.

We now estimate
\|V(x)t_{M}(y)\|_{\ell^{p}}
.
Note that, for any
x,y\in[0,1)
,

(17.22)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|V(x)t_{M}(y)\|_{\ell^{p}}^{p}&=\sum_{k=0}^{2^{M}-1}\lambda_{2^{M}+k}^{p}\Big\|V(x)\sum_{\ell=0,\ldots,2^{M}-1,|\ell-k|\leq 4}\mathfrak{e}_{2^{M}+\ell}\Big\|_{\ell^{p}}^{p}{\bf 1}_{Q_{j,k}}(y)\\
&=2^{M(\beta-1)}M^{-p}\sum_{k=0}^{2^{M}-1}\Big[\sum_{\ell=0,\ldots,2^{M}-1,|\ell-k|\leq 4}|x-2^{-M}\ell|^{p(\beta-1)}\Big]{\bf 1}_{Q_{j,k}}(y).\end{split}

Fix
y\in Q_{j,k}
,
if
|x-y|\geq 2^{-M+1}
, then

\displaystyle|x-2^{-M}k|\geq|x-y|-|y-2^{-M}k|\geq|x-y|-2^{-M}\geq\frac{1}{2}|x-y|;

if
|x-y|<2^{-M+1}
, then choose
\ell=0,\ldots,2^{M}-1

satisfying
|\ell-k|=4
, which further implies

\displaystyle|x-2^{-M}\ell|

\displaystyle\geq|2^{-M}\ell-2^{-M}k|-|2^{-M}k-y|-|y-x|

\displaystyle\geq 2^{-M}(|\ell-k|-1)-|x-y|=3\cdot 2^{-M}-|x-y|>\frac{1}{2}|x-y|.

Therefore, by (
17.22
), we obtain, for any
x,y\in[0,1)
,

\displaystyle\|V(x)t_{M}(y)\|_{\ell^{p}}^{p}\gtrsim 2^{M(\beta-1)}M^{-p}|x-y|^{p(\beta-1)}.

Then we have

\displaystyle 2^{Ma}

\displaystyle\approx\operatorname{RHS}\eqref{sharp-ade1}\gtrsim\operatorname{LHS}\eqref{sharp-ade1}

\displaystyle\gtrsim 2^{M\frac{(\beta-1)}{p}}M^{-1}\Bigg\{\int_{0}^{1}\Bigg[\int_{0}^{1}|x-y|^{u(\beta-1)}\,dy\Bigg]^{\frac{p}{u}}\,dx\Bigg\}^{\frac{1}{p}}\quad\text{by \eqref{sharp-ade3}}

\displaystyle\approx 2^{M\frac{(\beta-1)}{p}}M^{-1}.

Thus, letting
M\to\infty
, we find
a\geq\frac{\beta-1}{p}
,
which completes the proof of Proposition
17.15
.
∎

Part IV
Abstract discretizable spaces

18.
An axiomatic framework and examples

In this remaining part of this work, we extend various further results from the theory of (unweighted, weighted, or matrix-weighted) Besov and Triebel–Lizorkin spaces to the operator-weighted spaces that we have considered.
Having obtained the
\varphi
-transform characterization
and the boundedness of almost diagonal operators on a given function space,
many further real-variable properties can be established in an axiomatic way.
For this purpose, we introduce the following framework.

Definition 18.1
.

Let
d
,
e
, and
f
be three real numbers.
A quasi-normed space
\dot{\mathbf{A}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})\subset\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})
is
called a
homogeneous
(d,e,f)
-discretizable space
if
the following three conditions hold:

(i)

\mathscr{S}_{\infty}\otimes\mathfrak{X}\subset\dot{\mathbf{A}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})
,
where
\mathscr{S}_{\infty}\otimes\mathfrak{X}
is as in (
15.7
).

(ii)

There exists a quasi-norm space
\dot{\mathbf{a}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})

consisting of sequences
t=\{t_{Q}\}_{Q\in\mathcal{D}}
, where each
t_{Q}\in\mathfrak{X}
,
such that, for any Littlewood–Paley
functions
\varphi,\psi
that satisfy (
15.3
),

S_{\varphi}:\dot{\mathbf{A}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})\to\dot{\mathbf{a}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})\quad\text{and}\quad T_{\psi}:\dot{\mathbf{a}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})\to\dot{\mathbf{A}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})

are bounded and
T_{\psi}\circ S_{\varphi}
acts as the identity on
\dot{\mathbf{A}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})
.

(iii)

For any triple
(D,E,F)
that satisfies
D>d
,
E>e
, and
F>f
and for any
(D,E,F)
-almost diagonal operator
B
,
the series
\sum_{R\in\mathscr{D}}b_{QR}t_{R}

is absolutely convergent in
\mathfrak{X}
for all

Q\in\mathscr{D}
and all sequences

t=\{t_{R}\}_{R\in\mathscr{D}}\in\dot{\mathbf{a}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})
,
and
B
is bounded on
\dot{\mathbf{a}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})
.

Lemma 18.2
.

Let
p\in(0,\infty)
,
q\in(0,\infty]
, and
s\in\mathbb{R}
.

(1)

If
\rho=\{\rho_{Q}\}_{Q\in\mathscr{D}}

is a family of
(u,a,b,c)
-norms (Definition
15.1
) with

u\in(0,1]
and
a,b,c\in[0,\infty)
,
then

\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho)
is
(d,e,f)
-discretizable with

d=J^{(u)}+c,\quad e=\frac{n}{2}+s+a,\quad f=J^{(u)}-\frac{n}{2}-s+b,

where
J^{(u)}
is as in (
17.3
).

(2)

If
V\in\mathscr{A}_{r}
with
r\in[p,\infty)
has doubling dimension
\beta\in[n,\infty)
and optimal reverse Hölder lifting index
\varepsilon_{V}>0
, then

\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(V)=\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho_{\textit{\L}^{r}(\mathscr{D},V)}),\quad\text{where}\quad q\in\begin{cases}(0,\infty]&\text{if }A=B,\\
(0,r+\varepsilon_{V})&\text{if }A=F,\end{cases}

is
(d,e,f)
-discretizable with

d=J+\frac{\beta-n}{r},\quad e=\frac{n}{2}+s,\quad f=J-\frac{n}{2}-s+\frac{\beta-n}{r},

where
\varepsilon_{V}
is the optimal reverse Hölder lifting index of
V
and
J
is as in (
17.2
).

Proof.

Part (
1
) follows from Theorems
15.3
and
17.5
and Remark
17.10
, and part (
2
) from Theorems
15.4
and

17.12
and Remark
17.14
.
∎

In the next Section
19
, we will prove a characterization of
(d,e,f)
-discretizable spaces in terms of so-called molecules. We already introduce the relevant concepts here, to show how the various parameters of molecules and discretizable spaces interact with each other.

We first recall some notation related to integer and fractional parts of numbers.
For
r\in\mathbb{R}
, let

(18.1)

\begin{cases}\lfloor r\rfloor:=\max\{k\in\mathbb{Z}:\ k\leq r\},\\
\lfloor\!\lfloor r\rfloor\!\rfloor:=\max\{k\in\mathbb{Z}:\ k<r\},\end{cases}\begin{cases}\lceil r\rceil:=\min\{k\in\mathbb{Z}:\ k\geq r\},\\
\lceil\!\lceil r\rceil\!\rceil:=\min\{k\in\mathbb{Z}:\ k>r\},\end{cases}

and

(18.2)

\begin{cases}r^{*}:=r-\lfloor r\rfloor\in[0,1),\\
r^{**}:=r-\lfloor\!\lfloor r\rfloor\!\rfloor\in(0,1].\end{cases}

We introduce the concept of molecules as follows; see, for instance,
[
12
, Definition 3.4]
.
Since we are dealing with space of
\mathfrak{X}
-valued distributions, it might appear natural to also make the molecules
\mathfrak{X}
-valued. However, we will only need scalar-valued molecules; with
\mathfrak{X}
-valued coefficients, they will serve as building blocks of
\mathfrak{X}
-valued distributions in the molecular characterization further below.

Definition 18.3
.

Let
K,M\in[0,\infty)
and
L,N\in\mathbb{R}
.
A function
m_{Q}
is called a

(K,L,M,N)
-molecule on a cube
Q
if

(i)

(size)
for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

|m_{Q}(x)|\leq|Q|^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Big[1+\frac{|x-x_{Q}|}{\ell(Q)}\Big]^{-K},

(ii)

(cancellation)
for any
\gamma\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{n}

satisfying
|\gamma|\leq L
,

(18.3)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}m_{Q}(x)x^{\gamma}\,dx=0,

(iii)

(smoothness)
for any
\gamma\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{n}
satisfying

|\gamma|\leq\lfloor\!\lfloor N\rfloor\!\rfloor

and for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

\displaystyle|\partial^{\gamma}m_{Q}(x)|\leq|Q|^{-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{|\gamma|}{n}}\Big[1+\frac{|x-x_{Q}|}{\ell(Q)}\Big]^{-M}

and, for any
\gamma\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{n}
satisfying
|\gamma|=\lfloor\!\lfloor N\rfloor\!\rfloor

and for any
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

\displaystyle|\partial^{\gamma}m_{Q}(x)-\partial^{\gamma}m_{Q}(y)|

\displaystyle\leq|Q|^{-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{|\gamma|}{n}}\Big[\frac{|x-y|}{\ell(Q)}\Big]^{N^{**}}\sup_{|z|\leq|x-y|}\Big[1+\frac{|x+z-x_{Q}|}{\ell(Q)}\Big]^{-M}.

Definition 18.4
.

Let
\dot{\mathbf{A}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})

be a homogeneous
(d,e,f)
-discretizable space with parameters
(d,e,f)
.
Assume that
m_{Q}
is
a
(K,L,M,N)
-molecule on a cube
Q
.
Then
m_{Q}
is called

(i)

an
\dot{\mathbf{a}}
-analysis
molecule
on
Q
if

K>\max\big\{n,d,e+\frac{n}{2}\big\},\quad L>e-\frac{n}{2},\quad M>\max\{d,n\},\quad N>f-\frac{n}{2};

(ii)

an
\dot{\mathbf{a}}
-synthesis
molecule
on
Q
if

K>\max\big\{n,d,f+\frac{n}{2}\big\},\quad L>f-\frac{n}{2},\quad M>\max\{d,n\},\quad N>e-\frac{n}{2}.

Moreover, when we talk about a family of

\dot{\mathbf{a}}
-analysis or synthesis molecules, we
understand that they should be the
(K,L,,M,N)
-molecules for some fixed

(K,L,M,N)
that satisfies the condition above.

Example 18.5
.

Let
p\in(0,\infty)
,
q\in(0,\infty]
,
and
s\in\mathbb{R}
.
By Lemma
18.2
, we conclude that,
under some assumptions, the average-weighted space
\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho)

and the pointwise-weighted space

\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(V)
are
(d,e,f)
-discretizable spaces
for some
d
,
e
, and
f
. Hence, we obtain the following special cases of Definition
18.4
:

(i)

Let
\rho=\{\rho_{Q}\}_{Q\in\mathscr{D}}

be a family of
(u,a,b,c)
-norms with

u\in(0,1]
and
a,b,c\in[0,\infty)
.
From Lemma
18.2
(
1
),
one can choose

(18.4)

\displaystyle d=J^{(u)}+c,\quad e=\frac{n}{2}+s+a,\quad f=J^{(u)}-\frac{n}{2}-s+b,

where
J^{(u)}
is as in (
17.3
).
Then a
(K,L,M,N)
-molecule on a cube
Q
is

•

an
\dot{a}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho)
-analysis molecule
if
(K,L,M,N)
satisfies Definition

18.4
(
i
) for
(d,e,f)
as in (
18.4
), i.e.,

\begin{cases}K>\max(n,J^{(u)}+c,n+s+a),&L>s+a,\\
M>\max(n,J^{(u)}+c),&N>J^{(u)}-n-s+b;\end{cases}

•

an
\dot{a}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho)
-synthesis molecule
if
(K,L,M,N)
satisfies Definition

18.4
(
ii
) for
(d,e,f)
as in (
18.4
), i.e.,

\begin{cases}K>\max(n,J^{(u)}+c,J^{(u)}-s+b),&L>J^{(u)}-n-s+b,\\
M>\max(n,J^{(u)}+c),&N>s+a.\end{cases}

(ii)

Let
V\in\mathscr{A}_{r}
with
r\in[p,\infty)
and the doubling
dimension
\beta\in[n,\infty)
, and let
q\in(0,\varepsilon_{V})

when
A=F
, where
\varepsilon_{V}
is
the optimal reverse Hölder lifting index of
V
.
Lemma
18.2
(
2
) shows that
one can choose

(18.5)

\displaystyle d=J+\frac{\beta-n}{r},\quad e=\frac{n}{2}+s,\quad f=J-\frac{n}{2}-s+\frac{\beta-n}{r},

where
J
is as in (
17.2
).
Then a
(K,L,M,N)
-molecule on a cube
Q
is

•

an
\dot{a}^{s}_{p,q}(V)=\dot{a}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho_{\textit{\L}^{r}(\mathscr{D},V)})
-analysis molecule
if
(K,L,M,N)
satisfies Definition

18.4
(
i
) for
(d,e,f)
as in (
18.5
), i.e.,

\begin{cases}K>\max(n,J+\frac{\beta-n}{r},n+s),&L>s,\\
M>\max(n,J+\frac{\beta-n}{r}),&N>(J-n)-s+\frac{\beta-n}{r};\end{cases}

•

an
\dot{a}^{s}_{p,q}(V)=\dot{a}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho_{\textit{\L}^{r}(\mathscr{D},V)})
-analysis molecule
if
(K,L,M,N)
satisfies Definition

18.4
(
ii
) for
(d,e,f)
as in (
18.5
), i.e.,

\begin{cases}K>\max(n,J+\frac{\beta-n}{r},J-s+\frac{\beta-n}{r}),&L>(J-n)-s+\frac{\beta-n}{r},\\
M>\max(n,J+\frac{\beta-n}{r}),&N>s.\end{cases}

For later convenience, we record the following simple observation:

Remark 18.6
.

If
f-\frac{n}{2}<0
, then
\dot{\mathbf{a}}
-synthesis molecules are
not required to have any cancellation conditions (
18.3
). Indeed, in this case, Definition
18.4
(
ii
) allows us to take
L\in(f-\frac{n}{2},0)
so that the range of multi-indices in Definition
18.3
(
ii
) is empty.
By Example
18.5
,
for the average-weighted space
\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho)

and the pointwise-weighted space
\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(V)
introduced in
Definition
12.3
,
the condition
f-\frac{n}{2}<0
requires that the smoothness parameter
s
is sufficiently large,
i.e.,

(18.6)

\begin{cases}s>J^{(u)}-n+b&\text{for}\quad\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho),\\
s>J-n+\frac{\beta-n}{r}&\text{for}\quad\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(V),\end{cases}

where all the assumptions are as in
Example
18.5
.

19.
Molecular characterization

The goal of this section is to establish the molecular characterization of operator-weighted Besov and Triebel–Lizorkin spaces within the abstract framework of
(d,e,f)
-discretizable spaces from Definition
18.1
.
Before we state the molecular characterization,
we need to show that certain dual pairings
\langle f,\Phi\rangle
are well defined.

Proposition 19.1
.

Let
\dot{\mathbf{A}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})

be a homogeneous
(d,e,f)
-discretizable space with some real
parameters
(d,e,f)
. If

f\in\dot{\mathbf{A}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})
and

\Phi
is an
\dot{\mathbf{a}}
-analysis
molecule on
P\in\mathscr{D}
, then, for any Littlewood–Paley
functions
\varphi,\psi
that satisfy (
15.3
), the expression

\displaystyle\langle f,\Phi\rangle:=\sum_{R\in\mathscr{D}}\left\langle f,\varphi_{R}\right\rangle\langle\psi_{R},\Phi\rangle

is well defined; that is,
the series above converges absolutely and its value is independent of the
choices of
\varphi
and
\psi
.

To prove this proposition, we need some preparations. We recall the following result from
[
12
]
:

Lemma 19.2

(
[
12
]
, Lemma 3.7)
.

Let
m_{Q}
be a
(K_{m},L_{m},M_{m},N_{m})
-molecule on a cube
Q
and let
b_{R}
be
a
(K_{b},L_{b},M_{b},N_{b})
-molecule on a cube
R
, where
K_{m},M_{m},K_{b},M_{b}>n
.
Then the matrix
\{\langle m_{Q},b_{R}\rangle\}_{Q,R\in\mathscr{D}}
is
(D,E,F)
-almost diagonal with

\begin{split}D&=\min\{K_{m},M_{m},K_{b},M_{b}\},\\
E&=\tfrac{n}{2}+\min\{N_{b},L_{m},(K_{m}-n-\alpha)\}_{+},\\
F&=\tfrac{n}{2}+\min\{N_{m},L_{b},(K_{b}-n-\alpha)\}_{+},\end{split}

where
\alpha>0
can be chosen as we wish.

Lemma 19.3
.

Let
\dot{\mathbf{A}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})

be a homogeneous
(d,e,f)
-discretizable space with some real
parameters
(d,e,f)
and let
\varphi,\psi
be Littlewood–Paley functions.
Let
\{m_{Q}\}_{Q\in\mathscr{D}}
and
\{b_{Q}\}_{Q\in\mathscr{D}}
be families of
\dot{\mathbf{a}}
-analysis and
\dot{\mathbf{a}}
-synthesis molecules, respectively. Then both matrices

(i)

\{\langle m_{Q},\psi_{R}\rangle\}_{Q,R\in\mathscr{D}}

(ii)

\{\langle\varphi_{Q},b_{R}\rangle\}_{Q,R\in\mathscr{D}}

are
(D,E,F)
-almost diagonal for some

D>\max\{d,n\},\qquad E>e,\qquad F>f.

In particular, they are bounded on
\dot{\mathbf{a}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})
.

Proof.

Once we prove the almost diagonality, the boundedness is immediate from Definition
18.1
(
iii
).

We first consider (
i
). Assume that

\{m_{Q}\}_{Q\in\mathscr{D}}
are
(K,L,M,N)
-molecules
where the parameters satisfy Definition
18.4
(
i
), i.e.,

K>\max\{n,d,e+\tfrac{n}{2}\},\quad L>e-\tfrac{n}{2},\quad M>\max\{d,n\},\quad N>f-\tfrac{n}{2}.

On the other hand, each

\psi_{R}
is a
(\widehat{K},\widehat{L},\widehat{M},\widehat{N})
-molecule
on
R
for arbitrarily large
\widehat{K},\widehat{L},\widehat{M},\widehat{N}
.
Using Lemma
19.2
with
\psi_{R}
in place of
b_{R}
‚ it follows that

\{\langle m_{Q},\psi_{R}\rangle\}_{Q,R\in\mathscr{D}}
is
(D,E,F)
-almost diagonal with

D=\min\{K,M\}>\max\{d,n\},\qquad F=N+\tfrac{n}{2}>f,

and

E=\min\{L+\tfrac{n}{2},K-\tfrac{n}{2}-\alpha\}_{+}>e,

provided that
\alpha>0
is chosen small enough.

Turning to (
ii
), we now assume that
\{b_{R}\}_{R\in\mathscr{D}}
are
(K,L,M,N)
-molecules with parameters as in Definition
18.4
(
ii
), i.e.,

K>\max\{n,d,f+\tfrac{n}{2}\},\quad L>f-\tfrac{n}{2},\quad M>\max\{d,n\},\quad N>e-\tfrac{n}{2},

while each
\varphi_{Q}
is a
(\widehat{K},\widehat{L},\widehat{M},\widehat{N})
-molecule for arbitrarily large parameters. Then, by Lemma
19.2
with
\varphi_{Q}
in place of
m_{Q}
‚ it follows that

\{\langle\varphi_{Q},b_{R}\rangle\}_{Q,R\in\mathscr{D}}
is
(D,E,F)
-almost diagonal with

D=\min\{K,M\}>\max\{d,n\},\qquad E=N+\tfrac{n}{2}>e,

and

F=\min\{L+\tfrac{n}{2},K-\tfrac{n}{2}-\alpha\}_{+}>f

provided that
\alpha>0
is chosen small enough.

This finishes the proof of Lemma
19.3
in both cases.
∎

We also need the following
estimate, whose proof is similar to that of

[
25
, Theorem D.2]
.

Lemma 19.4
.

If
D_{1},D_{2}\in(n,\infty)
and

E_{1},E_{2},F_{1},F_{2}\in\mathbb{R}

satisfy
E_{1}\neq E_{2}
,
F_{1}\neq F_{2}
,
and
E_{1}+F_{2},E_{2}+F_{1}\in(\min\{D_{1},D_{2}\},\infty)
, then,
for any
Q,R\in\mathscr{D}
,

\displaystyle\sum_{P\in\mathscr{D}}B_{-E_{1},-F_{1},-D_{1}}(Q,P)B_{-E_{2},-F_{2},-D_{2}}(P,R)\lesssim B_{-E,-F,-D}(Q,R),

where
G:=\min\{G_{1},G_{2}\}
for any
G\in\{D,E,F\}
and the
implicit positive constant is independent of
Q
and
R
.

Proof.

By the symmetry, we only consider the case
\ell(Q)\leq\ell(R)
here.
Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{P\in\mathscr{D}}B_{-E_{1},-F_{1},-D_{1}}(Q,P)B_{-E_{2},-F_{2},-D_{2}}(P,R)

\displaystyle\quad=\sum_{\{P:\ell(P)<\ell(Q)\}}\cdots+\sum_{\{P:\ell(Q)\leq\ell(P)<\ell(R)\}}\cdots+\sum_{\{P:\ell(R)\leq\ell(P)\}}\cdots

\displaystyle\quad=:{\rm I}+{\rm II}+{\rm III}.

First, we deal with
{\rm I}
. From the definition of
B
, we deduce that

(19.1)

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\rm I}&\lesssim\sum_{2^{-j}<\ell(Q)}\sum_{P\in\mathscr{D}_{j}}\left[\frac{\ell(P)}{\ell(Q)}\right]^{F_{1}}\left[\frac{\ell(P)}{\ell(R)}\right]^{E_{2}}\\
&\qquad\times\left[1+\frac{|x_{Q}-x_{P}|}{\max\{\ell(Q),\ell(P)\}}\right]^{-D_{1}}\left[1+\frac{|x_{R}-x_{P}|}{\max\{\ell(R),\ell(P)\}}\right]^{-D_{2}}.\end{split}

Note that, by
[
25
, Lemma D.1]
, we find that, for any
j\in\mathbb{Z}
,

(19.2)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\sum_{P\in\mathscr{D}_{j}}\left[1+\frac{|x_{Q}-x_{P}|}{\max\{\ell(Q),\ell(P)\}}\right]^{-D_{1}}\left[1+\frac{|x_{R}-x_{P}|}{\max\{\ell(R),\ell(P)\}}\right]^{-D_{2}}\\
&\quad\lesssim\left[1+\frac{|x_{Q}-x_{R}|}{\max\{2^{-j},\ell(R)\}}\right]^{-D}\max\{1,2^{j}\ell(Q)\}^{n},\end{split}

where the maximum is
2^{j}\ell(Q)>1
by the summation condition in term I.

Substituting (
19.2
) back to (
19.1
), we obtain

\displaystyle{\rm I}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left[1+\frac{|x_{Q}-x_{R}|}{\max\{\ell(Q),\ell(R)\}}\right]^{-D}\sum_{2^{-j}<\ell(Q)}\left[\frac{2^{-j}}{\ell(Q)}\right]^{F_{1}}\left[\frac{2^{-j}}{\ell(R)}\right]^{E_{2}}\left[2^{j}\ell(Q)\right]^{n}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left[\frac{\ell(Q)}{\ell(R)}\right]^{E}\left[1+\frac{|x_{Q}-x_{R}|}{\max\{\ell(Q),\ell(R)\}}\right]^{-D}\qquad\text{since }F_{1}+E_{2}>D>n

\displaystyle\approx B_{-E,-F,-D}(Q,R)\qquad\text{since }\ell(Q)\leq\ell(R).

This is the desired estimate of
{\rm I}
.

Next, we focus on
{\rm II}
. Applying
the definition of
B
and (
19.2
) again and the
assumption that
E_{1}\neq E_{2}
, we obtain

\displaystyle{\rm II}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{\ell(Q)\leq 2^{-j}<\ell(R)}\sum_{P\in\mathscr{D}_{j}}\left[\frac{\ell(Q)}{\ell(P)}\right]^{E_{1}}\left[\frac{\ell(P)}{\ell(R)}\right]^{E_{2}}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\left[1+\frac{|x_{Q}-x_{P}|}{\max\{\ell(Q),\ell(P)\}}\right]^{-D_{1}}\left[1+\frac{|x_{R}-x_{P}|}{\max\{\ell(R),\ell(P)\}}\right]^{-D_{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{\ell(Q)\leq 2^{-j}<\ell(R)}\left[2^{j}\ell(Q)\right]^{E_{1}}\left[\frac{2^{-j}}{\ell(R)}\right]^{E_{2}}\left[1+\frac{|x_{Q}-x_{R}|}{\max\{2^{-j},\ell(R)\}}\right]^{-D}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left[\frac{\ell(Q)}{\ell(R)}\right]^{E}\left[1+\frac{|x_{Q}-x_{R}|}{\max\{\ell(Q),\ell(R)\}}\right]^{-D}\approx B_{-E,-F,-D}(Q,R),

which completes the estimation of
{\rm II}
.

Finally, we estimate
{\rm III}
.
Similarly to the above argument, we have

(19.3)

\displaystyle{\rm III}\lesssim\sum_{2^{-j}\geq\ell(R)}\left[2^{j}\ell(Q)\right]^{E_{1}}\left[2^{j}\ell(R)\right]^{F_{2}}\left[1+2^{j}|x_{Q}-x_{R}|\right]^{-D}.

Observe that

\displaystyle 1+2^{j}|x_{Q}-x_{R}|\geq 2^{j}\ell(R)\left[1+\frac{|x_{Q}-x_{R}|}{\max\{\ell(Q),\ell(R)\}}\right].

Substituting this into (
19.3
), we conclude that

\displaystyle{\rm III}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left[1+\frac{|x_{Q}-x_{R}|}{\max\{\ell(Q),\ell(R)\}}\right]^{-D}\sum_{2^{-j}\geq\ell(R)}\left[2^{j}\ell(Q)\right]^{E_{1}}\left[2^{j}\ell(R)\right]^{F_{2}-D}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left[\frac{\ell(Q)}{\ell(R)}\right]^{E}\left[1+\frac{|x_{Q}-x_{R}|}{\max\{\ell(Q),\ell(R)\}}\right]^{-D}\qquad\text{since }E_{1}+F_{2}>D

\displaystyle\approx B_{-E,-F,-D}(Q,R).

This finishes the estimation of
{\rm III}
and hence Lemma
19.4
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
19.1
.

Let
(\varphi_{1},\psi_{1})
and
(\varphi_{2},\psi_{2})

be two pairs of Littlewood–Paley
functions that satisfy (
15.3
).
Define
\Phi_{P}:=\Phi
and
\Phi_{S}:=0
if
S\neq P
.
Then
\{\Phi_{S}\}_{S\in\mathscr{D}}

is a family of
\dot{\mathbf{a}}
-analysis molecules.
Hence, by Lemma
19.3
, we can
choose
D_{1}>\max\{n,d\}
,
E_{1}>e
, and
F_{1}>f
such that

\{\langle\Phi_{S},\psi_{Q}^{(2)}\rangle\}_{S,Q\in\mathscr{D}}

is
(D_{1},E_{1},F_{1})
-almost diagonal. Moreover,
since
\psi^{(1)},\varphi^{(2)}\in\mathscr{S}_{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
we can choose sufficiently large
(D_{2},E_{2},F_{2})
such that the pairs

\{(D_{i},E_{i},F_{i})\}_{i=1}^{2}
satisfy all the assumptions of
Lemma
19.4
and

\{\langle\varphi_{Q}^{(2)},\psi_{R}^{(1)}\rangle\}_{Q,R\in\mathscr{D}}

is
(D_{2},E_{2},F_{2})
-almost diagonal. Thus, from
Lemma
19.4
, we infer that, for any
S,R\in\mathscr{D}
,

\displaystyle t_{SR}:=\sum_{Q\in\mathscr{D}}|\langle\Phi_{S},\psi_{Q}^{(2)}\rangle\langle\varphi_{Q}^{(2)},\psi_{R}^{(1)}\rangle|\lesssim B_{-E_{1},-F_{1},-D_{1}}(S,R).

This shows that
\{t_{SR}\}_{S,R\in\mathscr{D}}

is
(D_{1},E_{1},F_{1})
-almost diagonal.
Combining this and Definition
18.1
, we
further conclude that,
for any
S\in\mathscr{D}
and
f\in\dot{\mathbf{A}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})
,
the series
\sum_{R\in\mathscr{D}}t_{SR}\langle f,\varphi_{R}^{(1)}\rangle

is absolutely convergent in
\mathfrak{X}
; in particular,

\displaystyle\sum_{Q,R\in\mathscr{D}}\langle f,\varphi_{R}^{(1)}\rangle\langle\psi_{R}^{(1)},\varphi_{Q}^{(2)}\rangle\langle\psi_{Q}^{(2)},\Phi\rangle

is absolutely convergent.
Thus,

\displaystyle\sum_{R\in\mathscr{D}}\langle f,\varphi_{R}^{(1)}\rangle\langle\psi_{R}^{(1)},\Phi\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{R\in\mathscr{D}}\langle f,\varphi_{R}^{(1)}\rangle\Big[\sum_{Q\in\mathscr{D}}\langle\psi_{R}^{(1)},\varphi_{Q}^{(2)}\rangle\langle\psi_{Q}^{(2)},\Phi\rangle\Big]

\displaystyle=\sum_{Q\in\mathscr{D}}\Big[\sum_{R\in\mathscr{D}}\langle f,\varphi_{R}^{(1)}\rangle\langle\psi_{R}^{(1)},\varphi_{Q}^{(2)}\rangle\Big]\langle\psi_{Q}^{(2)},\Phi\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{Q\in\mathscr{D}}\langle f,\varphi_{Q}^{(2)}\rangle\langle\psi_{Q}^{(2)},\Phi\rangle.

This implies that the present lemma holds.
∎

Now, we are able to prove the
molecular characterization of

\dot{\mathbf{A}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})
.

Theorem 19.5
.

Let
\dot{\mathbf{A}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})

be a homogeneous
(d,e,f)
-discretizable space with some real
parameters
(d,e,f)
.

(i)

If
\{m_{Q}\}_{Q\in\mathscr{D}}
are

\dot{\mathbf{a}}
-analysis molecules on their indicated cubes,
then there exists a positive constant
C

such that, for any
f\in\dot{\mathbf{A}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})
,

\displaystyle\big\|\big\{\langle f,m_{Q}\rangle\big\}_{Q\in\mathscr{D}}\big\|_{\dot{\mathbf{a}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})}\leq C\|f\|_{\dot{\mathbf{A}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})}.

(ii)

If

\{b_{Q}\}_{Q\in\mathscr{D}}
are
\dot{\mathbf{a}}
-synthesis molecules on their indicated cubes
and if
t:=\{t_{Q}\}_{Q\in\mathscr{D}}\in\dot{\mathbf{a}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})
,
then

f:=\sum_{Q\in\mathscr{D}}t_{Q}b_{Q}\in\dot{\mathbf{A}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})

and
there exists a positive constant
C

such that

\|f\|_{\dot{\mathbf{A}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})}\leq C\|t\|_{\dot{\mathbf{a}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})}.

Proof.

Let
\varphi,\psi
be two Littlewood–Paley functions that satisfy (
15.3
).
We first show (
i
). From Proposition
19.1
, we infer that

\{\langle f,m_{Q}\rangle\}_{Q\in\mathscr{D}}

is well defined. Using the definition, we obtain,
for any
Q\in\mathscr{D}
,

\displaystyle\langle f,m_{Q}\rangle=\sum_{R\in\mathscr{D}}\langle f,\varphi_{R}\rangle\langle\psi_{R},m_{Q}\rangle=\sum_{R\in\mathscr{D}}\langle m_{Q},\psi_{R}\rangle(S_{\varphi}f)_{R}.

Moreover, by Lemma
19.3
(
i
), we find that

B:=\{\langle m_{Q},\psi_{R}\rangle\}_{Q,R\in\mathscr{D}}

is bounded on
\dot{\mathbf{a}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})
.
Consequently,
it follows that

\displaystyle\big\|\big\{\langle f,m_{Q}\rangle\big\}_{Q\in\mathscr{D}}\big\|_{\dot{\mathbf{a}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})}

\displaystyle=\|B\circ S_{\varphi}f\|_{\dot{\mathbf{a}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})}\lesssim\|S_{\varphi}f\|_{\dot{\mathbf{a}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|f\|_{\dot{\mathbf{A}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})}\qquad\text{by Definition \ref {def:seq}\eqref{def:seq-it1}},

which confirms (
i
).

Next, we prove (
ii
). From Lemma
19.3
(
ii
),
we deduce that the matrix

\widetilde{B}:=\{\langle\varphi_{Q},b_{R}\rangle\}_{Q,R\in\mathscr{D}}

is
(D,E,F)
-almost diagonal for some
D>d
,
E>e
, and
F>f

and hence, by Definition
18.1
(
iii
),
for any
Q\in\mathscr{D}
,
the series
\sum_{R\in\mathscr{D}}\langle\varphi_{Q},b_{R}\rangle t_{R}

is absolutely convergent.
Therefore, for any
Q\in\mathscr{D}
,

\displaystyle(S_{\varphi}f)_{Q}=\langle f,\varphi_{Q}\rangle=(\widetilde{B}t)_{Q}.

Combining this and Definition
18.1
again,
we further obtain

\displaystyle\|f\|_{\dot{\mathbf{A}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|S_{\varphi}f\|_{\dot{\mathbf{a}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})}=\|\widetilde{B}t\|_{\dot{\mathbf{a}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})}\lesssim\|t\|_{\dot{\mathbf{a}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})}.

This finishes the proof of (
ii
) and hence Theorem
19.5
.
∎

By Lemma
18.2
and Example
18.5
,
we immediately obtain the following applications of Theorem
19.5
,
which provide the molecular characterizations of
the Besov and the Triebel–Lizorkin spaces introduced in Definition
12.3
;
we omit the straightforward details.

Corollary 19.6
.

Let
p\in(0,\infty)
,
q\in(0,\infty]
,
and
s\in\mathbb{R}
.

(i)

If
\rho=\{\rho_{Q}\}_{Q\in\mathscr{D}}

is a family of
(u,a,b,c)
-norms with

u\in(0,1]
and
a,b,c\in[0,\infty)
, then
Theorem
19.5
applies to the average-weighted space
\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho)

and the
\dot{a}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho)
-analysis and synthesis molecules.

(ii)

If
V\in\mathscr{A}_{r}
with
r\in[p,\infty)
has doubling
dimension
\beta\in[n,\infty)
and if
q\in(0,r+\varepsilon_{V})

when
A=F
, where
\varepsilon_{V}
is
the optimal reverse Hölder lifting index of
V
,
then Theorem
19.5
applies to the space
