\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(V)=\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho_{\textit{\L}^{r}(\mathscr{D},V)})

and the
\dot{a}^{s}_{p,q}(V)=\dot{a}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho_{\textit{\L}^{r}(\mathscr{D},V)})
-analysis and synthesis molecules.

20.
Wavelet characterization

In this section, we first establish the wavelet
characterization of the abstract space
\dot{\mathbf{A}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})

and, as an application, we give the
wavelet characterization of Besov and Triebel–Lizorkin spaces
introduced in Definition
12.3
.

To begin with, we recall some notation
on the Daubechies wavelet system (see, for example,

[
51
,
68
]
). Take a univariate scaling function

\varphi\in C_{\rm c}^{N}(\mathbb{R})
and an associated univariate
wavelet function
\psi\in C_{\rm c}^{N}(\mathbb{R})
for some

N\in\mathbb{N}
and define

\psi^{(0)}:=\varphi
and
\psi^{(1)}:=\psi
. For any
\lambda\in\{0,1\}^{n}

and
x:=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
let
\Psi^{(\lambda)}(x):=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\psi^{(\lambda_{i})}(x_{i})
.
Also, assume that, for any
\lambda\in\Lambda_{n}:=\{0,1\}^{n}\setminus\{{\bf 0}\}

and
\alpha:=(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n})\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{n}
with
|\alpha|:=|\alpha_{1}|+\cdots+|\alpha_{n}|\leq N
,

(20.1)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}x^{\alpha}\Psi^{(\lambda)}(x)\,dx=0.

For any
\lambda\in\{0,1\}^{n}
,
Q\in\mathscr{D}
, and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
define

\displaystyle\Psi^{(\lambda)}_{Q}(x):=|Q|^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Psi^{(\lambda)}\Big(\frac{x-x_{Q}}{\ell(Q)}\Big).

In many considerations, we only deal with the cancellative wavelets corresponding to
\lambda\in\Lambda_{n}
, but it is convenient to have the non-cancellative wavelets with
\lambda={\bf 0}
also included in the definition.

For brevity, let
\Omega_{n}:=\Lambda_{n}\times\mathscr{D}
and,
for any
\omega=(\lambda,Q)\in\Omega_{n}
,
define
\lambda_{\omega}:=\lambda
,
Q_{\omega}:=Q
, and

\Psi_{\omega}:=\Psi^{(\lambda)}_{Q}
.
It is well known that these functions can be constructed so that, in addition to the properties stated,

\{\Psi_{\omega}\}_{\omega\in\Omega_{n}}

forms a complete orthonormal basis of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
The number
N
is called the
order
of this wavelet system.

The following proposition is a
consequence of the definitions
of molecules (namely Definition
18.4
) and wavelets and Proposition
19.1
.

Proposition 20.1
.

Let
\dot{\mathbf{A}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})

be a
(d,e,f)
-discretizable space for some real parameters

(d,e,f)
.
Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
satisfy

(20.2)

N>\max\{e,f\}-\frac{n}{2},

and let
\Psi^{(\lambda)}_{Q}
be Daubechies wavelets of order
N
.

(i)

The wavelet
\Psi_{Q}^{(\lambda)}

is a constant multiple of both an
\dot{\mathbf{a}}
-analysis molecule and
an
\dot{\mathbf{a}}
-synthesis molecule on
Q

for all
\lambda\in\Lambda_{n}
and
Q\in\mathscr{D}
.

(ii)

If
f-\frac{n}{2}<0
, then

\Psi_{Q}^{\lambda}
is a constant multiple of an
\dot{\mathbf{a}}
-synthesis molecule on
Q

for all
\lambda\in\{0,1\}^{n}
and
Q\in\mathscr{D}
.

(iii)

If
\varphi,\psi
are Littlewood–Paley
functions satisfying (
15.3
),
then, for any
f\in\dot{\mathbf{A}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})

and
\omega\in\Omega_{n}
,

\left\langle f,\Psi_{\omega}\right\rangle:=\sum_{R\in\mathscr{Q}}\left\langle f,\varphi_{R}\right\rangle\left\langle\psi_{R},\Psi_{\omega}\right\rangle

is well defined; that is, the series converges absolutely
and its value is independent of the choice of
\varphi
and
\psi
.

Note the difference between cases (
i
) and (
ii
): The latter makes an additional assumption (
18.6
) and only applies to synthesis molecules, but then allows the full range of
\lambda\in\{0,1\}^{n}
, including the non-cancellative case
\lambda={\bf 0}
, which is excluded by the assumption that
\lambda\in\Lambda_{n}
in case (
i
).

Proof of Proposition
20.1
.

We first consider (
i
).
By Definitions
18.4
and
18.3
,
\dot{\mathbf{a}}
-analysis and

\dot{\mathbf{a}}
-synthesis molecules are

(K,L,M,N)
-molecules with sufficiently large values of the parameters,
as specified in Definition
18.4
.

From
\varphi,\psi\in C_{\rm c}^{N}(\mathbb{R})
and the way that
\Psi_{Q}^{(\lambda)}
is formed by scaling and translation, the molecular size condition (Definition
18.3
(
i
)) holds with
K
as large as we want, and the molecular smoothness condition (Definition
18.3
(
iii
) holds with the same
N
as the order of the wavelets and arbitrarily large
M
. Hence, both
K
and
M
(trivially) and
N
(by assumption (
20.2
)) are sufficiently large for both types of molecules.

For
\lambda\in\Lambda_{n}
, assumption (
20.1
) shows that the molecular cancellation condition (Definition
18.3
(
ii
)) also holds with
L=N
, which is sufficiently large for analysis/synthesis molecules (Definition
18.4
) by assumption (
20.2
).

For (
ii
),
under the additional assumption
f-\frac{n}{2}<0
, Remark
18.6
shows that we can take
L<0
, meaning that no cancellation is needed for synthesis molecules. (This is needed for
\lambda^{\prime}={\bf 0}
, in which case there is no cancellation.) The fact that
\Psi_{Q}^{(\lambda)}
satisfies the other molecular conditions (size and smoothness) with relevant
K,M,N
is the same as in case (
i
).

Finally, we deal with (
iii
).
By part (
i
), we know that
\Psi_{\omega}=\Psi^{(\lambda)}_{Q}
(with
\lambda\in\Lambda_{n}
) is an
\dot{\mathbf{a}}
-analysis molecule. Then the claim of (
iii
) is a direct application of Proposition
19.1
with
\Phi=\Psi_{\omega}
.
∎

Now, we give the wavelet characterization of

\dot{\mathbf{A}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})
as follows.

Theorem 20.2
.

Let
\dot{\mathbf{A}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})

be a
(d,e,f)
-discretizable space for some real parameters

(d,e,f)
.
If
N\in\mathbb{N}
satisfies (
20.2
)
and
\{\Psi_{\omega}\}_{\omega\in\Omega_{n}}

is a Daubechies wavelet system of order
N
,
then every
f\in\dot{\mathbf{A}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})
has a series representation

(20.3)

f=\sum_{\omega\in\Omega_{n}}\left\langle f,\Psi_{\omega}\right\rangle\Psi_{\omega}

convergent in
\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})
and

(20.4)

\displaystyle\|f\|_{\dot{\mathbf{A}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})}

\displaystyle\approx\|f\|_{\dot{\mathbf{A}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})_{\mathrm{w}}}:=\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{n}}\big\|\big\{\langle f,\Psi^{(\lambda)}_{Q}\rangle\big\}_{Q\in\mathscr{D}}\big\|_{\dot{\mathbf{a}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})},

where the positive equivalence constants are independent of
f
.

Proof.

Fix
f\in\dot{\mathbf{A}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})

and two Littlewood–Paley
functions
\varphi
and
\psi

satisfying (
15.3
).
For any
\lambda\in\Lambda_{n}
and
Q\in\mathscr{D}
,
define
t_{Q}^{(\lambda)}:=\langle f,\Psi_{Q}^{(\lambda)}\rangle
.
Proposition
20.1
(
iii
)
shows that
t_{Q}^{(\lambda)}
is well defined and

\displaystyle t_{Q}^{(\lambda)}=\sum_{R\in\mathscr{D}}\langle f,\varphi_{R}\rangle\langle\psi_{R},\Psi_{Q}^{(\lambda)}\rangle=(B^{(\lambda)}\circ S_{\varphi}f)_{Q},

where
B^{(\lambda)}:=\{\langle\Psi_{Q}^{(\lambda)},\psi_{R}\rangle\}_{Q,R\in\mathscr{D}}
.
From Proposition
20.1
(
i
)
and Lemma
19.3
(
i
), we deduce that

B^{(\lambda)}
is bounded on
\dot{\mathbf{a}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})
.
Combined with Definition
18.1
(
ii
), this implies that

\displaystyle\big\|\big\{t_{Q}^{(\lambda)}\big\}_{Q\in\mathscr{D}}\big\|_{\dot{\mathbf{a}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})}

\displaystyle=\big\|B^{(\lambda)}\circ S_{\varphi}f\big\|_{\dot{\mathbf{a}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})}\lesssim\|f\|_{\dot{\mathbf{A}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})}.

Summing over the finitely many
\lambda\in\Lambda_{n}
, this implies “
\gtrsim
” in (
20.4
).

Conversely, fix
\lambda\in\Lambda_{n}
, and suppose that

\{t_{Q}^{(\lambda)}\}_{Q\in\mathscr{D}}\in\dot{\mathbf{a}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})
.
Therefore, from Proposition
20.1
(
i
) and Theorem

19.5
(
ii
),
it follows that
\sum_{Q\in\mathscr{D}}t_{Q}^{(\lambda)}\Psi_{Q}^{(\lambda)}

converges in
\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})

and

(20.5)

\displaystyle\Big\|\sum_{Q\in\mathscr{D}}t_{Q}^{(\lambda)}\Psi_{Q}^{(\lambda)}\Big\|_{\dot{\mathbf{A}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})}\lesssim\big\|\big\{t_{Q}^{(\lambda)}\big\}_{Q\in\mathscr{D}}\big\|_{\dot{\mathbf{a}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})}.

Moreover, similarly to the proof of Proposition
19.1
,
we conclude that, for any
\phi\in\mathscr{S}_{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, the series

\displaystyle\sum_{Q,R\in\mathscr{D}}\langle f,\varphi_{R}\rangle\langle\psi_{R},\Psi_{Q}^{(\lambda)}\rangle\langle\Psi_{Q}^{(\lambda)},\phi\rangle

is absolutely convergent.
Together with the fact that
\{\Psi_{\omega}\}_{\omega\in\Omega_{n}}

is an orthonormal basis of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
the assumed Littlewood–Paley property (
15.3
),
this further implies

\displaystyle\sum_{\omega\in\Omega_{n}}\langle f,\Psi_{\omega}\rangle\langle\Psi_{\omega},\phi\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{n}}\sum_{Q,R\in\mathscr{D}}\langle f,\varphi_{R}\rangle\langle\psi_{R},\Psi_{Q}^{(\lambda)}\rangle\langle\Psi_{Q}^{(\lambda)},\phi\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{R\in\mathscr{D}}\langle f,\varphi_{R}\rangle\sum_{\omega\in\Omega_{n}}\langle\psi_{R},\Psi_{\omega}\rangle\langle\Psi_{\omega},\phi\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{R\in\mathscr{D}}\langle f,\varphi_{R}\rangle\langle\psi_{R},\phi\rangle=\langle f,\phi\rangle.

That is, (
20.3
) holds in
\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})
and

\displaystyle\|f\|_{\dot{\mathbf{A}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})}

\displaystyle=\Big\|\sum_{\omega\in\Omega_{n}}\langle f,\varphi_{R}\rangle\langle\psi_{R},\phi\rangle\Big\|_{\dot{\mathbf{A}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})}\lesssim\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{n}}\Big\|\sum_{Q\in\mathscr{D}}t_{Q}^{(\lambda)}\Psi_{Q}^{(\lambda)}\Big\|_{\dot{\mathbf{A}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{n}}\big\|\big\{t_{Q}^{(\lambda)}\big\}_{Q\in\mathscr{D}}\big\|_{\dot{\mathbf{a}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})}\quad\text{by \eqref{thm-wavelet-e3}}

\displaystyle=\|f\|_{\dot{\mathbf{A}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})_{\rm w}}.

This is the desired upper estimate of (
20.4
)
and hence finishes the proof of Theorem
20.2
.
∎

Combining Lemma
18.2
and Example
18.5
,
Theorem
20.2
yields the
following wavelet characterizations of
the function spaces introduced in Definition
12.3
;
we omit the details.

Corollary 20.3
.

Let
p\in(0,\infty)
,
q\in(0,\infty]
,
and
s\in\mathbb{R}
.

(i)

Let
\rho=\{\rho_{Q}\}_{Q\in\mathscr{D}}

be a family of
(u,a,b,c)
-norms with

u\in(0,1]
and
a,b,c\in[0,\infty)
.
If
N\in\mathbb{N}
satisfies

(20.6)

\displaystyle N>\max\big\{s+a,J^{(u)}-n-s+b\big\}

with
J^{(u)}
as in (
17.3
),
then Proposition
20.1
and Theorem
20.2
apply to
\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho)
in place of
\dot{\mathbf{A}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})
with condition
f-\frac{n}{2}<0
of Proposition
20.1
(
ii
) replaced by the
\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho)
case of (
18.6
).

(ii)

Let
V\in\mathscr{A}_{r}
with
r\in[p,\infty)
and the doubling
dimension
\beta\in[n,\infty)
, and let
q\in(0,r+\varepsilon_{V})

when
A=F
, where
\varepsilon_{V}
is
the optimal reverse Hölder lifting index of
V
. If

(20.7)

\displaystyle N>\max\big\{s,J-n-s+\frac{\beta-n}{r}\big\}

with
J
as in (
17.2
),
then Proposition
20.1
and Theorem
20.2
apply to the space
\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(V)=\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho_{\textit{\L}^{r}(\mathscr{D},V)})
in place of
\dot{\mathbf{A}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})
with condition
f-\frac{n}{2}<0
of Proposition
20.1
(
ii
) replaced by the
\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(V)
case of (
18.6
).

21.
Calderón–Zygmund operators

In this section, we study the boundedness of
Calderón–Zygmund operators and the related
T(1)
theorem.
First, we give a general extension
criterion of a given operator
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathscr{S},\mathscr{S}^{\prime})

in Subsection
21.1
.
Then we establish the
T(1)
theorem
of Calderón–Zygmund operators in Subsection
21.2
.
Finally, in Subsection
21.3
,
we present some concrete examples of Calderón–Zygmund operators
and corresponding
T(1)
theorems.

21.1.
Extension of weakly defined operators

In what follows, we use the
symbol

C_{\mathrm{c}}^{\infty}
to denote
the set of all functions
f\in C^{\infty}
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
with compact support.
We need the following definition of atoms. As in the case of molecules, despite dealing with
\mathfrak{X}
-valued distributions, our atoms will be scalar-valued, and they be equipped with
\mathfrak{X}
-valued coefficients when decomposing
\mathfrak{X}
-valued distributions.

Definition 21.1
.

Let
L,N\in(0,\infty)
.
A function
a_{Q}\in C_{\mathrm{c}}^{\infty}
is called an
(L,N)
-atom
on a cube
Q
if

(i)

\operatorname{supp}a_{Q}\subset 3Q
,

(ii)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}x^{\gamma}a_{Q}(x)\,dx=0

for any
\gamma\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{n}
with
|\gamma|\leq L
,

(iii)

|D^{\gamma}a_{Q}(x)|\leq|Q|^{-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{|\gamma|}{n}}

for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\gamma\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{n}
with
|\gamma|\leq N
.

Furthermore, denote by
I_{\mathfrak{X}}
the identity operator
on
\mathfrak{X}
. Then, for any linear operator
T
on a
space
of scalar-valued functions
D
and any

f:=\sum_{k=1}^{N}f_{k}\mathfrak{e}_{k}\in D\otimes\mathfrak{X}
,
we define

\displaystyle(T\otimes I_{\mathfrak{X}})(f):=\sum_{k=1}^{N}T(f_{k})\mathfrak{e}_{k}.

Then we have the following extension criterion
for general linear operators.

Proposition 21.2
.

Let
\dot{\mathbf{A}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})

be a homogeneous
(d,e,f)
-discretizable space with some real
parameters
(d,e,f)
, and let
L,N\in(0,\infty)
.

(i)

If
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathscr{S},\mathscr{S}^{\prime})

maps
(L,N)
-atoms to
\dot{\mathbf{a}}
-synthesis molecules,
then there exists an operator

\widetilde{T}\in\mathcal{L}(\dot{\mathbf{A}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X}))

that agrees with
T\otimes I_{\mathfrak{X}}
on
\mathscr{S}_{\infty}\otimes\mathfrak{X}
.

(ii)

If, in addition, there exists an operator
T_{2}\in\mathcal{L}(L^{2})

that agrees with
T
on
\mathscr{S}
,
then
\widetilde{T}
agrees with
T\otimes I_{\mathfrak{X}}
on

(\mathscr{S}\otimes\mathfrak{X})\cap\dot{\mathbf{A}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})
.

Proof.

First, we prove (
i
).
Let
\varphi,\psi
be two Littlewood–Paley functions that satisfy (
15.3
). Then, from the proof of

[
12
, Proposition 6.5]
, we deduce that,
for any
R\in\mathscr{D}
, there exists
a sequence
\{a_{P}^{R}\}_{\{P\in\mathscr{D}:\ell(P)=\ell(R)\}}
,
where each
a_{P}^{R}
is an
(L,N)
-atom on
P
, such that, for any
Q\in\mathscr{D}
,

(21.1)

\left\langle\varphi_{Q},T\psi_{R}\right\rangle=\sum_{P\in\mathscr{D}}\big\langle\varphi_{Q},T\big(a_{P}^{R}\big)\big\rangle b_{PR},

where, for any
P,R\in\mathscr{Q}
,

b_{PR}:=\begin{cases}\displaystyle{\Big[1+\frac{|x_{R}-x_{P}|}{\ell(R)}\Big]^{-D^{\prime}}}&\text{if}\ \ell(P)=\ell(R),\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

and
D^{\prime}
can be arbitrarily large. In particular,
(b_{PR})_{PR\in\mathscr{D}}
is
(D^{\prime},E^{\prime},F^{\prime})
-almost diagonal for any
D^{\prime},E^{\prime},F^{\prime}
.
Moreover,

•

each
a^{R}_{P}
is an
(L,N)
-atom on
P
, as we already observed,

•

hence each
T(a^{P}_{R})
is an
\dot{\mathbf{a}}
-synthesis molecules by our assumption on the operator
T
,

•

thus, by Lemma
19.3
(
ii
),

|\langle\varphi_{Q},T\big(a_{P}^{R}\big)\rangle|\lesssim B_{-E,-F,-D}(Q,P),

where the parameters
(D,E,F)
satisfy

D>\max\{n,d\}
,
E>e
, and
F>f
.

Substituting into (
21.1
), we find that

\begin{split}|\left\langle\varphi_{Q},T\psi_{R}\right\rangle|&\lesssim\sum_{P\in\mathscr{D}}B_{-E,-F,-D}(Q,P)B_{-E^{\prime},-F^{\prime},-D^{\prime}}(P,R)\\
&\lesssim B_{-E,-F,-D}(Q,R)\qquad\text{by Lemma \ref {ad-com}},\end{split}

where the assumptions of Lemma
19.4
are easily satisfied, since
D>n
, and
D^{\prime},E^{\prime},F^{\prime}
can be chosen as large as needed.
Thus
B_{T}:=\{\langle\varphi_{Q},T\psi_{R}\rangle\}_{Q,R\in\mathscr{D}}
is also
(D,E,F)
-almost diagonal,
and hence, by Definition
18.1
(
iii
),
it is bounded on
\dot{\mathbf{a}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})
.

Together with Definition
18.1
(
ii
), this
implies that

(21.2)

\widetilde{T}:=T_{\psi}\circ B_{T}\circ S_{\varphi}\in\mathcal{L}(\dot{\mathbf{A}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})).

Moreover,

(21.3)

\begin{split}B_{T}\circ S_{\varphi}=S_{\varphi}\circ T\quad&\text{on}\quad\mathscr{S}_{\infty}\quad\text{by \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{BHYY:III}{}{}, Lemma 6.1]}, and}\\
&\text{on}\quad\mathscr{S}_{\infty}\otimes\mathfrak{X}\quad\text{by linearity}.\end{split}

On the other hand, we know from
[
70
, Lemma 2.1]
that

(21.4)

T_{\psi}\circ S_{\varphi}=I\quad\text{on}\quad\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{\prime}.

Combining these identities, it follows that, on
\mathscr{S}_{\infty}\otimes\mathfrak{X}
,

(21.5)

\begin{split}\widetilde{T}&=T_{\psi}\circ B_{T}\circ S_{\varphi}\quad\text{by \eqref{eq:tildeT=TBS}}\\
&=T_{\psi}\circ S_{\varphi}\circ T\quad\text{by \eqref{eq:BS=ST}}\\
&=T\quad\text{by \eqref{eq:TS=I}}.\end{split}

This finishes the proof of (
i
).

Moreover, applying the assumption
T_{2}\in\mathcal{L}(L^{2})

and
[
12
, (6.3)]
, we conclude that

S_{\varphi}\circ T=B_{T}\circ S_{\varphi}
on
\mathscr{S}
,
which implies
S_{\varphi}\circ T=B_{T}\otimes S_{\varphi}
on
\mathscr{S}\otimes\mathfrak{X}
.
Consequently, from Definition
18.1
(
ii
), we further infer that
(
21.5
) also holds
on
(\mathscr{S}\otimes\mathfrak{X})\cap\dot{\mathbf{A}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})
,
which then completes the proof of (
ii
) and hence Proposition

21.2
.
∎

21.2.
T(1)
theorem for Calderón–Zygmund operators

We now turn to the actual discussion of Calderón–Zygmund operators.
The following classical definitions are standard.
Let
\mathcal{D}:=C_{\mathrm{c}}^{\infty}
with the usual inductive limit topology.
We denote by
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
the space of
all continuous linear functionals on
\mathcal{D}
,
equipped with the weak-
*
topology.
If
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathscr{S},\mathscr{S}^{\prime})
,
then, by the well-known Schwartz kernel theorem,
we find that there exists
\mathcal{K}\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n})

such that

\langle T\varphi,\psi\rangle=\langle\mathcal{K},\varphi\otimes\psi\rangle
for all
\varphi,\psi\in\mathscr{S}
.
This
\mathcal{K}
is called the
Schwartz kernel
of
T
.

The
adjoint operator

T^{*}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathscr{S},\mathscr{S}^{\prime})

of
T
is defined by setting

\langle T\varphi,\psi\rangle=\langle T^{*}\psi,\varphi\rangle

for all
\varphi,\psi\in\mathscr{S}
.
Then, from the definition, we easily infer that,
if
\mathcal{K}\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n})

is the Schwartz kernel of
T
, then
the Schwartz kernel
\mathcal{K}^{*}
of
T^{*}

satisfies
\langle K,\varphi\otimes\psi\rangle=\langle K^{*},\psi\otimes\varphi\rangle

for all
\varphi,\psi\in\mathscr{S}
.

Inspired by
[
29
,
64
]
,
to establish the boundedness of
Calderón–Zygmund operators on Besov–Triebel–Lizorkin spaces,
we use the following kernel estimates from
[
12
, Definition 6.11]
.

Definition 21.3
.

Let
E,F\in\mathbb{R}
,
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathscr{S},\mathscr{S}^{\prime})
,
and
\mathcal{K}\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n})
be its Schwartz kernel.
We say that
T\in\operatorname{CZK}^{0}(E;F)

if the restriction of
\mathcal{K}
to

\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}:\ x\neq y\}

is a continuous function such that all derivatives below
exist as continuous functions and there exists a positive constant
C
such that,
for any
\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{n}
with
|\alpha|\leq\lfloor\!\lfloor E\rfloor\!\rfloor_{+}

and for any
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
with
x\neq y
,

(21.6)

|\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\mathcal{K}(x,y)|\leq C|x-y|^{-n-|\alpha|},

that, for any
\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{n}
with
|\alpha|=\lfloor\!\lfloor E\rfloor\!\rfloor

and for any
x,y,u\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
with
|u|<\frac{1}{2}|x-y|
,

(21.7)

|\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\mathcal{K}(x,y)-\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\mathcal{K}(x+u,y)|\leq C|u|^{E^{**}}|x-y|^{-n-E},

and that, for any
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{n}
with
|\alpha|\leq\lfloor\!\lfloor E\rfloor\!\rfloor_{+}

and
|\beta|=\lfloor\!\lfloor F-|\alpha|\rfloor\!\rfloor

and for any
x,y,v\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
with
|v|<\frac{1}{2}|x-y|
,

(21.8)

\displaystyle\left|\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{y}^{\beta}\mathcal{K}(x,y)-\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{y}^{\beta}\mathcal{K}(x,y+v)\right|\leq C|v|^{(F-|\alpha|)^{**}}|x-y|^{-n-|\alpha|-(F-|\alpha|)},

where
\lfloor\!\lfloor E\rfloor\!\rfloor
and
E^{**}
are as in (
18.1
) and (
18.2
).

We say that
T\in\operatorname{CZK}^{1}(E;F)

if
T\in\operatorname{CZK}^{0}(E;F)
and, in addition,
there exists a positive constant
C
such that,
for any
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{n}
with

|\alpha|=\lfloor\!\lfloor E\rfloor\!\rfloor
and
|\beta|=\lfloor\!\lfloor F-E\rfloor\!\rfloor

and for any
x,y,u,v\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
with
|u|+|v|<\frac{1}{2}|x-y|
,

(21.9)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\left|\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{y}^{\beta}\mathcal{K}(x,y)-\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{y}^{\beta}\mathcal{K}(x+u,y)-\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{y}^{\beta}\mathcal{K}(x,y+v)+\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{y}^{\beta}\mathcal{K}(x+u,y+v)\right|\\
&\quad\leq C|u|^{E^{**}}|v|^{(F-E)^{**}}|x-y|^{-n-E-(F-E)}.\end{split}

Remark 21.4
.

Let
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathscr{S},\mathscr{S}^{\prime})
.
We have the following observations.

(i)

If
F\leq 0<E
, then the conditions
(
21.8
) and (
21.9
) are both void and hence,
for any
\sigma\in\{0,1\}
,

\operatorname{CZK}^{\sigma}(E;F)=\operatorname{CZK}^{0}(E;0)
.
Hence, the set
\operatorname{CZK}^{0}(E;0)

means the set of operators with Calderón–Zygmund estimates of order

E
in the
x
variable, with no assumptions on the
y
variable.
Furthermore, if
E\in(0,1]
, then the operator

T\in\operatorname{CZK}^{0}(E;0)
satisfying

T^{*}\in\operatorname{CZO}^{0}(F;0)
is precisely the classical
Calderón–Zygmund operator presented in
[
21
, Definition 5.11]
.

(ii)

For more examples of operators in
\operatorname{CZK}^{\sigma}(E;F)
,
we refer to
[
12
, Remark 6.13]
.

To study the boundedness
of Calderón–Zygmund type operators
on
\dot{\mathbf{A}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})
,
we extend the domain of operators
introduced in Definition
21.3

to include polynomials (which are not in
\mathscr{S})

in the following way:

Remark 21.5
.

Let
T\in\operatorname{CZK}^{\sigma}(E;F)
with

\sigma\in\{0,1\}
and let
E,F\in\mathbb{R}
.
By
[
12
, Lemma 6.9]

(see also
[
64
, Lemma 2.2.12]
),
we find that, if
E\in(0,\infty)
, then

T(x^{\gamma})
can be defined for any

\gamma\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{n}
such that

|\gamma|\leq\lfloor\!\lfloor E\rfloor\!\rfloor
;
if
F\in(0,\infty)
, then

T^{*}(x^{\theta})
can be defined for any

\theta\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{n}
such that

|\gamma|\leq\lfloor\!\lfloor F\rfloor\!\rfloor
.

Then we introduce the
Calderón–Zygmund conditions
following
[
12
, Definition 6.17]
.

Definition 21.6
.

Let
\sigma\in\{0,1\}
,
E,F\in\mathbb{R}
, and
G,H\in\mathbb{Z}

satisfy
G\leq\lfloor\!\lfloor E\rfloor\!\rfloor
and

H\leq\lfloor\!\lfloor F\rfloor\!\rfloor
.
We say that
T\in\operatorname{CZO}^{\sigma}(E,F,G,H)

if
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathscr{S},\mathscr{S}^{\prime})
and

(i)

T
satisfies the
weak boundedness property

(which is denoted by
T\in\operatorname{WBP}
); that is,
for any bounded subset
\mathcal{B}
of
\mathcal{D}
,
there exists a positive constant
C=C(\mathcal{B})
such that,
for any
\varphi,\eta\in\mathcal{B}
,
h\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and
r\in(0,\infty)
,

\Big|\Big\langle T\Big[\varphi\Big(\frac{\cdot-h}{r}\Big)\Big],\eta\Big(\frac{\cdot-h}{r}\Big)\Big\rangle\Big|\leq Cr^{n},

(ii)

T\in\operatorname{CZK}^{\sigma}(E;F)
,

(iii)

T(y^{\gamma})=0
for all
\gamma\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{n}
with
|\gamma|\leq G
, (This is void if
G<0
.)

(iv)

T^{*}(x^{\theta})=0
for all
\theta\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{n}
with
|\theta|\leq H
. (This is void if
H<0
.)

Remark 21.7
.

Definition
21.6
is identical to
[
12
, Definition 6.17]
except for the fact that we have added the parameter restrictions
G\leq\lfloor\!\lfloor E\rfloor\!\rfloor
and
H\leq\lfloor\!\lfloor F\rfloor\!\rfloor
, which are needed to ensure that
T(y^{\gamma})
and
T^{*}(x^{\theta})
in (
iii
) and (
iv
) are well defined for all
\gamma,\theta
appearing there according to Remark
21.5
. We point out that the same restriction should have been made in
[
12
, Definition 6.17]
as well. However, its omission is a relatively harmless oversight, since this additional restriction is valid in all concrete instances where
[
12
, Definition 6.17]
is used in
[
12
]
. Notably, this applies to
[
12
, Proposition 6.19]
, which is restated below as Lemma
21.9
.

We now state the
T(1)
theorem as follows.

Theorem 21.8
.

Let
\dot{\mathbf{A}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})

be a homogeneous
(d,e,f)
-discretizable space with some real
parameters
(d,e,f)
.
Suppose
T\in\operatorname{CZO}^{\sigma}(E,F,G,H)

in the sense of Definition
21.6
, where

\sigma\in\{0,1\}
and the parameters
(E,F,G,H)
satisfy

\begin{split}&\sigma\geq\mathbf{1}_{[0,\infty)}\big(e-\frac{n}{2}\big),\quad E>\big(e-\frac{n}{2}\big)_{+},\quad F>\max\big\{n,d,f+\frac{n}{2}\big\}-n,\\
&G\geq\big\lfloor e-\frac{n}{2}\big\rfloor_{+},\quad\text{and}\quad H\geq\big\lfloor f-\frac{n}{2}\big\rfloor.\end{split}

Then the following two statements hold:

(i)

There exists an operator

\widetilde{T}\in\mathcal{L}(\dot{\mathbf{A}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X}))

that agrees with
T\otimes I_{\mathfrak{X}}
on
\mathscr{S}_{\infty}\otimes\mathfrak{X}
.

(ii)

If further
H\geq 0
,
then there exists
\widetilde{T}\in\mathcal{L}(\dot{\mathbf{A}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X}))

that agrees with
T\otimes I_{\mathfrak{X}}
on

(\mathscr{S}\otimes\mathfrak{X})\cap\dot{\mathbf{A}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})
.

We need the following estimate from
[
12
]
.

Lemma 21.9

(
[
12
, Proposition 6.19]
)
.

Suppose that
T\in\operatorname{CZO}^{\sigma}(E,F,G,H)

in the sense of Definition
21.6
.
Then
T
maps sufficiently regular atoms to
(K,L,M,N)
-molecules
provided that the following conditions are satisfied:

\displaystyle\sigma

\displaystyle\geq\mathbf{1}_{(0,\infty)}(N),\quad\begin{cases}E\geq N,\\
E>\lfloor N\rfloor_{+},\end{cases}\quad\begin{cases}F\geq(K\vee M)-n,\\
F>\lfloor L\rfloor,\end{cases}\quad G\geq\lfloor N\rfloor_{+},\quad\text{and}\quad H\geq\lfloor L\rfloor.

Proof of Theorem
21.8
.

By the assumptions on
\sigma
,
E
,
F
,
G
, and
H
,
we can choose
(K,L,M,N)
satisfying:

\displaystyle K\in\big(\max\big\{n,d,f+\frac{n}{2}\big\},F+n\big),\quad L\in\big(f-\frac{n}{2},\big\lceil\!\big\lceil f-\frac{n}{2}\big\rceil\!\big\rceil\big),\quad M\in(\max\{d,n\},F+n),

and

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &\displaystyle{N:=0\in\big(e-\frac{n}{2},E\big)}\hskip 41.82558pt\text{if}\ \displaystyle{e-\frac{n}{2}<0},\\
&\displaystyle{N\in\big(e-\frac{n}{2},\big\lceil\!\big\lceil e-\frac{n}{2}\big\rceil\!\big\rceil\wedge E\big)}\hskip 14.22636pt\text{if}\ \displaystyle{e-\frac{n}{2}\geq 0}.\end{aligned}\right.

Note that the lower bounds in the choice of these parameters ensure that
(K,L,M,N)
-molecules are
\dot{\mathbf{a}}
-synthesis molecules in the sense of Definition
18.4
(
ii
).

From the choice of the parameters and some basic calculations, we infer that

\sigma
,
(E,F,G,H)
, and
(K,L,M,N)
satisfy the
assumptions on Lemma
21.9
.
Therefore, using Lemma
21.9
, we find that

T
maps sufficiently regular atoms to

(K,L,M,N)
-molecules, which are

\dot{\mathbf{a}}
-synthesis molecules by what we observed.
Thus, Proposition
21.2
(
i
) completes the proof of Theorem
21.8
(
i
).

Now, we prove case (
ii
) of the theorem.
From the assumptions on
\sigma
and
(E,F,G,H)
,
we deduce that
E,F>0
and
G,H\geq 0
,
which further implies

T\in\operatorname{WPB}\cap\operatorname{CZK}^{0}(E;0),\quad T^{*}\in\operatorname{CZK}^{0}(F;0),\quad\text{and}\quad T(1)=0=T^{*}(1)

for these
E
and
F
.
Therefore, by the well-known
T(1)
theorem of David and Journé
[
20
]
,
we conclude that there exists
T_{2}\in\mathcal{L}(L^{2})

that agrees with
T
on
\mathscr{S}
.
Thus, the assumptions of Proposition
21.2
(
ii
) are satisfied, and the said proposition
implies the existence of the operator
\widetilde{T}
with the additional property as desired.
This finishes the proof of Theorem
21.8
.
∎

By considering special cases of
\dot{\mathbf{A}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})
,
we obtain the following
T(1)
theorem for the Besov and Triebel–Lizorkin spaces of Definition
12.3
.
This is a direct consequence of Lemma
18.2
and Example
18.5
, and we omit the straightforward details.

Corollary 21.10
.

Let
p\in(0,\infty)
,
q\in(0,\infty]
,
and
s\in\mathbb{R}
.

(i)

Let
\rho=\{\rho_{Q}\}_{Q\in\mathscr{D}}

be a family of
(u,a,b,c)
-norms with

u\in(0,1]
and
a,b,c\in[0,\infty)
.
If
T\in\operatorname{CZO}^{\sigma}(E,F,G,H)

in the sense of Definition
21.6
, where

\sigma\in\{0,1\}
and the parameters
(E,F,G,H)
satisfy

\begin{split}&\sigma\geq\mathbf{1}_{[0,\infty)}(s+a),\quad E>(s+a)_{+},\quad F>J^{(u)}-n+\max\{c,b-s\},\\
&G\geq\lfloor s+a\rfloor_{+},\quad\text{and}\quad H\geq\big\lfloor J^{(u)}-n-s+b\big\rfloor,\end{split}

where
J^{(u)}
is as in (
17.3
),
then Theorem
21.8
applies to
\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho)
in place of
\dot{\mathbf{A}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})
.

(ii)

Let
V\in\mathscr{A}_{r}
with
r\in[p,\infty)
has doubling
dimension
\beta\in[n,\infty)
, and let
q\in(0,r+\varepsilon_{V})

when
A=F
, where
\varepsilon_{V}
is
the optimal reverse Hölder lifting index of
V
.
If
T\in\operatorname{CZO}^{\sigma}(E,F,G,H)

in the sense of Definition
21.6
, where

\sigma\in\{0,1\}
and the parameters
(E,F,G,H)
satisfy

(21.10)

\begin{split}&\sigma\geq\mathbf{1}_{[0,\infty)}(s),\quad E>s_{+},\quad F>J-n+s_{-}+\frac{\beta-n}{r},\\
&G\geq\lfloor s\rfloor_{+},\quad\text{and}\quad H\geq\Big\lfloor J-n-s+\frac{\beta-n}{r}\Big\rfloor,\end{split}

where
J
is as in (
17.2
),
then Theorem
21.8
applies to
\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(V)=\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho_{\textit{\L}^{r}(\mathscr{D},V)})
in place of
\dot{\mathbf{A}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})
.

Remark 21.11
.

Even in the unweighted case
V\equiv I
, the result of Corollary
21.10
on the boundedness of general Calderón–Zygmund operators on
\mathfrak{X}
-valued Besov and Triebel–Lizorkin spaces
\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})
seems to be largely new. In this direction, we are only aware the results of Kaiser
[
44
]
, which deals with the Besov spaces
\dot{B}^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})
in the range
p,q\in[1,\infty]
. Noting that
J=\beta=n
in this case, the conditions (
21.10
) reduce to

\sigma\geq\mathbf{1}_{[0,\infty)}(s),\quad E>s_{+},\quad F>s_{-},\quad G\geq\lfloor s\rfloor_{+},\quad H\geq\lfloor-s\rfloor,

and we note in particular that, for spaces of positive smoothness
s>0
, the condition on
H
is trivial, i.e., there exist no conditions of the type
T^{*}(x^{\theta})=0
in this case. These assumptions essentially agree with those of
[
44
, Theorem 3.1]
in the case of
\mathfrak{X}
-valued extensions of scalar-valued operators
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathscr{S}_{\infty},\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{\prime})
that we consider. However,
[
44
, Theorem 3.1]
deals with more general operators
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathscr{S}_{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X}),\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{Y}))
with
\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{Y})
-valued kernels for two Banach spaces
\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{Y}
. Hence, while Corollary
21.10
goes beyond
[
44
, Theorem 3.1]
in several directions (a larger range of indices
p,q
, both Besov and Triebel–Lizorkin spaces, and operator-weighted versions), the two results are not strictly comparable.

21.3.
Examples

When specialized to
\mathfrak{X}=\mathbb{C}^{m}
and matrix weights, Corollary
21.10
falls slightly short of reproducing the best available result in this setting, which is the recent
[
12
, Theorem 6.18]
. Namely, compared to the related condition
[
12
, (6.20)]
, the present (
21.10
) has the additional nonnegative term
\frac{\beta-n}{r}
in the lower bounds for
F
and
H
. Nevertheless, Corollary
21.10
is strong enough to recover the main results on Calderón–Zygmund operators on matrix-weighted Besov and Triebel–Lizorkin spaces known before
[
12
]
, including the still quite recent
[
28
]
. To facilitate this comparison, we recall the following
[
28
, Definition 5.6]
:

Definition 21.12
.

Let
L\in\mathbb{N}
.
A operator
T:\mathscr{S}\to\mathscr{S}^{\prime}

is called an
L
-smooth Calderón–Zygmund operator

if

(i)

there exists
K\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}

such that, for any
f\in\mathcal{S}
,
T(f):=K\ast f
,

(ii)

K
coincides with a
function belonging to
C^{L}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{{\bf 0}\})

in the sense that, for any given
f\in\mathscr{S}
with
compact support and for any
x\notin\operatorname{supp}f
,

T(f)(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}K(x-y)f(y)\,dy,

(iii)

there exists a positive
constant
C
such that,
for any
\gamma\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{n}
with
|\gamma|\leq L

and for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{{\bf 0}\}
,

(21.11)

\left|\partial^{\gamma}K(x)\right|\leq\frac{C}{|x|^{n+|\gamma|}},

(iv)

for any
0<R_{1}<R_{2}<\infty
,

\int_{R_{1}<|x|<R_{2}}K(x)\,dx=0.

In
[
28
,
59
]
, the authors first present results for general Calderón–Zygmund operators in the spirit of Corollary
21.10
, and then give a separate treatment for convolution-type operators. However, the need for this latter seems to come from a slight misunderstanding in
[
59
, Remark 9.12]
: “Note that the condition
T^{*}(y^{\gamma})=0
,
|\gamma|\leq N
, can be very restrictive; for example, the Hilbert transform does not satisfy this condition for
|\gamma|>0
.” This is repeated in
[
28
, pp. 526–527]
.

However, if one interprets
T^{*}(x^{\gamma})
in the usual way as a distribution modulo polynomials of order
|\gamma|
, then we do have
T^{*}(x^{\gamma})=0

for all multi-indices
\gamma
for the Hilbert, the Riesz, and other classical convolution transforms with sufficiently regular kernels. Indeed, by
[
64
, Proposition 2.2.17]
,
every
L
-smooth Calderón–Zygmund operator
T
satisfies

T(x^{\gamma})=0
(resp.
T^{*}(x^{\theta})=0
)
as a distribution on
\mathscr{D}_{|\gamma|}

(resp.
\mathscr{D}_{|\theta|}
), where

\mathscr{D}_{\ell}=\Big\{\varphi\in\mathscr{D}:\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}x^{\gamma}\varphi(x)\,dx=0,\ \forall\ |\gamma|\leq\ell\Big\},\quad\ell\in\mathbb{N}

for all
\gamma,\theta\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{n}
with
|\gamma|,|\theta|\leq L-1
.

This fact seems to have been partially overlooked in
[
59
,
28
]
. Instead of needing a separate treatment for operators of Definition
21.12
, these can be readily dealt with as a special case of the results above:

Corollary 21.13
.

Assume
L\in\mathbb{N}
and
T
is an

L
-smooth Calderón–Zygmund operator.
Then
T\in\operatorname{CZO}^{\sigma}(L,L,L-1,L-1)

for
\sigma\in\{0,1\}
. As a consequence,
for every
p\in(0,\infty)
,
q\in(0,\infty]
,
and
s\in\mathbb{R}
, the following two
results hold.

(i)

Let
\rho=\{\rho_{Q}\}_{Q\in\mathscr{D}}

be a family of
(u,a,b,c)
-norms with

u\in(0,1]
and
a,b,c\in[0,\infty)
.
If

\displaystyle L>\max\big\{(s+a)_{+},J^{(u)}-n+\max\{c,b-s\}\big\},

where
J^{(u)}
is as in (
17.3
), then
there exists
\widetilde{T}\in\mathcal{L}(\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho))

that agrees with
T\otimes I_{\mathfrak{X}}
on

(\mathscr{S}\otimes\mathfrak{X})\cap\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho)
.

(ii)

Let
V\in\mathscr{A}_{r}
with
r\in[p,\infty)
and the doubling
dimension
\beta\in[n,\infty)
, and let
q\in(0,r+\varepsilon_{V})

when
A=F
, where
\varepsilon_{V}
is
the optimal reverse Hölder lifting index of
V
.
If

\displaystyle L>\max\Big\{s_{+},J-n+s_{-}+\frac{\beta-n}{r}\Big\},

where
J
is as in (
17.2
),
then there exists
\widetilde{T}\in\mathcal{L}(\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(V))

that agrees with
T\otimes I_{\mathfrak{X}}
on

(\mathscr{S}\otimes\mathfrak{X})\cap\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(V)
.

Proof.

If we show
T\in\operatorname{CZO}^{\sigma}(L,L,L-1,L-1)

with
\sigma\in\{0,1\}
, then
the remaining results follow from Corollary
21.10

directly.
Hence, we now turn to prove
T\in\operatorname{CZO}^{\sigma}(L,L,L-1,L-1)
.
Indeed, by the discussion preceding the present corollary,
the cancelation conditions (
iii
)
and (
iv
) of Defintition(
21.6
) hold with

G=H=L-1
.
Suppose
K
is the kernel in the sense of Definition
21.12
.
Applying (
21.11
) and the mean value theorem,
the size conditions (
21.6
), (
21.7
), and (
21.8
)
hold with
\mathcal{K}(x,y):=K(x,y)
and
E=F=L
and, if we take
E=F
, then
condition (
21.9
) is void. This means that

T\in\operatorname{CZK}^{\sigma}(L;L)
for
\sigma\in\{0,1\}
.
Finally, as pointed out in
[
50
]
(see also
[
63
, p. 35]
),

T\in\operatorname{WBP}
.
Therefore,
T\in\operatorname{CZO}^{\sigma}(L,L,L-1,L-1)
,

\sigma\in\{0,1\}
, in the sense of Definition
21.6
.
This then finishes the proof of Corollary
21.13
.
∎

Remark 21.14
.

Corollary
21.13
has several applications as follows.

(i)

Since the Hilbert transform
\mathcal{H}

and every Riesz transform
\mathcal{R}_{j}
,
j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
,
satisfy Definition
21.12
for arbitrarily large
L\in\mathbb{N}
,
the boundedness conclusion of Corollary
21.13
holds automatically for

\mathcal{H}
and
\{R_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}
.
Different from the proof of Corollary
15.15
,
we therefore provide an alternative proof of the boundedness of
the Hilbert transform on both
\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho)
and
\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(V)
.

(ii)

If
p\in(0,\infty)
,
q\in(0,\infty]
,
s\in\mathbb{R}
, and

V\in\mathscr{A}_{p}
has doubling dimension
\beta\in[n,\infty)
,
and if we let
\rho:=\{\rho_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,V)}\}_{Q\in\mathscr{D}}
, then,
from Remark
15.2
(
i
), we infer that
the ranges of
L
in (
i
) and (
ii
) of
Corollary
21.13
become, respectively,

(21.12)

\displaystyle L>\max\Bigg\{\Big(s+\frac{n}{p}\Big)_{+},J-n+\max\Big\{\frac{\beta}{p},\frac{\beta-n}{p}-s\Big\}\Bigg\}

and

(21.13)

\displaystyle L>\max\Big\{s_{+},J-n+s_{-}+\frac{\beta-n}{p}\Big\}.

These ranges coincide only if
s\leq-\frac{n}{p}
. When

s>-\frac{n}{p}
, (
21.13
) is strictly larger than (
21.12
).
On the other hand, the range (
21.12
) coincides with that in
[
28
, Theorem 5.8]
.
Hence, in the Triebel–Lizorkin case, by the equivalence between
\dot{F}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho)
and

\dot{F}^{s}_{p,q}(V)
when
\mathfrak{X}=\mathbb{C}^{m}
, Corollary

21.13
(
i
) coincides with
[
28
, Theorem 5.8]
;
furthermore, if
q\in(0,p+\varepsilon_{V})
(the optimal
reverse Hölder lifting index of
V
), then
Corollary
21.13
(
ii
) improves
[
28
, Theorem 5.8]
.
Finally, in the Besov case, Corollary
21.13
(
ii
)
extends
[
59
, Corollary 9.15]
and
[
26
, Theorem 4.2(2)]

from
s\in[0,1)
to arbitrary
s\in\mathbb{R}
.

Moreover, Frazier and Roudenko
[
59
,
26
]

also consider a class of
non-convolution Calderón–Zygmund operators

\operatorname{CZO}(N+\varepsilon)
with
N\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
and
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
,
which is precisely the class
\operatorname{CZK}^{0}(N+\varepsilon;N+\varepsilon)

in the sense of Definition
21.3
(see
[
59
, p. 303]
for details).
Hence, applying Corollary
21.10
(
ii
)
and the discussion preceding Corollary
21.13
,
we immediately obtain the following

T(1)
theorem for
\operatorname{CZO}(N+\varepsilon)

on
\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(V)
; we omit details.

Corollary 21.15
.

Let
N\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
,
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
,
and
T\in\operatorname{CZO}(N+\varepsilon)
.
Suppose
p\in(0,\infty)
,
q\in(0,\infty]
,
and
s\in\mathbb{R}
.
Let
V\in\mathscr{A}_{r}
with
r\in[p,\infty)
and the doubling
dimension
\beta\in[n,\infty)
, and let
q\in(0,r+\varepsilon_{V})

when
A=F
, where
\varepsilon_{V}
is
the optimal reverse Hölder lifting index of
V
.
If
N
,
\varepsilon
, and
T
satisfy

\displaystyle\begin{split}&T\in\operatorname{WBP},\quad N+\varepsilon>\max\Big\{s_{+},J-n+s_{-}+\frac{\beta-n}{r}\Big\},\\
&N\geq\max\Big\{\lfloor s\rfloor_{+},\Big\lfloor J-n-s+\frac{\beta-n}{r}\Big\rfloor\Big\},\quad T(x^{\gamma})=0\quad\forall\,|\gamma|\leq\lfloor s\rfloor_{+},\end{split}

and
T^{*}(x^{\theta})=0
for all
|\theta|\leq\lfloor J-n-s+\frac{\beta-n}{r}\rfloor
(this assumption is void if the upper bound is negative),
then

(i)

there exists an operator

\widetilde{T}\in\mathcal{L}(\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(V))

that agrees with
T\otimes I_{\mathfrak{X}}
on
\mathscr{S}_{\infty}\otimes\mathfrak{X}
,

(ii)

if further
\lfloor J-n-s+\frac{\beta-n}{r}\rfloor\geq 0
,
then there exists
\widetilde{T}\in\mathcal{L}(\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(V))

that agrees with
T\otimes I_{\mathfrak{X}}
on

(\mathscr{S}\otimes\mathfrak{X})\cap\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(V)
.

Remark 21.16
.

If, in Corollary
21.15
,
r=p\in(0,1]
(resp.
\in[1,\infty)
)
and
s\in[0,1)
, then
the assumptions on
N
,
\varepsilon
, and
T

coincide with those of
[
26
, Theorem 4.2(1)]

(resp.
[
59
, Corollaries 9.10 and 9.11]
);
hence, we extend their results from Besov spaces and

\mathfrak{X}=\mathbb{C}^{m}
to general

\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(V)\subset\mathscr{S}_{\infty}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})
.

22.
Trace and extension operators

In this section, we study the trace and the extension operators.
Due to the specific structure of these two classes of operators,
it seems difficult to obtain satisfactory results within the
general framework
\dot{\mathbf{A}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathfrak{X})
.
Hence, we focus on the pointwise and the average-weighted spaces
in this section directly.

22.1.
Sequence spaces

We first consider a toy model in the sequence spaces
\dot{a}^{s}_{p,q}
. The underlying key estimate is the following lemma in the unweighted, scalar-valued case. It is implicitly contained in the proofs in
[
12
, Section 5]
, but we detail it here for convenience of reference.

Lemma 22.1
.

Given a sequence
u=\{u_{I}\}_{I\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, let
\hat{u}^{(k)}=\{\hat{u}^{(k)}_{Q}\}_{Q\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}
be defined by

(22.1)

\hat{u}^{(k)}_{Q}:=\begin{cases}\ell(I)^{\frac{1}{2}}u_{I}&\text{if}\quad Q=Q(I,k):=I\times[k\ell(I),(k+1)\ell(I))\\
0&\text{else}.\end{cases}

Then

(22.2)

\|\hat{u}^{(k)}\|_{\dot{a}^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\approx\|u\|_{\dot{b}^{s-\frac{1}{p}}_{p,r}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})},

where

(22.3)

r=\begin{cases}q,&\text{in the Besov case }(a=b),\\
p,&\text{in the Triebel--Lizorkin case }(a=f)\end{cases}

and the positive equivalence constants are independent of
u
and
k
.

Proof.

We prove the Besov and the Triebel–Lizorkin cases separately. In the Besov case, we will obtain an exact identity

\|\hat{u}^{(k)}\|_{\dot{b}^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}=\|u\|_{\dot{b}^{s-\frac{1}{p}}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})},

whose proof is a direct computation:

(22.4)

\begin{split}\|u\|_{\dot{b}^{s-\frac{1}{p}}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}^{q}&=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}2^{j(s-\frac{1}{p})q}2^{j(n-1)(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p})q}\Big(\sum_{I\in\mathscr{D}_{j}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}|u_{I}|^{p}\Big)^{\frac{q}{p}}\\
&=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}2^{jsq}2^{jn(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p})q}\Big(\sum_{I\in\mathscr{D}_{j}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}|\ell(I)^{\frac{1}{2}}u_{I}|^{p}\Big)^{\frac{q}{p}}\\
&=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}2^{jsq}2^{jn(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p})q}\Big(\sum_{Q\in\mathscr{D}_{j}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}|\hat{u}_{Q}^{(k)}|^{p}\Big)^{\frac{q}{p}}=\|\hat{u}^{(k)}\|_{\dot{b}^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{q}.\end{split}

We then turn to the Triebel–Lizorkin case of (
22.2
), restated for concreteness as

(22.5)

\|\hat{u}^{(k)}\|_{\dot{f}^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\approx\|u\|_{\dot{b}^{s-\frac{1}{p}}_{p,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}.

To prove this, we define

E(I,k):=I\times[(k+\tfrac{1}{3})\ell(I),(k+\tfrac{2}{3})\ell(I))\subset Q(I,k)\subset\mathbb{R}^{n},\qquad I\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1}),\quad k\in\mathbb{Z},

which obviously satisfies
|E(I,k)|=\frac{1}{3}|Q(I,k)|
.
We then apply case
p=q
of the previous computation (
22.4
) to find that

\begin{split}\|u\|_{\dot{b}^{s-\frac{1}{p}}_{p,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}^{p}&=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}2^{jsp}2^{jn(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p})p}\sum_{I\in\mathscr{D}_{j}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}|\ell(I)^{\frac{1}{2}}u_{I}|^{p}\\
&=3\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}2^{jsp}2^{jn(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p})p}\sum_{I\in\mathscr{D}_{j}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}|\ell(I)^{\frac{1}{2}}u_{I}|^{p}\frac{\mathbf{1}_{E(I,k)}(x)}{|Q(I,k)|}\,dx\\
&=3\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}2^{jsp}\sum_{I\in\mathscr{D}_{j}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}|\ell(I)^{\frac{1}{2}}u_{I}|^{p}\frac{\mathbf{1}_{E(I,k)}(x)}{|Q(I,k)|^{\frac{p}{2}}}\,dx.\end{split}

As observed in
[
12
, (5.25) et seq.]
, the sets
\{E(I,k)\}_{I\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}
are pairwise disjoint, so there is at most one non-zero term in the previous double sum for each fixed
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Thus

(22.6)

\begin{split}\|u\|_{\dot{b}^{s-\frac{1}{p}}_{p,p}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}^{p}&=3\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\Big(\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\Big|2^{js}\sum_{I\in\mathscr{D}_{j}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}\ell(I)^{\frac{1}{2}}u_{I}\frac{\mathbf{1}_{E(I,k)}}{|Q(I,k)|^{\frac{1}{2}}}\Big|^{q}\Big)^{\frac{p}{q}}\,dx\\
&=3\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\Big(\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\Big|2^{js}\sum_{Q\in\mathscr{D}_{j}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\hat{u}_{Q}^{(k)}\frac{\mathbf{1}_{E_{Q}}}{|Q|^{\frac{1}{2}}}\Big|^{q}\Big)^{\frac{p}{q}}\,dx,\end{split}

where

E_{Q}:=Q^{\prime}\times[(h+\frac{1}{3})\ell(Q),(h+\frac{2}{3})\ell(Q))\quad\text{for all}\quad Q=Q^{\prime}\times[h\ell(Q),(h+1)\ell(Q))\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{n}).

Since
E_{Q}\subset Q
and
|E_{Q}|=\frac{1}{3}|Q|\approx|Q|
for all
Q\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, it follows from
[
25
, Proposition 2.7]
that

\operatorname{RHS}\eqref{eq:fTrace-step}\approx\|\hat{u}_{Q}^{(k)}\|_{\dot{f}^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{p}.

Substituting this into (
22.6
), we complete the proof of (
22.5
) and hence Lemma
22.1
.
∎

Lemma 22.2
.

Let
p\in(0,\infty)
,
q\in(0,\infty]
,
and
d=\{d_{I}\}_{I\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}

and
\rho=\{\rho_{Q}\}_{Q\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}

be two families of quasi-norms on a Banach space
\mathfrak{X}
.
For each
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, the following statements are equivalent:

(i)

For all
I\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})
and
\mathfrak{e}\in\mathfrak{X}
,

(22.7)

d_{I}(\mathfrak{e})\lesssim\rho_{Q(I,k)}(\mathfrak{e})

with the implicit positive constant independent of
I
and
\mathfrak{e}
.

(ii)

For all
t=\{t_{I}\}_{I\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}\in\mathfrak{X}^{\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}
and
\hat{t}^{(k)}
as in (
22.1
)

(22.8)

\|t\|_{\dot{b}^{s-\frac{1}{p}}_{p,r}(d,\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}\lesssim\|\hat{t}^{(k)}\|_{\dot{a}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho,\mathbb{R}^{n})}

with
r
as in (
22.3
)
and the implicit positive constant independent of
t
.

A similar equivalence holds with both instances of “
\lesssim
” replaced by “
\gtrsim
”.

Proof.

Let
u_{I}:=d_{I}(t_{I})
and
v_{I}:=\rho_{Q(I,k)}(t_{I})
.
Then

(22.9)

\|t\|_{\dot{b}^{s-\frac{1}{p}}_{p,r}(d,\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}=\|u\|_{\dot{b}^{s-\frac{1}{p}}_{p,r}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})},\qquad\|\hat{t}^{(k)}\|_{\dot{a}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho,\mathbb{R}^{n})}=\|\hat{v}^{(k)}\|_{\dot{a}^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}.

For (
i
)
\Rightarrow
(
ii
), we have

\begin{split}\|u\|_{\dot{b}^{s-\frac{1}{p}}_{p,r}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}&\lesssim\|v\|_{\dot{b}^{s-\frac{1}{p}}_{p,r}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}\quad\text{by assumption \eqref{it:seq-test}}\\
&\approx\|\hat{v}^{(k)}\|_{\dot{a}^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\quad\text{by Lemma \ref {lem:bfTrace}}.\end{split}

Together with (
22.9
), this proves (
ii
).

For (
ii
)
\Rightarrow
(
i
), take
t_{I}=\delta_{I,J}\mathfrak{e}
with arbitrary
J\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})
and
\mathfrak{e}\in\mathfrak{X}
. Then

\begin{split}\operatorname{LHS}\eqref{eq:sec-trace}&=\ell(J)^{-(s-\frac{1}{p})}\ell(J)^{(n-1)(\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2})}d_{J}(\mathfrak{e})=\ell(J)^{-s}\ell(J)^{n(\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2})}\ell(J)^{\frac{1}{2}}d_{J}(\mathfrak{e}),\\
\operatorname{RHS}\eqref{eq:sec-trace}&=\ell(J)^{-s}\ell(J)^{n(\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2})}\rho_{Q(J,k)}(\ell(J)^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathfrak{e})=\ell(J)^{-s}\ell(J)^{n(\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2})}\ell(J)^{\frac{1}{2}}\rho_{Q(J,k)}(\mathfrak{e}).\end{split}

Cancelling out the common factor, we see that (
ii
) implies
d_{J}(\mathfrak{e})\lesssim\rho_{Q(J,k)}(\mathfrak{e})
, i.e., (
i
).

The proof of the similar equivalence with both “
\lesssim
” replaced by “
\gtrsim
” is exactly the same, making the same replacements in the proof.
∎

Remark 22.3
.

If the family
\rho=\{\rho_{Q}\}_{Q\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}
satisfies an estimate of the form

(22.10)

\frac{\rho_{Q}(\mathfrak{e})}{\rho_{R}(\mathfrak{e})}\lesssim\Big(1+\frac{|c_{Q}-c_{R}|}{\ell(Q)}\Big)^{c}

for all
\mathfrak{e}\in\mathfrak{X}
and all pairs of cubes of equal size
\ell(Q)=\ell(R)
with the implicit
positive constant independent of
\mathfrak{e}
,

Q
, and
R
, then condition (
22.7
) is equivalent (up to the dependence of the implicit constant on
k
) to

(22.11)

d_{I}(\mathfrak{e})\lesssim\rho_{Q(I,0)}(\mathfrak{e})

with the implicit positive constant independent of
\mathfrak{e}
and
I
.
Indeed, (
22.11
) implies (
22.7
) with constant
(1+|k|)^{c}
and vice versa. The same applies to “
\gtrsim
” in place of “
\lesssim
” in both (
22.7
) and (
22.11
).

22.2.
Function spaces

We now turn to the trace and the extension operators acting between function spaces over
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
.

For a sufficiently nice function
\Psi
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, its
trace
on
\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
is simply

(22.12)

(\operatorname{Tr}\Psi)(x^{\prime}):=\Psi(x^{\prime},0),\qquad x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}.

For general elements
f\in\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho,\mathbb{R}^{n})
, the idea is to expand
f
in terms sufficiently regular wavelets
\Psi_{Q}^{(\lambda)}
and apply (
22.12
) component-wise to each
\Psi_{Q}^{(\lambda)}=\Psi^{(\lambda^{\prime},\lambda_{n})}_{Q(I,k)}
.
Then it is useful to note that

(22.13)

\begin{split}\operatorname{Tr}\Psi_{Q(I,k)}^{(\lambda^{\prime},\lambda_{n})}&:=\Psi_{Q(I,k)}^{(\lambda^{\prime},\lambda_{n})}(\cdot,0),\qquad\lambda^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{n-1},\quad\lambda_{n}\in\{0,1\},\\
&=\psi_{[k,k+1)\ell(I)}^{(\lambda_{n})}(0)\cdot\Psi_{I}^{(\lambda^{\prime})}=\ell(I)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\psi^{(\lambda_{n})}(-k)\cdot\Psi_{I}^{(\lambda^{\prime})}.\end{split}

is a constant multiple of a wavelet
\Psi_{I}^{(\lambda^{\prime})}
on
\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
.

The case of the extension is slightly more tricky. In principle, given a function
F
on
\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
‚ we want to construct a function
\widetilde{F}=\operatorname{Ext}F
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that
\widetilde{F}(x^{\prime},0)=F(x^{\prime})
. Obviously, there is a great deal of flexibility in the choice
\widetilde{F}
. Starting with the wavelets first, we make the following choice:

Lemma 22.4
.

(i)

For any system of Daubechies wavelets, there is
k_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
\psi^{(0)}(-k_{0})=\varphi(-k_{0})\neq 0
.

(ii)

Using the number
k_{0}
from (
i
) and defining

(22.14)

\operatorname{Ext}\Psi_{I}^{(\lambda^{\prime})}:=\frac{\ell(I)^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\psi^{(0)}(-k_{0})}\Psi^{(\lambda^{\prime},0)}_{Q(I,k_{0})},\qquad I\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1}),\quad\lambda^{\prime}\in\Lambda_{n-1},

it follows that

(22.15)

\displaystyle(\operatorname{Tr}\circ\operatorname{Ext})\Psi^{(\lambda^{\prime})}_{I}=\Psi_{I}^{(\lambda^{\prime})}.

Proof.

Part (
i
) is
[
12
, Remark 5.2]
. Part (
ii
) then follows from the computation

\begin{split}(\operatorname{Tr}\circ\operatorname{Ext})\Psi^{(\lambda^{\prime})}_{I}&=\frac{\ell(I)^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\psi^{(0)}(-k_{0})}\operatorname{Tr}\Psi^{(\lambda^{\prime},0)}_{Q(I,k_{0})}\quad\text{by \eqref{eq:def-ext}}\\
&=\frac{\ell(I)^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\psi^{(0)}(-k_{0})}\frac{\psi^{(0)}(-k_{0})}{\ell(I)^{\frac{1}{2}}}\Psi^{(\lambda^{\prime})}_{I}=\Psi^{(\lambda^{\prime})}_{I}\quad\text{by \eqref{eq:trace-PsiQ}}.\end{split}

This completes the proof.
∎

Theorem 22.5
.

Let
p\in(0,\infty)
,
q\in(0,\infty]
,

d=\{d_{I}\}_{I\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}

be a family of
(u_{d},a_{d},b_{d},c_{d})
-norms,
and
\rho=\{\rho_{Q}\}_{Q\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}

be a family of
(u_{\rho},a_{\rho},b_{\rho}\,c_{\rho})
-norms,
where
u_{i}\in(0,1]
and
a_{i},b_{i},c_{i}\in[0,\infty)

for
i\in\{d,\rho\}
.
Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
be sufficiently large
such that (
20.6
) holds for the parameters
corresponding to
both spaces

\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho,\mathbb{R}^{n}),\qquad\dot{B}^{s-\frac{1}{p}}_{p,r}(d,\mathbb{R}^{n-1}),\quad\text{where}\quad r=\begin{cases}q&\text{if }A=B,\\
p&\text{if }A=F,\end{cases}

and let
\{\Psi_{Q}^{(\lambda)}\}_{(\lambda,Q)\in\Omega_{n}}
be a Daubechies wavelet system of order
N
.
Then the following assertions hold:

(i)

With
\operatorname{Ext}\Psi_{I}^{(\lambda^{\prime})}
defined as in (
22.14
), the rule

\operatorname{Ext}:f\mapsto\sum_{\lambda^{\prime}\in\Lambda_{n-1}}\sum_{I\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}\langle f,\Psi_{I}^{(\lambda^{\prime})}\rangle\operatorname{Ext}\Psi_{I}^{(\lambda^{\prime})}

gives a well-defined bounded linear operator

\operatorname{Ext}:\ \dot{B}^{s-\frac{1}{p}}_{p,r}(d,\mathbb{R}^{n-1})\to\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho,\mathbb{R}^{n})

if and only if

(22.16)

d_{I}(\mathfrak{e})\gtrsim\rho_{Q(I,0)}(\mathfrak{e}),\qquad\forall\ I\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1}),\ \mathfrak{e}\in\mathfrak{X}

with the implicit positive constant independent of
I
and
\mathfrak{e}
.

(ii)

Suppose further that

(22.17)

s>\frac{1}{p}+(n-1)\Big(\frac{1}{\max\{p,u_{d}\}}-1\Big)+b_{d}.

With
\operatorname{Tr}\Psi_{Q}^{(\lambda)}
defined as in (
22.13
), the rule

\operatorname{Tr}:f\mapsto\sum_{Q\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\langle f,\Psi_{Q}^{(\lambda)}\rangle\operatorname{Tr}\Psi_{Q}^{(\lambda)}

gives a well-defined bounded linear operator

\operatorname{Tr}:\ \dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho,\mathbb{R}^{n})\to\dot{B}^{s-\frac{1}{p}}_{p,r}(d,\mathbb{R}^{n-1})

if and only if

(22.18)

d_{I}(\mathfrak{e})\lesssim\rho_{Q(I,0)}(\mathfrak{e}),\qquad\forall\ I\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1}),\ \mathfrak{e}\in\mathfrak{X}

with the implicit positive constant independent of
I
and
\mathfrak{e}
.

(iii)

Under all assumptions (
22.16
), (
22.17
), and (
22.18
), the composition
\operatorname{Tr}\circ\operatorname{Ext}
is the identity on
\dot{B}^{s-\frac{1}{p}}_{p,r}(\rho)
.

Proof.

We begin with some observations that are relevant to both parts (
i
) and (
ii
).

First, the assumption that
\rho
is a family of
(u_{\rho},a_{\rho},b_{\rho},c_{\rho})
-norms (Definition
15.1
) implies condition (
22.10
) of Remark
22.3
with
c=c_{\rho}
. Thus, by the said remark, both (
22.16
) and (
22.18
) are equivalent to versions with
Q(I,k)
in place of
Q(I,0)
, allowing the implicit positive constants to depend on
k\in\mathbb{Z}
. These are precisely assumption (
22.7
) of Lemma
22.2
and its reverse version, giving us access to that lemma.

Second, by the assumption on
N
, Corollary
20.3
(referring to Proposition
20.1
(
i
)) guarantees that

(22.19)

\Psi_{Q}^{(\lambda)}
(
Q\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
\lambda\in\Lambda_{n}
) are analysis and synthesis molecules for
\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho,\mathbb{R}^{n})

and that

(22.20)

\text{$\Psi_{I}^{(\lambda^{\prime})}$ ($I\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})$, $\lambda\in\Lambda_{n-1}$) are analysis and synthesis molecules for $\dot{B}^{s-\frac{1}{p}}_{p,r}(d,\mathbb{R}^{n-1})$}.

Moreover, the additional assumption (
22.17
) in part (
ii
) is exactly the assumption (
18.6
) corresponding to
(u_{d},a_{d},b_{d},c_{d})
-norms in dimension
n-1
in place of
(u,a,b,c)
-norms in dimension
n
. Thus, under this assumption, Corollary
20.3
(referring to Proposition
20.1
(
ii
)) further guarantees that

(22.21)

\text{$\Psi_{I}^{(0)}$ ($I\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})$) are synthesis molecules for $\dot{B}^{s-\frac{1}{p}}_{p,r}(d,\mathbb{R}^{n-1})$}.

Below, we will repeatedly use the mapping properties of analysis and synthesis molecules established in Theorem
19.5
. We will do this without quoting Theorem
19.5
, only referring to the relevant property among (
22.19
), (
22.20
), and (
22.21
).

The proofs of the “only if” claims in both parts (
i
) and (
ii
) follow routinely by testing the boundedness of
\operatorname{Ext}
on functions of the form
f=\Psi_{I}^{(\lambda^{\prime})}\mathfrak{e}
and the boundedness of
\operatorname{Tr}
on functions of the form
f=\Psi_{Q}^{(\lambda)}\mathfrak{e}
, and we omit details. We then consider the “if” claims of each part in turn:

(
i
):
From (
22.14
), we see that the extension operator

\operatorname{Ext}f=\frac{1}{\psi^{(0)}(-k_{0})}\sum_{\lambda^{\prime}\in\Lambda_{n-1}}\sum_{I\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}\ell(I)^{\frac{1}{2}}\langle f,\Psi_{I}^{(\lambda^{\prime})}\rangle\Psi^{(\lambda^{\prime},0)}_{Q(I,k_{0})}

is a finite linear combination over compositions of the following maps:

(a)

the wavelet analysis map
f\mapsto\{u_{I}:=\langle f,\Psi_{I}^{(\lambda^{\prime})}\rangle\}_{I\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}
, which is bounded from
\dot{B}^{s-\frac{1}{p}}_{p,r}(d,\mathbb{R}^{n-1})
to
\dot{b}^{s-\frac{1}{p}}_{p,q}(\rho,\mathbb{R}^{n})
by the analysis part (
22.20
);

(b)

the map
u=\{u_{I}\}_{I\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}\mapsto\hat{u}^{(k_{0})}=:\{t_{Q}\}_{Q\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}
, which is bounded from
\dot{b}^{s-\frac{1}{p}}_{p,r}(d,\mathbb{R}^{n-1})
to
\dot{a}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho,\mathbb{R}^{n})
by Lemma
22.2
and Remark
22.3
, under the assumption (
22.18
);

(c)

the wavelet synthesis map
\displaystyle\{t_{Q}\}_{Q\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\mapsto\sum_{Q\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}t_{Q}\Psi_{Q}^{(\lambda^{\prime},0)}
, which is bounded from
\dot{a}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho,\mathbb{R}^{n})
to
\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho,\mathbb{R}^{n})
by the synthesis part of (
22.19
).

As a finite linear combination of compositions of these bounded maps,
\operatorname{Ext}
defines a bounded linear operator from
\dot{B}^{s-\frac{1}{p}}_{p,r}(d,\mathbb{R}^{n-1})
to
\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho,\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

(
ii
): We note from (
22.13
) that
\operatorname{Tr}\Psi_{Q(I,k)}^{(\lambda)}
is non-zero only if
\psi^{(\lambda_{n})}(-k)\neq 0
. Recalling that the Daubechies wavelets are compactly supported, this happens only if
|k|\leq K
for some finite
K
. Thus

\begin{split}\operatorname{Tr}f&=\sum_{|k|\leq K}\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{n}}\sum_{I\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}\langle f,\Psi_{Q(I,k)}^{(\lambda)}\rangle\operatorname{Tr}\Psi_{Q(I,k)}^{(\lambda)}\\
&=\sum_{|k|\leq K}\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{n}}\psi^{(\lambda_{n})}(-k)\sum_{I\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}\ell(I)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\langle f,\Psi_{Q(I,k)}^{(\lambda)}\rangle\Psi_{I}^{(\lambda^{\prime})}\quad\text{by \eqref{eq:trace-PsiQ}}\end{split}

is a finite linear combination over compositions of the following maps:

(a)

the wavelet analysis map
f\mapsto\{t_{Q}:=\langle f,\Psi_{Q}^{(\lambda)}\rangle\}_{Q\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}
, which is bounded from
\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho,\mathbb{R}^{n})
to
\dot{a}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho,\mathbb{R}^{n})
by the analysis part of (
22.19
);

(b)

the truncation map
\{t_{Q}\}_{Q\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\mapsto\{\mathbf{1}_{\mathscr{Q}_{k_{0}}}(Q)t_{Q}\}_{Q\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}
, where
\mathscr{Q}_{k_{0}}=\{Q(I,k):I\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})\}
, which is obviously bounded on
\dot{a}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho,\mathbb{R}^{n})
;

(c)

the map
\{\mathbf{1}_{\mathscr{Q}_{k_{0}}}(Q)t_{Q}\}_{Q\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\mapsto\{u_{I}:=\ell(I)^{-\frac{1}{2}}t_{Q(I,k)}\}_{I\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}
, which is bounded from (a subspace of)
\dot{a}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho,\mathbb{R}^{n})
to
\dot{b}^{s-\frac{1}{p}}_{p,r}(d,\mathbb{R}^{n-1})
by Lemma
22.2
and Remark
22.3
, under the assumption (
22.18
);

(d)

the wavelet synthesis map

\displaystyle\{u_{I}\}_{I\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}\mapsto\sum_{I\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}u_{I}\Psi_{I}^{(\lambda^{\prime})},

which is bounded from
\dot{b}^{s-\frac{1}{p}}_{p,r}(d,\mathbb{R}^{n-1})
to
\dot{B}^{s-\frac{1}{p}}_{p,r}(d,\mathbb{R}^{n-1})
by the synthesis part of (
22.20
) and (
22.21
); we note that the latter is needed, since the partial vector
\lambda^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{n-1}
can be identically
0
even if
\lambda\in\Lambda_{n}:=\{0,1\}^{n}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}
.

As a finite linear combination of compositions of these bounded maps,
\operatorname{Tr}
defines a bounded linear operator from
\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho,\mathbb{R}^{n})
to
\dot{B}^{s-\frac{1}{p}}_{p,r}(d,\mathbb{R}^{n-1})
.

(
iii
):
Let
f\in\dot{B}^{s-\frac{1}{p}}_{p,r}(d,\mathbb{R}^{n-1})
.
Noting that

\{\operatorname{Ext}\Psi_{I}^{(\lambda^{\prime})}\}_{\lambda^{\prime}\in\Lambda_{n-1},I\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}

is a subset of the wavelet system
\{\Psi_{Q}^{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{n},Q\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}

on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we infer that

\displaystyle(\operatorname{Tr}\circ\operatorname{Ext})f

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Tr}\Big(\sum_{\lambda^{\prime}\in\Lambda_{n-1}}\sum_{I\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}\langle f,\Psi_{I}^{(\lambda^{\prime})}\rangle\operatorname{Ext}\Psi_{I}^{(\lambda^{\prime})}\Big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\lambda^{\prime}\in\Lambda_{n-1}}\sum_{I\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}\langle f,\Psi_{I}^{(\lambda^{\prime})}\rangle(\operatorname{Tr}\circ\operatorname{Ext})\Psi_{I}^{(\lambda^{\prime})}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\lambda^{\prime}\in\Lambda_{n-1}}\sum_{I\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})}\langle f,\Psi_{I}^{(\lambda^{\prime})}\rangle\Psi_{I}^{(\lambda^{\prime})}=f\qquad\text{by \eqref{tr-ext}}

holds in
\mathscr{S}^{\prime}_{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1};\mathfrak{X})
.
This completes the proof of Theorem
22.5
.
∎

Finally, we obtain the corresponding boundedness of the trace and the extension operators on pointwise-weighted spaces as follows.

Corollary 22.6
.

Let
p\in(0,\infty)
,
and let
V\in\mathscr{A}_{v}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
W\in\mathscr{A}_{w}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})
with
v,w\in[p,\infty)

have doubling dimensions
\beta_{V},\beta_{W}
.
Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
be sufficiently large
such that (
20.7
) holds for the parameters
corresponding to
both spaces

\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(V,\mathbb{R}^{n}),\quad\dot{B}^{s-\frac{1}{p}}_{p,r}(W,\mathbb{R}^{n-1}),\quad\text{where}\quad\begin{cases}q\in(0,\infty]\quad\&\quad r=q&\text{if }A=B,\\
q\in(0,v+\varepsilon_{V})\quad\&\quad r=p&\text{if }A=F,\end{cases}

where
\varepsilon_{V}
is the optimal reverse Hölder lifting index of
V
, and let
\{\Psi_{Q}^{(\lambda)}\}_{(\lambda,Q)\in\Omega_{n}}
be a Daubechies wavelet system of order
N
.
For
\operatorname{Ext}
and
\operatorname{Tr}
as in Theorem
22.5
, the following results hold:

(1)

\operatorname{Ext}
defines a bounded linear operator

\operatorname{Ext}:\ \dot{B}^{s-\frac{1}{p}}_{p,r}(W,\mathbb{R}^{n-1})\to\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(V,\mathbb{R}^{n})

if and only if

(22.22)

\rho_{\textit{\L}^{w}(I,W)}(\mathfrak{e})\gtrsim\rho_{\textit{\L}^{v}(Q(I,0),V)}(\mathfrak{e})\qquad\forall\ I\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1}),\ \mathfrak{e}\in\mathfrak{X}

with the implicit positive constant independent of
I
and
\mathfrak{e}
.

(2)

Suppose further that

(22.23)

s>\frac{1}{p}+(n-1)\Big(\frac{1}{\max\{1,p\}}-1\Big)+\frac{\beta_{W}-n+1}{w}.

Then
\operatorname{Tr}
defines a bounded linear operator

\operatorname{Tr}:\ \dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(V,\mathbb{R}^{n})\to\dot{B}^{s-\frac{1}{p}}_{p,r}(W,\mathbb{R}^{n-1})

if and only if

(22.24)

\rho_{\textit{\L}^{w}(I,W)}(\mathfrak{e})\lesssim\rho_{\textit{\L}^{v}(Q(I,0),V)}(\mathfrak{e})\qquad\forall\ I\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1}),\ \mathfrak{e}\in\mathfrak{X}

with the implicit positive constant independent of
I
and
\mathfrak{e}
.

(3)

Under all the assumptions (
22.22
), (
22.23
), and (
22.24
), the composition
\operatorname{Tr}\circ\operatorname{Ext}
is the identity on
\dot{B}^{s-\frac{1}{p}}_{p,r}(W,\mathbb{R}^{n-1})
.

Proof.

By Theorems
14.3
and
14.7
, we have the coincidence of spaces

\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(V,\mathbb{R}^{n})=\dot{A}^{s}_{p,q}(\rho,\mathbb{R}^{n}),\quad\text{where}\quad\rho:=\{\rho_{Q}:=\rho_{\textit{\L}^{v}(Q,V)}\}_{Q\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{n})},

and

\dot{B}^{s-\frac{1}{p}}_{p,r}(W,\mathbb{R}^{n})=\dot{B}^{s-\frac{1}{p}}_{p,r}(d,\mathbb{R}^{n-1}),\quad\text{where}\quad d:=\{d_{I}:=\rho_{\textit{\L}^{w}(I,W)}\}_{I\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})},

By Remark
15.2
, these are families of
(u_{i},a_{i},b_{i},c_{i})
-norms,
i\in\{\rho,d\}
, where in particular

(u_{d},a_{d},b_{d},c_{d})=(\min\{1,w\},\frac{n-1}{w},\frac{\beta_{W}-(n-1)}{w},\frac{\beta_{W}}{w}).

Since
w\in[p,\infty)
by assumption, it follows that
\min\{p,u_{d}\}=\min\{p,1,w\}=\min\{p,1\}
. Thus assumption (
22.23
) of the corollary coincides with assumption (
22.17
) of Theorem
22.5
for the special case of the quasi-norms now under consideration. Similarly, the assumption (
20.7
) on
N
is the special case, for these parameters, of assumption (
20.6
) made in Theorem
22.5
. Thus Corollary
22.6
follows by specialising Theorem
22.5
to this case and using the equivalence of the pointwise-weighted and average-weighted spaces already mentioned.
∎
